ii 
 
PREFACE 
 
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on 
Health and Family Welfare, having been authorised by the Committee, present this 174th 
Report of the Committee on the Demands for Grants (Demand No.4) of the Ministry of 
Ayush for the year 2026-27. 
 
2. The Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committees are entrusted with the 
constitutional role of ensuring accountability of executive to the Parliament.  In this regard, 
Rule 270 (a) of Rules & Procedure and Conduct & Business in the Council of States (Rajya 
Sabha) empowered the Department-related Standing Committees to consider the Demands for 
Grants of the related Departments/Ministries with the objective of ensuring financial 
accountability of the executive and report thereon. In exercise of its constitutional mandate, 
the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health & Family Welfare 
scrutinized the Demands for Grants for the year 2026-27 (Demand No. 4) of the Ministry of 
Ayush. 
 
3.  The Committee, in its meeting held on 23rd February, 2026, heard the Secretary, 
Ministry of Ayush and other Officers on the Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of 
Ayush. 
 
4.  The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied 
upon the following documents: - 
 
(i) Detailed Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Ayush for the year 2026-27; 
(ii) Annual Report of the Ministry for the year 2025-26; 
(iii) Output-Outcome framework (2026-27) for schemes of the Ministry; 
(iv) Background Note on Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Ayush for the 
year 2026-27; 
(v) Written replies furnished by the Department to the Questionnaires sent to them 
by the Secretariat; 
(vi) Presentation made by the Secretary, Ministry of Ayush and other 
officersconcerned; 
(vii) Economic Survey 2025-26 and Speech of Hon’ble President of India; 
(viii) Budget Speech of the Finance Minister; 
(ix) Outcome Budget- Expenditure Budget; 
(x) Other study materials / documents / reports relevant and incidental to 
consideration of Demand for Grants (2026-27) pertaining to the Ministry of 
Ayush 
 
5.  The Committee, in its meeting held on 18th March, 2026, considered the draft report 
and adopted the same. 
 
6.  For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the 
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report. 
 
 
 
NEW DELHI

1 
 
CHAPTER- I 
 
INTRODUCTION 
 
1.1 The Ministry of Ayush is the nodal Ministry of the Government of India responsible 
for policy formulation, development, education, research, regulation and propagation of 
traditional systems of medicine, namely Ayurveda, Yoga & Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, 
Sowa-Rigpa and Homoeopathy. These systems collectively contribute to a pluralistic and 
integrative healthcare framework within the country. 
 
1.2  The institutional evolution of the Ministry reflects progressive policy recognition of 
traditional medicine in the national healthcare architecture. The journey commenced with the 
establishment of the Department of Indian Systems of Medicine and Homoeopathy (ISM&H) 
in 1995 under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. In 2003, it was re-designated as the 
Department of Ayush to strengthen focus on education and research. In 2011, Sowa-Rigpa 
was formally included within its ambit. Recognising the strategic and public health 
significance of these systems, the Government elevated the Department to a full-fledged 
Ministry in 2014, thereby consolidating its mandate to lead India’s integrated healthcare 
future which today aligns with the national vision of “Viksit Bharat @2047”. 
  
1.3  The mandate of the Ministry encompasses policy formulation, standardisation, 
quality assurance, human resource development, research validation, international 
cooperation, medicinal plant development and integration of Ayush services within public 
health delivery mechanisms. The functioning of the Ministry is aligned with the broader 
national objectives of strengthening preventive healthcare, promoting holistic wellness and 
advancing India’s traditional knowledge systems. 
 
1.4  The Ministry administers six recognised systems of traditional medicine: Ayurveda, 
Yoga & Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, Sowa-Rigpa and Homoeopathy. Each system is based 
on distinct theoretical foundations and therapeutic principles, yet all share an emphasis on 
preventive care, natural therapies, lifestyle regulation, individualised treatment and holistic 
well-being. These systems are integrated within the broader healthcare framework through 
institutional education, research councils, clinical establishments, pharmacopoeial standards 
and regulatory bodies. Their role in primary healthcare, chronic disease management, 
wellness promotion and public health outreach has significantly broadened through initiatives 
like National Ayush Mission (NAM) and Ayushman Bharat emphasising preventive and 
holistic care at grass root level.    
 
1.5 Thematic Domains of the Ministry 
 
1.5.1 The Ministry of Ayush has delineated seven broad thematic domains to structure and 
streamline its activities of its Mission. These thematic domains reflect the core priorities and 
provide the policy framework within which schemes and programmes are conceptualised and 
implemented which serve as the foundational pillars in achieving its Mission objectives.

The Committee understands that the Demand for Grants 2026-27 seeks to strengthen institutional capacity, expand equitable access to Ayush services, promote 


scientific validation and digital governance and enhance regulatory oversight in the Ayush sector. The Committee observes that such initiatives are pivotal for consolidating the progress achieved in recent years and for strengthening the role of traditional systems of medicine in the national healthcare framework. The Committee howeverof the view that the proposed financial provisions must also be directed towards addressing emerging public health challenges while ensuring effective implementation and optimal utilisation of resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry ensure that the budgetstrategically utilised to consolidate past achievements, expand access to quality Ayush services and support innovation and research, in alignment with the national vision of Viksit Bharat @ 2047 and India’s aspiration to emerge as a global leader in traditional medicine systems.  1.8 Research, Standardisation and Regulatory Strengthening 1.8.1 The Secretary, Ministry of Ayush has furnishedresearch sector emphasising that Centre of Excellence have been established and multiple corroborative research studies such as Mission Utkarsh, Iron deficiency Anaemia study with ICMR etc. are being undertaken apart from issuance of selected disease conditions. The Committee observes that myths & misconceptions clouding over the Ayush affect public perception and limit the wider acceptance and services despite their long-standing traditional knowledge base and increasing scientific validation.The Committee, therefore, recommends that the undertake sustained and evidence-based public awareness initiatives to address prevailing misconceptions and disseminate accurate information regarding the safety, efficacy and regulatory oversight of Ayush medicines. country. The Committee also notes that enhancing the global competitiveness of Ayush 
products and services, including exports, will require sustained policy attention. 
 
1.12.2  The Committee recognises the need for continued institutional 
strengthening of the Ministry of Ayush in view of the expanding role of Ayush systems 
within the national healthcare framework. While the ongoing transition from system-
specific promotion towards structured integration of Ayush within public health 
delivery reflects a calibrated policy approach, the Committee is of the view that this 
transition requires sustained institutional capacity, effective implementation 
mechanisms and strengthened governance structures to translate policy objectives into 
tangible outcomes. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry take 
appropriate steps to further strengthen institutional capacities and governance 
mechanisms to support the progressive integration of Ayush systems within the national 
healthcare framework. The Committee further emphasises that infrastructure 
expansion, regulatory reforms and digital governance initiatives should be implemented 
in a coordinated manner so as to facilitate long-term institutional consolidation and 
effective delivery of Ayush services across the country. 
 
1.12.3  The Committee takes into account various initiatives undertaken by the 
Ministry for the promotion and development of Ayush systems across the country. The 
Committee observes that while these initiatives have contributed to strengthening 
institutional infrastructure and expanding outreach, there is a need to ensure that the 
outcomes of these interventions are systematically evaluated. However, the Committee is 
of the view that greater emphasis must now be placed on measurable health outcomes 
and impact assessment, effective capacity utilisation of newly established institutions, 
strengthening of quality assurance and pharmacovigilance mechanisms and improved 
coordination with State Governments in programme implementation. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry adopt a more outcome-oriented approach by 
promoting evidence-based research validation and ensuring optimal utilisation of 
budgetary allocations so as to maximise the overall effectiveness of Ayush interventions.

CHAPTER - II 
MACRO ANALYSIS OF DEMANDS FOR GRANTS 
2.1 The Budget Estimates for FY 2026-27 reflect the financial outlay and structural 
priorities of the Ministry of Ayush across its various components including establishment 
expenditure, statutory institutions, autonomous bodies, Central Sector Schemes and Centrally 
Sponsored Schemes. The Ministry of Ayush has been allocated a total sum of ₹ 4,408.93 
crore comprising both revenue and capital expenditure. The Budget allocation signifies the 
Government’s continued commitment towards strengthening Ayush services, promoting 
research and academic institutions, expanding educational infrastructure, supporting 
medicinal plant conservation, and ensuring focused regional development, particularly in the 
North Eastern Region. A comparative statement of the Budget Estimates, Revised Estimates, 
and Actual Expenditure is presented below:

2.2.1 The Ministry informed that the Budget Estimates (BE) of the Ministry of Ayush have 
increased from ₹1272.15 crore in 2014-15 to ₹4408.93 crore in 2026-27, registering an 
overall growth of 246.57 per cent during the period, which indicates a broad upward 
trajectory in allocations and a gradual expansion of Ayush services.  
2.2.2 The Committee observes that a closer examination of the Budget Estimates over 
the past five years reveals that the year-on-year growth has not followed a stable or 
predictable scaling pattern. The annual growth in BE has fluctuated considerably, 
ranging from 2.68 per cent in 2022-23 to 19.59 per cent in 2023-24, followed by a sharp 
moderation to 1.78 per cent in 2024-25 and thereafter moderate increases of 7.55 per 
cent in 2025-26 and 10.42 per cent in 2026-27. The Committee further observes that 
when these nominal increases are adjusted against average inflation levels, certain years 
reflected negative real growth, implying erosion in the effective purchasing power of 
allocations. The Committee is of the view that such volatility suggests episodic 
enhancement rather than a calibrated and programmatic scaling of budgetary support 
for the sector.The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush 
formulate its Budget Estimate projections after duly factoring in realistic assessment of 
each scheme/project and keeping in account prevailing and anticipated inflation as well 
so that real growth in allocations is maintained at a meaningful level to support 
sustained expansion of Ayush services. The Committee further recommends that the 
Ministry endeavour to adopt a steadier and more incremental pattern of budgetary 
growth to ensure predictability and continuity in financial planning. A calibrated and 
consistent approach would facilitate stable scaling of programmes, safeguard the 

10 
 
purchasing power of allocations and enable systematic strengthening of the Ayush 
sector over the medium to long term.

2.3.1 The Committee observes that the alignment between projected demand and Budget 
Estimates during 2021-22 to 2026-27 reflects repeated shortfalls except in 2022-23, which 
may affect medium-term planning certainty, prioritisation of projects and phased execution of 
schemes. The Committee is cognizant that deviations between projected demand and final 
allocations in public budgeting processes, particularly repeated shortfalls exceeding 20 per 
cent, may weaken the credibility of demand projections and reduce predictability in resource 
planning. 
2.3.2 The Committee appreciates the Ministry’s efforts in presenting comprehensive 
projected demands aimed at expanding the scope, reach and institutional capacity of the 
sector; however, sustained substantial deviations between projected demand and 
approved allocation at BE and RE stage may adversely impact planning stability and 
execution strategy.  The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
toadvanceplea for the outcome-linked and evidence-based justification of its projected 
demands by clearly demonstrating measurable impact, implementation readiness and 
expected sectoral returns. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry adopt 
a phased and prioritised approach in structuring its demands by distinguishing core 
commitments from expansionary and long-term aspirational components so as to 
facilitate more realistic projection at the stage of budgetestimate keeping in view 
feasibility of project implementation and absorption capacity of implementing agencies. 
The Committee also recommends that the Ministry develop a multi-year financial 
projection framework to enable greater predictability in resource planning and 
smoother alignment with the budgetary processes of the Ministry of Finance.

2.4.1  The Committee observes with serious concern that against the total 
projected demand of ₹ 5,679.48 crore for the year 2026-27, an allocation of only ₹ 
4,408.93 crore has been made at the Budget Estimates stage, resulting in a substantial 
shortfall of ₹ 1,270.55 crore (22.37 %)of  the projected requirement.The Committee 
further observes that this fiscal compression is not uniformly distributed across 
components but is disproportionately concentrated in certain critical and expansion-
oriented segments which includes flagship implementation schemes like NAM & 
CSSCMP; Research bodies like CCRYN &CCRH; premier institutes NIH, AIIA and 
quality strengthening initiatives like the Ayush Oushadhi Gunvatta Evam 

12 
 
UtpadanSamvardhan Yojana (AOUGSY) which may have a cascading impact on 
capacity augmentation, infrastructure expansion, research continuity and the overall 
momentum of institutional growth in strategically important and emerging domains of 
the Ayush systems.The Committee also recalls that in 165th Report DFG 2025-26, it had 
recommended seeking supplementary grants at the Revised Estimates stage; however, 
the information furnished instead reflects a reduction of ₹ 321 crore at the RE stage.  
2.4.2 The Committee observes that the reduction in allocations at the Revised 
Estimates stage across several components raises serious concerns regarding the 
adequacy of fiscal support extended to the sector despite acknowledged requirements. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush, in consultation with 
the Ministry of Finance, undertake a mid-year review of allocations with a view to 
providing adequate supplementary support to bridge the critical funding gap.The 
Committee further desires that priority be accorded to key components, particularly 
flagship implementation schemes such as the National Ayush Mission (NAM), research 
councils, premier national institutes and quality-strengthening initiatives, so as to 
prevent disruption of ongoing projects, dilution of research momentum or deferment of 
infrastructure expansion. The Committee emphasizes that these strategic segments 
represent long-term capacity-building investments and should not be subjected to 
disproportionate fiscal contraction.The Committee also urges the Ministry to adopt a 
more realistic and consultative budgeting approach in future cycles to ensure 
adequateBE allocations so that no project/scheme should suffer for want of funds and 
thereby doubly safeguarding programmatic continuity and sustaining the growth 
trajectory of the sector. 
2.5  BE - RE  Discipline with Projected Demand  
                                                                          (Table 6)                                    (₹ in crore) 
Year Projected 
Demand BE RE BE vs PD

2025-26 5408.48 3992.90 3671.82 26.17% -8.04% 32.11% 
2026-27 5679.48 4408.93 - 22.37% - - 
 
2.5.1 The Committee finds that there has been a dual-level compression in allocation- first 
between the Projected Demand and Budget Estimates and thereafter between the Budget 
Estimates and Revised Estimates. While such fiscal filtering reflects budgetary prudence, the 
recurrence of dual-stage compression assumes significance as unless the BE-level budget is 

13 
 
fully utilised, the objective of reducing the gap between projected demand and budgetary 
allocation at the BE stage cannot be meaningfully achieved.Further, the Committee observes 
that there has been an overall decrease of about 32.11% from Projected Demand to the RE 
stage, reflecting that the originally envisaged initiatives required substantial recalibration, 
prioritisation of projects, and phased execution of schemes. The repeated shortfalls exceeding 
20% on account of dual-level compression may weaken demand credibility and reduce 
predictability in resource planning. 
2.5.2 The Ministry has stated the following major reasons for the reduction from BE to RE 
allocation in 2025-26: 
i. Slow pace of expenditure due to the introduction of the SNA SPARSH system and 
reduction of funds by MoF at the RE stage by almost ₹ 494 crore forming major 
component of reduction at RE stage 
ii. Repeated pattern of “non-approval of construction projects” across many schemes and 
programmes. 
iii.  As per actual requirement. 
iv. Staff non-engagement and vacancy-based reductions. 
v. Unutilised balance with CPWD. 
vi. Fewer project proposals approved in PSC/PAC and consequent reduction by MoF. 
2.5.3 The Committee is of the view that the Ministry needs to revisit and refine its 
approach to budget formulation and expenditure planning process so as to minimise 
recurrent dual-stage compression by more realistic estimation, stronger implementation 
preparedness and closer alignment between projection and absorptive capacity. The 
Committee further emphasises the importance of more effective pre-budget engagement 
with the Ministry of Finance by placing proposals based on outcome orientation and 
implementation ready, supported by defined timelines, clarity regarding approval status 
of projects, staffing position, and procurement readiness. The Committee emphasis that 
construction projects should be included in projections only after obtaining the requisite 
administrative and technical approvals. Similarly, existing vacancies, unspent balances 
with CPWD, and the status of proposals pending before PSC/PAC should be 
realistically assessed at the stage of demand formulation. The Committee considers it a 
budgeting prudence for the Ministry to institute regular quarterly reviews of 
expenditure, closely monitor the rollout of the SNA SPAR system at the State level and 
take timely corrective steps to avoid substantial surrenders or downward revisions at 
the RE stage. These measures so undertaken would contribute to greater credibility in 
budget projections, improve stability in resource planning, and ensure that allocations 
made at the BE stage are effectively and fully utilized while potentially implementing 
the scheme to optimum level thereby, setting bulwark against reduction of budget at 
stage.

2.6.1 The Committee highlights that the Ministry has consistently maintained high 
levels of utilisationand administrative discipline in deploying funds allocated at the 
Revised Estimate stage. The Committee notes the Ministry’s sustained utilisation 
performance of allocated funds at RE stage and the same demonstrates 
capacity/potential to effectively deploy resources if budgetary allocation is earmarked 
for utilization as per projected demand. In view of this consistent absorptive strength, 
the Committee is of the considered opinion that higher budgetary allocation levels are 
justified therefore, the Committee recommends that the Ministry should continue to 
strengthen quarterly expenditure planning and milestone-based fund release 
mechanisms to ensure balanced spending patterns and avoid excessive concentration of 
expenditure in the closing months of the financial year.Further, the Committee advises 
the Ministry to improve the Actual Expenditure of National Ayush Mission by 
addressing the transitional challenges being faced. 
2.7 Capital Intensity Vs Growth Ambition 
                                                                (Table 8)                               (₹ in crore) 
Year BE Total Capital Capital Exp. Share (%) 
2021-22 2970.30 0.00 - 
2022-23 3050.00 0.00 - 
2023-24 3647.50 5.94 0.16% 
2024-25 3712.49 8.75 0.24% 
2025-26 3992.90 55.45 1.39% 
2026-27 4408.93 27.07 0.61% 
2.7.1 The Committee observes that the capital outlay in the Ayush budget has remained 
modest, reflecting the Ministry’s predominantly revenue-oriented budgetary structure, with 

15 
 
limited identifiable capital expenditure under the direct budget head. The Committee notes 
that major infrastructure interventions in the Ayush sector are largely financed through grants 
under the National Ayush Mission and through component-wise allocations rather than 
through a distinct and consolidated capital outlay at the Ministry level. While acknowledging 
these efforts, the Committee expresses concern over the recurring underutilisation of funds 
under the National Ayush Mission and the reported slow pace of infrastructure development 
in certain autonomous bodies, as reflected in the Ministry’s submissions on infrastructure 
progress. The Committee also notes the Ministry’s submission that the reduction in capital 
outlay at the Revised Estimate stage in the Budget Estimates for 2025-26 was attributable to 
lower fund requirements projected under the Ayush Grid component. 
2.7.2 The Committee appreciates the Ministry’s efforts to expand infrastructure in the 
Ayush sector through scheme-based interventions and collaborative federal 
mechanisms, particularly under the National Ayush Mission and the integrative 
healthcare framework. However, the Committee is of the view that sustained sectoral 
growth of Ayush services requires a more predictable and strategically designed 
separate capital investment framework at the Ministry level. The preponderance of 
aspiration to position Ayush as a global leader in traditional medicine would require a 
budgetary architecture that moves beyond a predominantly revenue-driven framework, 
as durable growth depends upon sustained asset creation in institutions, laboratories, 
testing facilities and digital infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry progressively enhance identifiable capital expenditure so that it constitutes 
about 5-7 per cent of the total Budget Estimates over the next three financial years. 
 2.7.3 The Committee further recommends for creation of a dedicated Ayush 
Infrastructure Fund with multi years capital option for institutional upgradation, 
modern drug testing and quality control infrastructure, national research and clinical 
networks, and digital integration including Ayush Grid 2.0. Additionally, future 
Demands for Grants should consider annexing a consolidated statement of capital 
formation across direct budget heads, the National Ayush Mission, Research Councils, 
and Autonomous Bodies to reflect level of capital intensity and support structured long-
term investment planning.

2.8.1 The Committee observes that the Ministry’s proportional share within the health 
sector framework has improved over the decade (3.31% → 4.14%), with a faster growth rate 
(~246% vs ~177%) than the budget growth of Ministry of Health & Family Welfare. 
However, a 20% increase to ₹4,408 crore in 2026-27, the Ministry of Ayush still operates 
within a structurally asymmetrical fiscal space with proportional share hovering around 4% in 
last 5 years. 
2.8.2 The Committee appreciates the progressive strengthening of the Ministry’s 
proportional share within the overall health architecture over the past decade, however, 
considering the expanding mandate of the Ministry and its long-term aspirations for 
enhanced global positioning and global leadership, the Committee recommends that 
sustained expansion of Ayush services, research capacity and global engagement would 
require enhanced and consistent budgetary allocation in its share within the national 
health envelope. The Ministry therefore, continue to strengthen evidence-based 
justification of its programmes, demonstrate measurable public health outcomes and 
articulate a medium-term fiscal roadmap aligned with its long-term sectoral 
objectives.The Committee in this regardreiterates its earlier recommendationmade in 
128th Report (Demand for Grants 2025-26) that at least 10% of the total health sector 
budget be earmarked progressively for the Ministry of Ayush for effective pluralistic 
healthcare model.

2.9.1 The Committee notes that the Budget Estimate for the Ministry of Ayush for the year 
2026-27 stands at ₹4,408 crore against the total Union Government expenditure of ₹53.47 
lakh crore. The allocation thus constitutes approximately 0.082 per cent of the total Union 
Budget, indicating that the Ministry continues to occupy a relatively limited fiscal space 
within the broader expenditure framework of the Union Government. The Committee further 
observes that when assessed against India’s estimated nominal GDP of approximately 
₹345.47 lakh crore for FY 2025-26, the allocation to Ayush represents barely about 0.013 per 
cent of GDP. While the Ministry has recorded comparatively higher year-on-year growth 
rates in recent years, the absolute quantum of allocation remains modest, particularly in the 
context of the Ministry’s stated objective of advancing an integrated and pluralistic healthcare 
model. 
2.9.2 The Committee notes that the Ministry has articulated an ambitious long-term 
vision for the Ayush sector under the Ayush @ 2047 framework, including the objective 
of expanding the Ayush market size to a $200 billion economy by 2030 and 5% of the 
country GDP. The Committee observes that achieving such transformative goals would 
require sustained expansion of institutional capacity, research, education and service 
delivery across the sector. However, the Committee is of the view that the present level 
of budgetary support may not be adequate to fully realise these long-term aspirations. 
Incremental increases from a relatively low fiscal base may limit the pace at which the 
sector can scale its programmes and infrastructure.The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Finance consider undertaking a phased and calibrated 
enhancement of the budgetary allocation for the Ministry of Ayush so as to 
progressively strengthen its fiscal position within the overall Union expenditure 
framework. The Committee is of the considered opinion that a structured medium-term 
fiscal roadmap, aligned with measurable expansion in institutional capacity, research, 
education and service delivery, would be essential to ensure that the Ministry’s 
budgetary positioning adequately reflects its evolving policy mandate and long-term 
developmental objectives.

2.10.1 The Committee has examined the movement of funds from Projected Demand to 
Budget Estimates, Revised Estimates and Actual Expenditure during the 15th Finance 
Commission period. The data reveals a three-stage fiscal filtration process. At the Budget 
Estimate stage, significant rationalisation of projected demand was observed in certain years, 
particularly in 2021-22 (approximately 45% reduction from projected demand) and 2025-26 
(approximately 26% reduction). Subsequent moderation between Budget Estimates and 
Revised Estimates occurred in all years under review, with 2023-24 witnessing the sharpest 
mid-year compression (about 17.75%). Notwithstanding these reductions, utilisation of funds 
retained at the Revised Estimate stage remained consistently high in completed years, ranging 
between approximately 93% and 99%. The Committee notes that while projected demands 
were substantially filtered at allocation stages, the Ministry has demonstrated strong 
absorptive capacity in deploying the funds ultimately provided. This pattern indicates that 
fiscal constraint has primarily operated at the allocation stage rather than at the level of 
expenditure execution. 
2.10.2 The Committee appreciates the Ministry’s consistent utilisation performance and 
its demonstrated capacity to effectively deploy funds retained at the Revised Estimate 
stage, however, the Committee observes that there have been recurring and substantial 
rationalisation of the Ministry’s projected demands at both the Budget Estimate and 
Revised Estimate stages. The Committee is of the view that such repeated adjustments 
may affect planning predictability and thereby skewed implementation of the projects. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen the evidence-based 
justification of its projected financial requirements and align its demand projections 
more closely with realistic absorption capacity and phased implementation plans. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry undertake enhanced pre-budget 
consultations and adopt strategic financial planning approach to improve predictability 
in allocation flows and minimise the extent of mid-year rationalisation so that projects 
are implemented as per originally conceived vision. Such measures would help improve 
fiscal credibility, planning stability and better alignment between projected demands 
and actual expenditure outcomes.

2.12.3 The Committee notes that there exists a significant gap between the financial

areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush actively 
pursue the matter with the Ministry of Finance during forthcoming deliberations and 
appraisal meetings and seek enhanced financial outlays so as to ensure that the 
objectives of research promotion, capacity building and quality assurance in the Ayush 
sector are adequately supported during the current scheme cycle.

3.3.1 The Committee has examined the aggregate allocation pattern of the Ministry’s 
schemes, comprising the Central Sector Schemes and the Centrally Sponsored Scheme 
namely the National Ayush Mission (NAM) for the period from AE 2024-25 to BE 2026-27. 
The Committee observes that the schemes have been allocated ₹ 1705.60 crore in BE 2026-27 
which constitutes approximately 39 % of the total Budget allocation of the Ministry. Out of 
the total schemes allocation, the National Ayush Mission accounts for ₹ 1300.00 crore, 
representing about 76.21 % of the overall schemes outlay, thereby reflecting its pivotal 
position and strategic importance within the Ministry’s schemes architecture. The allocation 
under Central Sector Schemes has been enhanced to ₹ 405.60 crore in BE 2026-27 as against 
the Actual Expenditure of ₹ 233.91 crore in 2024-25 indicating an expansionary trend in 
financial commitment.  
 
3.3.2 The Committee expresses serious concern over the substantial downward 
revision of Budget Estimates (BE) at the Revised Estimates (RE) stage for 2025-26 
across several schemes, including the National Ayush Mission which indicates persistent 
underutilization of allocated funds. The Committee emphasizes that recurring BE-to-
RE compression reflects a disconnect between budget formulation and ground-level 
preparedness. The Ministry must ensure that Budget Estimates are aligned with 
implementation readiness, supported by approved action plans and confirmed project 
pipelines. The Committee further recommends overhauling pre-budget appraisal 
mechanisms, instituting strict quarterly monitoring of physical and financial progress 
and enhancing coordination with States and implementing bodies to remove procedural 
delays. A structured capacity-building and execution framework should also be put in 
place to prevent future underutilization and ensure timely and effective deployment of 
allocated funds. 
 
 

24 
 
3.3.3 The Committee further recommends that the Ministry undertake a 
comprehensive review of the implementation architecture under each scheme with a 
view to identifying structural and procedural bottlenecks affecting timely utilisation of 
funds. The Committee is of the view that delays in approvals, procedural complexities 
and implementation constraints may adversely affect the effective translation of 
budgetary allocations into tangible programme outcomes.The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry establish a time-bound corrective action framework 
aimed at improving implementation efficiency. In this regard, greater decentralisation 
of approvals, streamlining of procedural requirements, advance preparation of Annual 
Action Plans and strengthened monitoring of implementing agencies should be 
institutionalised. The Committee also expects the Ministry to present scheme-wise 
corrective measures along with clearly defined timelines in its Action Taken Notes on 
recommendations contained in this report so as to ensure that future budgetary 
allocations at BE stage is not slashed at RE in ensuing financial year so that originally 
conceived goals and objectives are achieved within stipulated timeframe with 
measurable outcomes.  
 
3.4  NATIONAL AYUSH MISSION 
 
3.4.1 The National Ayush Mission (NAM) is the flagship Centrally Sponsored Scheme of 
the Ministry of Ayush aimed at providing financial assistance to States and Union Territories 
for the promotion and development of Ayush systems of medicine across the country since 
2014-15. The Mission seeks to strengthen institutional capacity, expand healthcare 
infrastructure and enhance access to Ayush services in a structured and phased manner.  
 
3.4.2 The Mission supports operationalisation of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (Ayush) by 
upgrading dispensaries and Sub-Health Centres, co-locating Ayush facilities at PHCs, CHCs 
and District Hospitals, establishing standalone Ayush hospitals, supplying essential drugs, 
setting up new Ayush colleges, supporting public health programmes, and strengthening 
UG/PG institutions including PG, pharmacy and paramedical courses. It promotes co-location 
within mainstream public health institutions to offer patients multiple systems of medicine 
under one roof. While the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare supports engagement and 
training of Ayush personnel under NHM, the Ministry of Ayush provides infrastructure, 
equipment and medicines under NAM, reflecting shared institutional responsibility. The 
prominent activities supported under National Ayush Mission till 2025 are as following: 
 
i. 203 units supported for setting up of Integrated Ayush hospitals. 
ii. 518 Ayush Hospitals and 6234 Ayush dispensaries have been supported for Up-
gradation of infrastructure and other facilities.  
iii. 2387 PHCs, 746 CHCs and 319 DHs have been supported under co-location for 
recurring assistance of medicines and contingency on an average in each year. 
iv. 1157 Ayush Hospitals and 12677 Ayush dispensaries have been supported for supply 
of essential Ayush medicines on an average in each year.  
v. 21 units supported for establishment of New Ayush Educational Institutions. 
vi. 78 Under-Graduate and 46 Post-Graduate Ayush Educational Institutes have been 
supported for Upgradation of infrastructure, Library and other things.  
vii. 383 units supported for establishment of New Ayush Dispensaries. 
viii. 1429 Ayush Grams have been supported. 
ix. 12500 Ayush Health &Wellness Centres have been supported

3.5.1 The Committee observes with serious concern that the allocation for the National 
Ayush Mission (NAM) was reduced by 38.75% at the Revised Estimates (RE) stage in 
2025-26. Such substantial reduction is accompanied by poor utilisation of only about 
34% against the RE allocation of ₹ 780.96 crore, leaving an unspent balance of ₹ 513.54 
crore as on 03.02.2026. Such a combination of steep RE reduction and low expenditure 
reflects weaknesses in expenditure planning and evenly distributed expenditure in each 
quarter of financial year under the scheme. The Committee further finds that similar 
BE-to-RE reductions have occurred in preceding years (-18.75% in 2022-23, -32.08% in 
2023-24, -12.83% in 2024-25, and -38.75% in 2025-26), indicating a recurring structural 
issue rather than an isolated instance.  
 
3.5.2 The Committee notes that although there is increasing budgetary support 
provided to the Mission at the RE Stage. However, the Committee observes with 
concern that the trend of recurring pattern of reductions at the Revised Estimates stage 
and suboptimal fund utilisation during the past five years, which indicate persistent 
weaknesses in planning and absorptive capacity. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry undertake a comprehensive review of its annual 
budgeting practices under the Mission so as to align Budget Estimates more realistically 
formulated & allocated with preconceived implementation capacity, project execution 
readiness and bettering expenditure trends. The Committee further recommends for 
evenly distributed expenditure in each quarter of financial year. 
 
3.5.3 The Committee finds that the slow pace of fund utilisation has been attributed to 
the transition to the Single Nodal Agency (SNA-SPARSH) fund flow mechanism. While 
acknowledging the objective of improving transparency and financial discipline, the 
Committee emphasises that procedural reforms must not impede programme 
implementation pace. The Committee therefore, recommends that the Ministry institute 
structured and time-bound capacity-building measures for States/UTs to ensure 
seamless operationalisation of SNA accounts towards intended purpose. The Central 
Programme Management Unit (CPMU) may play pro-active & facilitative role by 
providing technical guidance, resolving onboarding bottlenecks and monitoring state-
wise compliance so that the fund flow reform must strengthens funds utilization rather 
than catalyze procedural delays. 
 
3.5.4  The Committee further observes that persistent delays in utilisation points 
out towards inadequacies in real-time financial and physical monitoring. The absence of 
periodic corrective review results in accumulation of unspent balances and subsequent 
RE-stage reductions. The Committee, therefore, recommends institutionalisation of a 
structured quarterly review mechanism at both Central and State/UT levels, integrating 
financial progress with measurable physical outputs. The framework should enable 

26 
 
early detection of bottlenecks by Central Project Management Unit and timely 
reallocation of funds to better-performing components, ensuring optimal utilisation 
within the financial year and reducing reliance on post-facto budgetary compression. 
 
3.5.5 The Committee observes that while policy emphasis has been placed on 
integration of Ayush services with mainstream healthcare, convergence with NHM 
remains uneven across States. Referral protocols, co-location models, and digital 
integration with national health platforms are not uniformly operationalised. Digital 
systems such as A-HMIS and real-time dashboards remain underutilised in several 
regions. The Committee recommends structured convergence of Ayush services with 
NHM through defined Standard Operating Procedures for co-location, promoting cross 
referral pathways and integration into national public health programmes, particularly 
in Non Communicable Diseases, maternal, and geriatric care. Full operationalisation of 
digital platforms including A-HMIS integration, telemedicine services, and real-time 
project tracking systems, should be prioritised alongside capacity-building initiatives to 
ensure effective utilization of allocated funds. 
 
3.5.6 The Committee has been given to understand regarding the detailed status 
statement of construction projects furnished by the Ministry that against a total 
sanctioned cost of ₹1232.21 crore, an expenditure of ₹ 746.55 crore has been incurred up 
to December 2025, reflecting an overall utilisation of approximately 60%. The project-
wise position reveals significant inter-State variation in physical progress, with only a 
limited number of projects completed and a substantial number remaining in Phase-I, 
Phase-II or preliminary stages. This uneven progress indicates differential 
implementation capacity and possible bottlenecks affecting timely completion. The 
Committee recommends that the Ministry adopt a structured, risk-based monitoring 
framework for infrastructure projects under the Mission. Projects progressing as per 
approved timelines may continue under routine review whereas projects that have 
exceeded stipulated completion schedules or exhibit disproportionately slow progress 
should be subjected to intensified supervision, including periodic senior-level review and 
mandatory reporting. The Committee further recommends that fund releases be 
progressively aligned with verified physical progress to prevent idle parking of funds. 
The Digital tracking mechanism of projects need to be strengthen Clear accountability 
mechanisms should be instituted in cases of avoidable delay attributable to 
administrative lapses at the State or implementing agency level. 
 
Third Party Evaluation of Ayushamn Arogya Mandir (Ayush)  
 
3.5.7 The Committee has taken note of the third party evaluation findings fursnished 
by the Ministry and is of the view that strengthening of AAM (Ayush) must be 
supported by measurable accountability mechanisms. The Committee recommends 
development of a State/UT-wise performance benchmarking framework based on 
indicators such as human resource availability, patient footfall, NCD screening 
coverage, telemedicine usage, medicine availability, and convergence with national 
health programmes. A composite performance index may be published annually and 
underperforming States/UTs should submit time-bound corrective action plans. The 
Committee further recommends that a portion of central assistance under NAM/AAM 
(Ayush) be specified to capacity building measures and IEC component activities for 
effective implementation of scheme and generating public awareness of the scheme. 
 
3.5.8 The Committee also recommends publication of an Annual AAM (Ayush) 
Performance Report in the public domain detailing infrastructure status, HR position, 
financial utilization, and service delivery indicators to enhance transparency and 

27 
 
legislative oversight. Additionally, a structured social audit mechanism with beneficiary 
feedback, public disclosure of services and medicine availability and grievance redressal 
tracking mechanism should be institutionalized to strengthen community participation 
and accountability. 
 
3.6  PROMOTION OF INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION 
 
3.6.1 The Central Sector Scheme for Promotion of International Co-operation sector in 
Ayush aims to enhance global awareness, recognition and acceptance of Ayush systems of 
medicine through structured international engagement. The scheme supports activities such as 
exchange of experts and officials, incentives to Ayush entrepreneurs and institutions for 
participation in international exhibitions and trade fairs, initiatives for international market 
development including establishment of Ayush Academic Chairs abroad, translation and 
publication of Ayush literature in foreign languages, establishment of Ayush Information 
Cells and health centres in other countries and provision of fellowships and scholarships to 
foreign nationals for pursuing Ayush education in India. Through these interventions, the 
scheme seeks to strengthen academic collaboration, promote global dissemination of 
authentic Ayush knowledge and expand international trade and institutional cooperation in 
Ayush systems. 
 
The budgetary allocation for IC scheme is tabled below:

3.6.2 The Committee notes that as against the actual expenditure of ₹103.86 crore in 2024-
25, an amount of ₹136 crore was allocated for 2025-26, which was subsequently revised to 
₹142 crore at the RE stage. The Ministry has informed that the increase in allocation at the 
RE stage in 2025-26 was primarily to meet the additional financial requirement of ₹15.10 
crore for organizing the Second WHO Global Summit on Traditional Medicine. Further, the 
allocation also includes provisions for two secondment positions at the P-4 level in the World 
Health Organization (WHO), apart from financial requirements towards supporting Ayush 
Chairs abroad. 
 
The Ministry of Ayush has undertaken following activities in 2025: 
i. Establishment of 3 Ayush information Cells in Belarus, Muscat (Oman) and Slovakia 
ii. 2 Country-to-country MoU with Angola and Kazakhstan 
iii. 5 Institute level MoU with Indonesia, USA, Ghana, Magnolia and Cuba

3.6.3 The Ministry also informed the Committee about the outcomes of the International 
Cooperation that a landmark development with signing of International Classification of 
Health Intervention (ICHI) Agreement with World Health Organisation (WHO). Further, the 
Ministry would also establish a BIMSTEC Centre of Excellence in India. The Ministry has 
also signed multinational cooperation in traditional mediciene in SCO, BRICS, IBSA, G20 

28 
 
etc. Moreover, FTA with European union and New Zealand, traditional medicine has been 
incorporated which will enable the traditional medicine practitioners to practice Ayurveda 
and yoga in those countries. 
 
3.6.4 The Committee observes that there has been a consistent increase in allocation 
under the Scheme for Promotion of International Cooperation, reflecting the 
Government’s commitment to strengthening India’s global leadership in traditional 
medicine. The Committee appreciates the Ministry for its sustained efforts for scaling of 
activity with substantial increased allocation particularly the execution of the ICHI 
agreement, the constitution of the BIMSTEC Task Force on Traditional Medicine, 
active multilateral cooperation across various international forums and the organisation 
of global events that have enhanced India’s diplomatic and technical stature in this 
sector. The Committee further notes with satisfaction that India’s digital initiatives, 
including the Aysuh Grid and AI-based innovations have received recognition at the 
WHO level, thereby reinforcing India’s leadership in integrating traditional medicine 
with emerging technologies. 
 
3.6.5 The Committee expresses pleasure over India’s growing global leadership in 
traditional medicine reflected through global engagement and outcomes generated in 
form of MoU, Academic Chair etc.  However, the Committee is of the view that these 
gains need consolidation and sustained once further through recently launched Ayush 
Global Portal. The Committee has also reviewed the Ayush Global Portal and the 
information available for the users on real time basis finding that Ayush Global Portal 
has not been much encouraging as the country specific information is not updated 
including for India and lacks user friendly interface. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends for strengthening of Ayush Global Portal to enhance global visibility, 
interoperability and access to authentic services. The Committee also recommends that 
the Ministry leverage the proposed digital interface to facilitate international 
collaboration, knowledge exchange and global access to authentic Ayush services 
including tele-consultation, academic partnerships and dissemination of evidence-based 
resources. Such an integrated platform would enhance India’s soft power in traditional 
medicine while improving transparency, accessibility and global engagement with 
Ayush systems. 
 
3.6.6 The Committee appreciates the extensive diplomatic outreach under the 
International Cooperation Scheme, including the signing of 27 bilateral MoUs, 
establishment of 15 Ayush Academic Chairs, and operationalisation of 43 Ayush 
Information Cells across 43 countries. However, the Committee is of the view that the 
momentum of global recognition should be sustained through structured and outcome-
oriented engagement. The Committee therefore recommends the Ministry to identify 
priority partner countries and develop targeted collaboration frameworks focusing on 
joint research projects, academic exchanges, clinical studies and capacity-building 
initiatives in traditional medicine so as to deepen scientific engagement and strengthen 
institutional linkages. The Committee further recommends instituting a structured 
performance assessment mechanism with periodic country-wise outcome reports to 
evaluate the operational status of MoUs, academic output of Chairs and the functional 
impact of Ayush Information Cells.  
 
3.7 INFORMATION, EDUCATION & COMMUNICATION (IEC) 
 
3.7.1 The IEC scheme is a strategic public outreach framework designed to create 
awareness, promote informed health choices and encourage adoption of preventive and 
promotive practices under the traditional systems of medicine. These activities aim to 

29 
 
disseminate authentic information, counter misinformation and enhance public confidence in 
Ayush systems through mass media campaigns, community engagement, observance of 
health days, publications and digital platforms. By fostering behavioural change and 
integrating traditional knowledge with contemporary health needs, the IEC initiative plays a 
vital role in strengthening holistic healthcare awareness at national and international levels. 
3.7.2 The Annual Report 2025-26 mentions that the Ministry has undertaken following IEC 
awareness activities in 2025: 
i. Arogya fairs at National Level (Jaipur, Rajasthan) & State Level fair (Jabalpur,MP& 
Shillong, Meghalaya) 
ii. Financial Assistance for three Ayurveda Parv / Yoga Fest 
iii. Financial assistance for two Seminars 
iv. Participation in  eight health fair/mela including Bharat Parv 2025 & IITF, Delhi 
v. Celebration of four health days i.e Unani, Homoeopathy, IDY, Ayurveda.

budgetary allocation on IEC 2022-23, 2023-24, 2024-25, 2025-26 is enumerated below: 
 
  
3.7.3 The Committee observes that the Budget Estimates under the IEC Scheme have 
largely remained at a stable level over successive years with minor modification 
primarily at the Revised Estimates stage to accommodate additional expenditure on 
International Day of Yoga (IDY) celebrations. The Committee recommends that 
adequate budgetary allocation at the Budget Estimate stage be ensured so that IEC 
activities aimed at disseminating Ayush outcomes and awareness at the grassroots level 
are not constrained or made contingent upon mid-year revisions. A more predictable 
and sufficiently provisioned budget would enable sustained outreach to the general 
public beyond event-specific expenditure.  
 
3.7.4 The Secretary, Ministry of Ayush has cited NSSO Survey 2022-23 on Ayush 
indicating that approximately 95 per cent of rural and 96 per cent of urban persons aged 15 
years and above are aware of Ayush systems of medicine. However, the Committee observes 
with concern that only 46.3 per cent of rural and 52.9 per cent of urban persons have been 
reported using Ayush for prevention or treatment of ailments during the last 365 days.

particularly targeting rural and geriatric populations who bear a disproportionate 
burden of lifestyle-related disorders. Given the comparatively half out-of-pocket

3.7.7 The Committee observes that IEC initiatives undertaken by the Ministry 
including fairs, seminars, conferences and promotional campaigns, are presently 
evaluated largely on the basis of the number of activities conducted rather than their 
measurable impact on utilisation of Ayush services. The Committee, therefore, desires 
to be apprised of outcome-based indicators to assess the outreach of initiatives that are 
effectively translating public awareness into behavioural change. The Committee 
therefore recommends that the Ministry establish an outcome-oriented monitoring 
framework under the IEC Scheme incorporating measurable key indicators such as 
increase in utilisation of Ayush services, repeat usage patterns, district-level outreach 
penetration and public confidence indices. Periodic impact assessments should be 
conducted so that IEC strategies may be refined based on evidence of their effectiveness. 
 
3.7.8 The Committee notes that a significant portion of IEC activities’s expenditure 
appears to be associated with large-scale events such as International Day of Yoga 
celebrations and national promotional programmes. While such events play an 
important role in enhancing visibility of Ayush systems, sustained behavioural adoption 
requires continuous and community-level engagement beyond episodic outreach 
activities. The Committee therefore recommends that the Ministry of Ayush may 
strengthen year-round community-based communication strategies, encouraging States 
and local implementing agencies to develop region-specific IEC content in local 
languages addressing area-specific health concerns and disease burdens. Greater 
decentralisation of communication efforts, supported by trained grassrootlevel 
functionaries and community institutions would help ensure deeper and more sustained 
outreach translating into actual utilization. 
 
3.7.9 The Committee is given to understand that International day of Yoga 2025 
celebrated on a massive scale which has led to global recognition with two Guinness 
Book of Records including largest Yoga session (3.02 lakh participants) and mass Surya 
Namaskar (22,122 tribal students). Further, the Ministry plans to launch Ayush 
Samagra Swasthya Mission including “Har Ghar Ayur-Yog- self care model in 75% 
households by 2029” as part of the National Ayush Mission. The Committee appreciates 
the global recognition by IDY in previous years and given the substantial public 
expenditure involved to the tune of ₹ 56 crore in 2025 and ₹ 25 crore in 2024, the 
Ministry may consider setting a progressive roadmap towards achieving 100 % 
household coverage in place of 75% under the “Har Ghar Ayur-Yog” initiative by 2029 
as the name of the initiative itself spells out. The Committee further recommends that a 
structured, advance promotion and preparedness campaign and implementation 
milestones be launched especially in schools, colleges to ensure sustained behavioural 
adoption beyond event-based participation. The Committee further recommends that 
the active engagement of trained Yoga Instructors and certified community-level 
trainers be institutionalised to ensure last-mile outreach and sustained household 
adoption under the “Har Ghar Ayur-Yog” initiative. 
 
3.7.10 The Committee has also been apprised by the Secretary, Ministry of Ayush about 
the current status of the nationwide campaign “Desh Ka Prakriti Parikshan”, under 
which more than 1.40 crore Indian citizens aged 18 years and above have undergone 

32 
 
app-based Prakriti assessment. The Committee appreciates the initiative and its wide 
outreach and recommends that the Ministry of Ayush leverage the data generated 
through these Prakriti Parikshan assessments for developing evidence-based preventive 
health strategies and personalised lifestyle advisories rooted in Ayurvedic principles. 
The Committee further recommends that the Ministry must ensure wider dissemination 
of the campaign through digital platforms, educational institutions and community 
health networks so that a larger segment of the population, particularly in rural and 
underserved areas can benefit from Prakriti-based health awareness and preventive 
healthcare practices. 
 
3.8 AYURGYAN SCHEME 
 
3.8.1 The Ministry of Ayush is implementing the Central Sector Scheme namely 
AYURGYAN Scheme which has 03 components viz.  
 
i. Capacity Building & Continuing Medical Education (CME) in Ayush - involves 
ongoing professional training and development initiatives for Ayush healthcare 
professionals, including doctors, teachers, and paramedics 
ii. Research & Innovation in Ayush- entails the exploration and development of new 
therapies, drugs, and practices within the Ayush systems of medicine 
iii. Ayurveda Biology Integrated Health Research- aims to bridge traditional Ayurveda 
principles with modern sciences like molecular biology, pharmacology, immunology, 
and bioinformatics. 
 
3.8.2 The budgetary allocation for Aurgyan during last five years alongwith percentage 
utilization is enumerated below: 
 
            (Table-15) 
Year BE RE RE vs BE (%)  Actual % Utilisation of RE 
2022-23 15.50 10.85 70% 9.38 86.45 
2023-24 9.00 15.00 214.28% 14.99 99.93 
2024-25 49.50 27.00 54.54 25.70 95.18 
2025-26 50 37.81 75.62 37.50*  99.18* 
2026-27 50 - - - - 
                         *As on 31.12.2025                                                           (₹ in crore) 
 
3.8.3 The Committee notes with concern that the allocation under the Ayurgyan 
Scheme has witnessed significant downward revision at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage 
in consecutive financial years In 2024-25, the Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹49.50 crore was 
reduced to ₹ 27.00 crore at the RE stage, representing a reduction of approximately 45 
per cent, with actual expenditure of ₹ 25.70 crore. Similarly, in 2025-26, the BE of ₹ 
50.00 crore was reduced to ₹ 37.81 crore at the RE stage. The Committee takes into 
account the submission of the Ministry that due to this reduction of allocation at the RE 
stage, funds could not be released to certain projects approved by the Project 
Sanctioning Committee (PSC). 
 
3.8.4 The Committee, while appreciating the efforts of the Ministry in supporting 
research activities under the Ayurgyan Scheme, is of the view that such recurring mid-
year contraction of funds at RE stage adversely affects the expansion and continuity of 
research projects and leads to delays in the execution of approved studies, particularly 
those sanctioned by the Project Sanctioning Committee. Particularly, the Committee 

33 
 
takes into consideration the submission of the Ministry that reduction of budgetary 
allocation of ₹ 50.00 crore at BE to ₹ 37.81 crore at RE 2025-26 led to non release of 
funds to certain projects as approved by the Committee is further of the view that such 
budgetary uncertainty must have undermined the confidence of participating 
institutions and impede the effective achievement of the objectives of the scheme. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry evolve a protected funding 
mechanism for ongoing research projects so as to avoid slashing of budgetary allocation. 
The Committee further recommends that multi-year research commitments under the 
scheme should be planned and funded in a manner that ensures stability and continuity 
and are not subjected to annual budgetary volatility. 
 
3.8.5 The Committee finds that there has been under-utilisation of funds available 
under the Ayurgyan Scheme during 2024-25. The Committee takes into account the 
submissions of the Ministry that the under-utilisation was primarily due to procedural 
and operational issues such as delays in utilisation of funds by implementing institutions 
and balances lying in the Central Nodal Agency (CNA) accounts beyond the admissible 
limits, which prevented further release of funds and resulted in savings. The Committee, 
in this regard recommends the Ministry to take appropriate precautionary measures so 
that the incident of underutilization funds under the scheme is not repeated in the 
ensuing financial year. 
 
3.8.6 The Committee is anguished to observe that on perusal of the list of projects 
which could not receive funds due to under-utilisation and the CNA ceiling limits,  it 
found that several of these projects involved reputed institutions such as the All India 
Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS), New Delhi; All India Institute of Ayurveda 
(AIIA), New Delhi; Rajendra Institute of Medical Sciences, Ranchi; Thiruvalluvar 
University, Vellore; Amrita Institute of Medical Sciences, Kochi; Guru Teg Bahadur 
Hospital, Delhi; and other academic and research institutions. The Committee further 
notes that the proposed studies covered diverse areas including clinical trials, 
pharmacological evaluation of drugs, microbial studies and integrative medicine 
interventions relating to conditions such as psoriasis, cancer, COVID-19 management, 
neurological disorders, depression and other health conditions. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommends that no projects should be deprived of fund.  
 
3.8.7 The Committee is of the view that while financial prudence and adherence to 
procedural requirements are essential but such bottlenecks should not impede the 
timely execution of research projects under a scheme intended to promote scientific 
validation and innovation in Ayush systems of medicine. The Committee in this 
connection, recommends the Ministry to evolve increased level of monitoring of projects 
sanctioned under the Scheme and institute periodic reviews of their progress so as to 
avoid recurrence of such situations in future. The Committee is constrained to observe 
that the non-execution of such PSC-approved studies represents a missed opportunity 
for generating scientific evidence and advancing research in Ayush systems of medicine.

 3.8.9 The Committee observes that under the Ayurgyan Scheme, the Ministry has supported 39 out of the targeted 50 resear78%). 3.9 SCHEME FOR CONSERVATION, DEVELOPMENT AND SUSTAINABLE 
MANAGEMENT OF MEDICINAL PLANTS UNDER NATIONAL MEDICINAL 
PLANTS BOARD (NMPB) 
 
3.9.1 The Ministry of Ayush is implementing a scheme for the conservation, development 
and sustainable management of medicinal plants through the National Medicinal Plants Board 
(NMPB). The Board is responsible for the execution of the scheme in coordination with State 
Medicinal Plants Boards, State Horticulture Departments, and State Forest Departments etc. 
Under this framework, financial assistance is provided to support activities such as cultivation 
of medicinal plants, establishment of nurseries, post-harvest management and other initiatives 
aimed at strengthening the medicinal plant sector and promoting sustainable utilisation of 
medicinal plant resources. 
The budgetary allocation flow table of CSSDMMP is as following: 
 
Table- 16 
Year BE RE RE vs BE (%)  Actual % Utilisation of RE 
2022-23 48.49 48.25 99.50 48.25 100 
2023-24 43.49 20 45.98 19.53 97.65 
2024-25 49.49 40 80.83 40 100 
2025-26 65 35 53 29.59*  84.53* 
2026-27 40 -                    *As on 10.02.2025                                                                 (₹ in crore ) 
 
3.9.2 The Committee finds that the budgetary allocation for the Central Sector 
Scheme for Conservation, Development and Sustainable Management of Medicinal 
Plants has witnessed significant fluctuations between the Budget Estimates and Revised 
Estimates in recent years. The Committee is of the view that such reductions at the 
Revised Estimate stage may affect the continuity and effectiveness of activities relating 
to conservation, resource augmentation and research support. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush, through the National Medicinal 
Plants Board, undertake more realistic assessment of financial requirements and 
strengthen planning and monitoring mechanisms so that funds allocated under the 
scheme are utilized in a timely and efficient manner, ensuring sustained progress in the 
conservation, development and sustainable management of medicinal plants. 
 
3.9.3 The Committee has been apprised by the Ministry that India currently has 1,178 
medicinal plant species in trade, of which 242 species are in high commercial demand and 
that historically a significant proportion of these plants has been sourced from forests. The 
Ministry has informed that it is promoting a shift from forest-based collection to organised 
cultivation through financial incentives, cultivation clusters, and development of quality 
planting material. 

36 
 
3.9.4 The Committee takes note of the policy shift in direction aimed at reducing 
pressure on forest resources to organized cultivation of quality planting material while 
ensuring sustainable supply of medicinal plants for the Ayush industry. However, the 
Committee recommends that the Ministry’s reply to include benchmarks or timelines 
for reducing forest dependency or measurable targets for expanding cultivation of high-
demand species of medicinal plants. The Committee recommends that the Ministry 
transition from a purely promotional role to a target-driven roadmap based on species-
specific categorisation and with charted timelines by mandating a minimum 
procurement quota from organized cultivation clusters for gradually reducing 
dependence on forest-sourced medicinal plants. The Committee further recommends 
that Ministry explore for creating guaranteed market for farmers, effectively bridging 
the price gap between forest-sourced materials and cultivated species. The Committee is 
of the view that Ministry should also check out Action Plan to encourage farmers for 
cultivation of medicinal plants in cluster. 
 
3.9.5 The Committee notes that the Ministry has launched the e-CHARAK portal to 
facilitate information exchange among stakeholders in the medicinal plant sector and to 
provide market price information and buyer seller connect and facility to post an item 
availability for approximately 100 medicinal plant species across different herbal markets in 
the country. 
 
3.9.6 The Committee acknowledges the E-Charak portal as a positive step towards 
improving market transparency in the medicinal plants sector. However, the Committee 
has taken hand on experience of the portal and is of the view that the platform presently 
functions largely as an information interface providing price details and buyer seller 
contact channel which may not fully address the challenges faced by cultivators in 
accessing reliable buyers and stable markets. The Committee further notes that 
transactions between buyers and sellers are ultimately concluded through offline 
payment mechanisms which may increase the risk of fraud, delays in supply and lack of 
transaction security. In this regard, the Committee recommends that the Ministry to 
further strengthen the E-Charak platform by incorporating additional features such as 
a digital trading interface with secure payment processing, a “soil-to-shelf” traceability 
system for quality assurance, certification and demand forecasting mechanisms for key 
medicinal plant species. The Ministry may also undertake focused efforts to promote 
wider adoption of the platform among farmers and Farmer Producer Organisations 
(FPOs) so as to enhance market access and ensure greater transparency and efficiency 
in the trade of medicinal plants. 
 
3.9.7 The Committee finds that the Ministry has supported seed germplasm centres, 
nurseries and rural collection centres under the Central Sector Scheme of the National 
Medicinal Plants Board. The Ministry has also reported support for quality planting 
material and training of farmers in Good Agricultural Practices. However, the 
adequacy of expansion of these initiatives is still the matter of concern. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry undertake an assessment of the national 
requirement of Quality Planting Material (QPM) for medicinal plants and expand 
nursery networks accordingly, particularly in major cultivation clusters in meeting the 
increasing demand for planting material, particularly in view of the expansion of 
medicinal plant cultivation. 
 
3.9.8 The Committee is given to understand that the Ministry has supported initiatives 
for Good Agricultural and Collection Practices (GACP) and voluntary certification of 
medicinal plant produce through capacity building programmes. However, the Ministry 
has indicated that presently no cultivation clusters are fully compliant with GACP 

37 
 
standards which suggest that the adoption of quality certification frameworks remains 
limited. The Committee, in this regard, recommends that the Ministry takes pro active 
approach to develop a structured roadmap for wider adoption of GACP certification by 
providing technical support and financial incentives for cultivation clusters seeking 
certification. 
 
3.9.9 Further briefing the Committee on the subject during the meeting of the Committee 
held on 23.2.2026, Secretary of the Ministry stated as under: 
 
“Regarding the suggestion made by the Chair of using Compensatory Afforestation Fund 
Management and Planning Authority (CAMPA) for medicinal plants, I don’t see any issue in 
that except that CAMPA Fund is used actually in lieu of the forest area which is 
compensated. So, wherever the degraded land is provided or the land is bought and provided 
to the department, these plants can be grown in such areas only provided the local conditions 
and the ecological conditions promote such medicinal plants. So those plantation activities 
which are a part of CAMPA can obviously be taken in.” 
 
3.9.10 The Committee has raised a specific query with respect to the utilization of CAMPA 
funds for growing of medicinal plants. The Ministry has stated that it has proposed for the 
inclusion of plantation of medicinal plants under the Compensatory Afforestation Fund 
Management and Planning Authority (CAMPA) framework and related programmes such as 
“Ek Ped Maa Ke Naam” implemented by the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate 
Change. The Ministry further states that a D.O. letter from the Secretary, Ayush was 
addressed to the Secretary, Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change on 
07.01.2026 and the matter is presently under active consideration. 
 
3.9.11 While appreciating the initiative taken by the Ministry to explore convergence 
with afforestation programmes, the Committee observes that the proposal is still at a 
preliminary stage of inter-ministerial consultation without any timeline for 
operationalising the inclusion of medicinal plant plantations under Compensatory 
Afforestation Fund Management and Planning Authority (CAMPA) supported 
activities. The Committee is of the strong view that integration of medicinal plant 
plantations within national afforestation programmes could significantly contribute to 
sustainable supply of raw materials for the Ayush sector while simultaneously 
supporting ecological restoration and livelihood opportunities. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must pursue the proposal 
with the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change in a time-bound manner 
so that plantation of medicinal plants may be appropriately incorporated within 
CAMPA-supported afforestation activities and related national plantation programmes 
like “Ek Ped Maa ke naam”. The Committee further recommends that, once the 
proposal is finalised, the National Medicinal Plants Board may develop suitable 
guidelines in coordination with State Forest Departments to ensure that plantation of 
medicinal plants are scientifically planned, monitored and linked with conservation as 
well as sustainable utilisation objectives of the Ayush sector.  
 
3.9.12 The Committee is of the considered view that Medicinal plants have been an 
integral part of traditional and modern healthcare systems due to their therapeutic 
potential and bioactive compounds. Medicinal plants offer natural, cost-effective 
remedies with fewer side effects for a wide range of health conditions, including chronic 
diseases, infections, and metabolic disorders. In order to gain maximum benefits of these 
traditional plants, the availability of such medicinal plants are essential. In view of this, 
the Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry of Environment, Forest and 
Climate Change should make it mandatory that, in case of afforestation in degraded 

38 
 
areas, at least 25 percent of the plantation from CAMPA funds should be of medicinal 
plants, suitable for that particular ecology. The Ministry should ensure that plantations 
contribute meaningfully to carbon sinks and biodiversity restoration rather than short-
term target fulfillment. 
 
3.9.13  The Committee is surprised to note the Ministry’s reply that the National 
Medicinal Plants Board (NMPB) does not possess recent data regarding the percentage 
decrease in forest-sourced raw material. The Board has referred to the report “Medicinal 
Plants in India: An Assessment of their Demand and Supply, 2017”, which indicates that 
1,178 medicinal plant species are traded in India, comprising 42% herbs, 27% trees and 31% 
shrubs and climbers. Of these, 242 species are in high commercial demand, with annual 
consumption exceeding 100 metric tonnes. The report further indicates that 15 species (6%) 
are imported, 54 species (22%) are obtained through cultivation, 59 species (25%) are 
collected from landscapes outside forests and 114 species (47%) continue to be harvested 
from forests.  
 
3.9.14 The Committee observes that the assessment of data regarding the percentage 
decrease in forest sourced raw material is nearly a decade old and the demand-supply 
dynamics of medicinal plants might have undergone significant changes in view of the 
Ministry’s recent initiatives for promotion and cultivation of medicinal plants. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry should undertake a fresh national 
assessment of demand and supply of medicinal plants to generate updated evidence for 
informed policy planning and scientific development of cultivation clusters. The 
Committee further recommends for establishing a dynamic National Medicinal Plants 
Demand-Supply Database, preferably integrated with the e-Charak Portal and updated 
periodically in coordination with industry stakeholders, State Medicinal Plants Boards 
and Ayush manufacturers. Such a mechanism would enable identification of high-
demand species, facilitate better planning of cultivation initiatives and help address 
issues relating to market uncertainty and price volatility in the medicinal plants sector. 
 
3.9.15 The Committee takes note of the submission of the Ministry that targets such as 
5 medicinal plant species conservation projects, 1500 hectares in-situ resource 
augmentation and 13.5 lakh saplings regeneration have been undertaken. The 
Committee, however, observes that the target set by the Ministry appear limited 
considering India’s vast medicinal plant biodiversity. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that while conservation initiatives are being undertaken, the scale of 
interventions need to be expanded to address threats to rare and endangered medicinal 
plant species. The Committee also recommends the Ministry to scale up conservation 
programmes, particularly for rare, endangered and endemic medicinal plant species, 
and promote community participation in conservation efforts. 
 
3.9.16 The Committee takes recent press release that the National Medicinal Plants Board 
has introduced a component titled Medicinal Plants Business Centre (MPBC) under the 
Central Sector Scheme for Conservation, Development and Sustainable Management of 
Medicinal Plants with the objective of strengthening post-harvest management, storage, 
grading, value addition, packaging and quality testing of medicinal plant produce. The 
Committee is of the view that the medicinal plant sector in the country continues to face 
challenges relating to fragmented value chains, inadequate post-harvest infrastructure and 
limited market-oriented processing facilities which often affect quality and reduce the 
economic returns to cultivators and collectors. 
 
3.9.17 The Committee welcomes the introduction of Medicinal Plants Business Centre 
(MPBC) as an important intervention towards strengthening the value chain mechanism of 

medicinal plants,   however, the Committee desires that the funding pattern, scale, implementation framework or timelines for establishment of such centres must be made clear. The Committee therefore recommends that the Ministry of Aestablishment of Medicinal Plants Business Centres with adequate financial provisioning for training local farmers & Farmer Producer Organisationsreduce post-harvest losses, facilitate valuemedicinal plants sector. The Committee further recommends thateach centre be tailored to the specificcatchment area to ensure 100% capacity utilization. 3.10.
