3.20.2 The Committee notes that the National Institute of Ayurveda is delivering substantial 
patient-centric services with increased 3.52 lakh OPD cases and 68,886 IPD admissions in 
2025-26 despite operating with a limited hospital capacity of 280 beds. The Committee 
appreciates that the Budget Estimate (BE) allocation of ₹ 227.25 crore for 2026-27 is broadly 

61 
 
justified considering the scale of clinical services, academic responsibilities and national role 
of the institute. However, the Committee observes that the BE 2026-27 allocation reflects a 
reduction compared to the Revised Estimate (RE) of ₹ 249.61 crore in 2025-26, which may 
constrain the institute’s expansion and service delivery. The Committee also notes with 
concern that the satellite centre at NIA, Panchkula has not been fully operationalised as of 
23.02.2026. Given the significant patient load at the Jaipur campus, the delay in 
operationalising the Panchkula centre has limited the institute’s ability to distribute clinical 
demand and expand regional outreach.  
 
3.20.3 The Committee emphasises that the existing patient load at NIA, Jaipur indicates the 
need for augmentation of hospital infrastructure and bed capacity. The Committee therefore 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush expedite the full operationalisation of the Panchkula 
campus and prioritise capital outlay for expansion of hospital bed capacity and clinical 
infrastructure at NIA, Jaipur so as to strengthen service delivery and prevent institutional 
strain. 
 
3.20.4 The Committee notes that the National Institute of Ayurveda has an inverted 
admission seat structure wherein the postgraduate (PG) intake of 130 seats exceeds the 
undergraduate (UG) intake of 125 seats. Conventionally, in medical and health education 
institutions, the UG intake forms the primary feeder base while PG seats are proportionately 
lower to maintain a sustainable academic pipeline.  
 
3.20.5 The Committee observes that an inverted admission seats structure of NIA, 
Jaipur could dilute the organic academic progression within the institution and may 
also raise concerns regarding the adequacy of clinical exposure, faculty availability and 
teaching infrastructure required for a large PG cohort. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush review the student-faculty-clinical load balance 
and overall academic structure of the institute to ensure that the UG-PG seat ratio 
remains academically sustainable and aligned with regulatory norms. The Ministry may 
consider gradually increasing UG seats to strengthen the institutional academic 
pipeline; or rationalising PG intake in accordance with available teaching faculty, 
clinical material and infrastructure. 
 
3.21 RASHTRIYA AYURVEDA VIDYAPEETH (RAV) 
 
3.21.1 The Committee has been given to understand that the Rashtriya Ayurveda Vidyapeeth 
is implementing the Guru Shishya Parampara programme with the objective of preserving 
and transmitting traditional Ayurvedic knowledge through mentorship between experienced 
practitioners (Gurus) and young Ayurveda graduates (Shishyas). The programme presently 
covers 100 Gurus and 250 students, with a stipend of ₹ 56,500 per student and an honorarium 
of ₹ 63,000 per Guru under Certificate of Rashtriya Ayurveda Vidyapeeth (CRAV) course.  
 
3.21.2 The Committee acknowledges that the Guru Shisya Parampara programme in 
RAV plays an important role in bridging the gap between classical textual knowledge 
and practical clinical application. However, the Committee observes that the coverage of 
the programme remains limited in scale considering the large pool of experienced 
traditional practitioners and the growing demand for advanced practical training 
among Ayurveda graduates. The Committee, therefore recommends that the Ministry 
may consider expanding the scope and outreach of the Guru-Shishya Parampara 
programme by increasing the number of participating Gurus and Shishyas and 
ensuring wider geographical coverage.  
 
 

62 
 
3.22 CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN SIDDHA  
 
3.22.1 The Ministry informed the Committee that the Central Council for Research in Siddha 
(CCRS), Chennai is actively strengthening its research framework. Over the past two years, 
the Council has launched 27 new intramural projects focused on medicinal plant surveys, 
literary research, drug standardization, and the clinical validation of Siddha principles. 
Currently, 19 clinical research projects are underway. Additionally, the Council is preserving 
ancient knowledge through Mission P2P (Palm to Paper), an initiative dedicated to the 
collection, digitization, translation, and publication of classical palm-leaf manuscripts. 
 
3.22.2 The Committee observes that these initiatives play an important role in 
promoting research and documentation of the Siddha system of medicine. However, the 
Committee is of the view that Siddha research activities and institutional presence 
remain largely concentrated in the southern region of the country, particularly in Tamil 
Nadu. The Committee further observes that the North Eastern Region, with its rich 
biodiversity and strong traditions of herbal medicine, offers significant potential for 
expanding Siddha research and medicinal plant cultivation. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush explore the expansion of Siddha research 
activities and institutional presence in suitable regions beyond southern India, including 
the North Eastern States/Railways/PSUs/CGHS facilities, in order to promote wider 
dissemination of Siddha knowledge and strengthen research on medicinal plants 
relevant to the Siddha system. The Committee believes that such initiatives would 
contribute to the broader development and national outreach of the Siddha system of 
medicine. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

63 
 
CHAPTER-IV 
ISSUES PERTAINING TO AYUSH 
 
The Chapter deals with the some of the major issues affecting the Ministry of Ayush and 
raised by the Committee Members during the course of the meeting of the Committee on 
Demand for Grants 2026-27. During the course of examination of the Demands for Grants of 
the Ministry of Ayush on 23rd February, 2026, the Members of the Committee raised several 
issues seeking the intervention of the Ministry for strengthening the Ayush sector, enhancing 
awareness and promotion of Ayush systems in the country, and for the consolidation, 
propagation, expansion and globalization of Ayush systems of medicine abroad. These 
concerns have been reflected in various parts of the Report. The Committee, however, intends 
the Ministry should to place further emphasis on the following areas: 
ISSUES RAISED BY THE MEMBERS 
4.1 PROPAGATION OF AYURVEDA 
4.1.1 The Member has sought to know the efforts being undertaken by the Ministry for the 
propagation of Ayurveda in the country, particularly with regard to medicinal plants such as 
Aloe vera, Giloy and Sarpagandha etc. The Ministry has informed that the Pharmacopoeia 
Commission for Indian Medicine and Homoeopathy (PCIM&H) has laid down formulary 
specifications and pharmacopoeial monographs of Ayurvedic drugs and Ayurveda is being 
promoted through teaching institutions and by Ayurveda practitioners providing treatment to 
patients. Clinical and experimental studies are also being undertaken at the National Institute 
of Ayurveda (NIA), Jaipur. In addition, multiple patient advisories have been developed and 
initiatives such as the Ayurswasthya Yojana aim to facilitate the rollout of authentic classical 
Ayush interventions. Awareness initiatives such as “Desh Ka Prakriti Parikshan” have also 
been undertaken to generate awareness about the concept of Prakriti. However, the Ministry 
has informed that there is no dedicated scheme exclusively for the propagation of Ayurveda 
and that all Ayush systems are being promoted collectively through Information, Education 
and Communication (IEC) initiatives. 
4.1.2 The Committee notes that the Ministry has undertaken several initiatives for the 
promotion of Ayurveda through research institutions, awareness programmes and 
outreach activities. The Committee however is of the view that these efforts need to 
translate into greater adoption of Ayurveda practices and increased use of medicinal 
plants among the general public. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry should intensify its outreach and awareness programmes with a focused 
approach towards encouraging the practical use of Ayurveda at the community level. In 
this regard, wider dissemination of knowledge regarding commonly used medicinal 
plants such as Aloe vera, Giloy and Sarpagandhashould be undertaken through 
educational institutions and community-level campaigns. The Committee expects that 
such initiatives will promote preventive healthcare practices and strengthen the 
grassroots acceptance of Ayurveda across the country. 
4.1.3 The Member have further sought to know the steps being taken to increase the market 
size of Ayush products. The Ministry, through the Ayush Export Promotion Council 
(Ayushexcil), has informed that there are ways to enhance the export potential and global 
market presence of Ayush products. The strategy includes policy initiatives such as expansion 
of Harmonized System of Nomenclature (HSN) codes for proper classification of Ayush 
goods and provision of export incentives through fiscal support for market access and product 

64 
 
registration in foreign countries. The AYUSHEXCIL has also highlighted need for efforts 
towards strengthening quality and standardization through certification benchmarks, scientific 
validation through clinical trials and standardized dosing formats. Further, initiatives aimed at 
increasing global awareness include leveraging digital outreach, educational diplomacy and 
promotion of Ayush through international platforms such as the International Day of Yoga for 
wider global acceptance of Ayurvedic nutrition and wellness products. 
4.1.4 The Committee notes the outreach initiatives taken by the Ministry to enhance 
the global market and export potential of Ayush products through policy support, 
quality standardization and international outreach. The Committee, however, is of the 
view that sustained efforts are required to further strengthen the global recognition, 
standardisation &regulatory acceptance and quality assurance of Ayush medicines and 
wellness products. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must 
intensify its efforts towards strengthening international certification mechanisms, 
scientific validation and market access support for Ayush products in foreign markets. 
The Committee further recommends for extending export incentives and hand holding 
facilitation for foreign market penetration and enhance India’s share in the global 
traditional medicine market and strengthen the international positioning of Ayush 
systems. 
4.2 HIGHER BUDGETARY ALLOCATION AND UTILISATION 
4.2.1 The Member sought to know the action plan of the Ministry to reduce the gap between 
the actual expenditure and the Revised Estimates. The Ministry has informed that during the 
financial year 2025-26, a Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹3992.90 crore was allocated, which was 
revised to ₹3671.82 crore at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage. Out of the RE allocation for 
2025-26, an expenditure of ₹3112.25 crore had been incurred till 28 February 2026 and the 
Ministry has stated that the remaining funds would be utilised by the end of the financial year. 
4.2.2 The Committee notes that a substantial portion of the allocated funds of RE 
2025-26 (around 16%) still remains to be utilised during the closing month of the 
financial year. The Committee is concerned that such last-minute expenditure may lead 
to “March-rush” spending, which has been consistently discouraged by the Ministry of 
Finance as well as by previous recommendations of the Committee. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen its financial planning and quarterly 
monitoring mechanisms to ensure more balanced and timely utilisation of funds 
throughout the financial year. The Committee expects that improved expenditure 
management will enhance financial discipline and ensure more effective implementation 
of Ayush programmes. 
4.3 QUALITY OF AYUSH MEDICAL EDUCATION 
4.3.1 The Member sought to know the steps being taken for improvement in quality 
education and awareness programmes, particularly in Ayurveda. The Ministry has informed 
that several outreach programmes such as Ayurveda Day celebrations, public health 
campaigns and school and community-level sensitisation programmes are being undertaken. 
The National Commission for Indian System of Medicine (NCISM) has also revised the 
syllabus for Ayurveda education by introducing a uniform and competency-based curriculum, 
prescribing minimum standards for infrastructure and strengthening monitoring and 
accountability through digitalisation and research validation. In addition, the Pharmacopoeia 
Commission for Indian Medicine and Homoeopathy (PCIM&H) has organised exposure 
visits for professional students to enhance awareness regarding quality control provisions and 
techniques for ASU&H drugs. 

65 
 
4.3.2 The Committee notes the multiple initiatives taken by the Ministry and the 
regulatory bodies to strengthen the quality of Ayush education through curriculum 
revision, competency-based training and awareness programmes. The Committee, 
however, is of the view that sustained efforts are required to further improve 
interdisciplinary academic standards, research orientationand practical clinical 
exposure for students pursuing Ayush education. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry continue to strengthen faculty development programmes, 
field level research integration and practical training in teaching institutions as well as 
in research councils. The Committee expects that such measures will contribute towards 
producing well-trained Ayush professionals capable of addressing evolving healthcare 
needs. 
4.3.3 The Members sought to know the status of updating the Ayush curriculum to 
incorporate modern diagnostic techniques and research literacy and whether any bridging 
programmes exist to promote cross-disciplinary understanding between Ayush and modern 
medicine. The Ministry informed that the National Commission for Indian System of 
Medicine (NCISM) and the National Commission for Homoeopathy (NCH) have updated 
their respective curricula by incorporating elements such as modern diagnostics, essential 
modern pharmacology, yoga, medical education technology and Basic Life Support (BLS) 
training. The Ministry further stated that the National Institute of Ayurveda (NIA), Jaipur is 
promoting cross-disciplinary understanding through collaborative academic interactions with 
modern medical institutions and through interdisciplinary seminars and academic 
programmes. 
4.3.4 The Committee observes that the Ministry has initiated measures to incorporate 
modern diagnostics and research literacy within the Ayush curriculum and to promote 
cross-disciplinary engagement with modern medical institutions. However, the 
Committee is of the view that sustained and structured efforts are required to further 
strengthen interdisciplinary learning and practical exposure to integrative healthcare 
systems. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry expand bridging 
programmes and institutional collaborations between Ayush and modern medical 
institutions. The Committee expects that such initiatives will enhance the professional 
competence of Ayush practitioners and contribute to the development of a robust 
integrative healthcare framework in the country.  
4.3.5 The Member sought to know the number of Ayush colleges in the country managed 
by private organisations and whether there are any plans to further increase their number. The 
Ministry informed that at present there are 550 private Ayush colleges under the Ayurveda, 
Siddha, Unani and Sowa-Rigpa (ASUS) streams and 251 private Homoeopathic medical 
colleges functioning in the country. 
4.3.6 The Committee observes that a significant proportion of Ayush medical colleges 
in the country are managed by private organisations. However, the Committee is of the 
view that expansion of Ayush education must be accompanied by strict adherence to 
prescribed standards of infrastructure, faculty strength and quality of training. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen its monitoring and 
regulatory mechanisms to ensure that private Ayush institutions maintain the required 
academic and clinical standards. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry 
should make concerted efforts to ensure the complete coverage of the country.  The 
Committee believes that such measures will help in maintaining the quality and 
credibility of Ayush medical education while supporting its expansion in the country. 
 

66 
 
 
4.4 SHORTAGE OF AYUSH DOCTORS& ALLIED HEALTHCARE 
4.4.1 The Member has highlighted the shortage of Ayush doctors in NABH-certified 
hospitals and emphasised the need to address the gap. The Ministry has informed that the 
shortage is being addressed through ongoing direct recruitment of doctors and by engaging 
practitioners on a contractual basis wherever required. 
4.4.2 The Committee takes note of the steps taken by the Ministry to address the 
shortage of Ayush doctors through recruitment and contractual engagement. The 
Committee, however, is of the view that a long-term strategy is required to ensure 
adequate availability of qualified Ayush practitioners in healthcare institutions. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry, in coordination with State 
Governments, undertake periodic structured assessment of manpower requirements 
and ensure timely recruitment of Ayush doctors. The Committee expects that the 
Ministry assess the feasibility of replacing contractual doctors with regular doctors for 
improved learning and better education. 
4.4.3 The Member also highlighted the shortage of Ayurvedic and Homoeopathic doctors in 
Government hospitals across the country, particularly in the State of Maharashtra, and 
emphasised the need for their recruitment to strengthen AYUSH healthcare services. In 
response, the Ministry informed that a total of 91,781 practitioners of Homoeopathy are 
registered in the State of Maharashtra, out of which 232 practitioners are currently engaged 
under different schemes. However, the National Ayush Mission (NAM) Section did not 
provide any specific clarification regarding the issue and merely responded with the remark 
“Noted”. 
4.4.4 The Committee observes that the response of the Ministry in regard to the 
number of registered Homoeopathy practitioners in Maharashtra, does not adequately 
address the concern raised regarding the shortage of Ayush doctors in Government 
hospitals. The Committee is of the view that availability of qualified Ayush practitioners 
in public healthcare institutions is essential for strengthening service delivery, 
particularly in underserved areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry, in coordination with the State Governments undertake a comprehensive 
assessment of the requirement of Ayurvedic and Homoeopathic doctors in Government 
hospitals and take necessary steps to address the existing shortages through appropriate 
recruitment and deployment. The Committee further recommends for accessing the 
active practitioners in all the system of medicine. 
4.4.5 The Member highlighted the need for appointing two Yoga teachers (one male and 
one female) in each Panchayat school and making Yoga sessions mandatory in schools across 
all States and Union Territories. In response, the Ministry informed that under the guidelines 
of the National Ayush Mission (NAM), there is a provision for engagement of one male and 
one female Yoga Instructor at each Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AYUSH). These instructors 
are expected to conduct outreach activities, including visits to nearby schools, to promote 
Yoga practices, create awareness about healthy lifestyles and encourage preventive 
healthcare. The Ministry further stated that States and Union Territories may undertake Yoga-
related activities under the flexi-pool component of the National Ayush Mission. 
4.4.6 The Committee observes that Yoga plays a vital role in promoting preventive 
healthcare and improving physical and mental well-being, particularly among school-
going children. While the existing provision under the National Ayush Mission enables 

67 
 
Yoga instructors to conduct outreach activities in schools, the Committee is of the view 
that a more structured approach is required to promote Yoga practices among students. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry, in coordination with the 
Ministry of Education and State Governments, explore the feasibility of strengthening 
Yoga programmes in schools and encourage wider adoption of regular Yoga sessions. 
The Committee desires that the Ministry of Ayush should take lead in ensuring regular 
Yoga session at school level and updation of school curriculum to promote healthy 
lifestyle practices and improving the overall well-being of students across the country. 
4.5 AYURVEDA FOR SKIN CARE 
4.5.1 The Member proposed that greater research be undertaken in the field of Ayurveda for 
the treatment and management of skin diseases. The Ministry has informed that Ayurveda has 
significant potential in the management of dermatological conditions and that several 
initiatives have been undertaken to strengthen research, clinical validation and evidence 
generation. Specialized Dermatology Clinics (TwakVikara) and Saundarya Ayurveda Clinics 
have been established for the management of chronic skin diseases. Clinical research studies 
on Ayurvedic formulations and treatment protocols are also being conducted. The All India 
Institute of Ayurveda (AIIA) operates dedicated dermatology OPDs and conducts 
Ayurprabha workshops for skill enhancement in skincare. Further, MD and PhD scholars are 
encouraged to undertake research in dermatological disorders. 
4.5.2 The Committee notes the research initiatives undertaken by the Ministry for the 
management of dermatological conditions through Ayurveda. The Committee, however, 
is of the view that greater emphasis is required to be given on scientific validation and 
wider dissemination of research findings in dermatology domain. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry should intensify the clinical research and 
promote evidence-based studies on Ayurvedic treatments for common and chronic skin 
diseases. The Committee expects the Ministry to ensure dissemination of 
outcomes/findings which will enhance the credibility and wider acceptance of Ayurvedic 
dermatological treatments. 
4.6 AVAILABILITY OF MEDICINE IN AYUSH FACILITIES 
4.6.1 The Member highlighted the need to ensure adequate availability of Ayush medicines 
in district hospitals, Primary Health Centres (PHCs) and Community Health Centres (CHCs) 
and also emphasised the need for establishing Ayurvedic consultation chambers in such 
facilities. The Ministry has informed that under the co-location model, Ayush doctors and 
paramedics are deployed in healthcare institutions, while support for infrastructure, 
equipment and medicines is provided under the National Ayush Mission (NAM). Training 
and human resource support are undertaken in coordination with the Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare. 
4.6.2 The Committee appreciates the co-location model adopted by the Ministry for 
providing Ayush services in public healthcare facilities. However, the Committee is of 
the view that many of the PHCs/CHCs are suffering from shortage of Ayush medicines 
and adequate consultation facilities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry strengthen E-Aushadhi portal for effective management and ensuring 
adequate and timely supply of Ayush medicines and explore the mechanism for setting 
Ayurvedic consultation cell at district and sub-district healthcare institutions. The 
Committee expects that such measures will improve accessibility and utilisation of 
Ayush healthcare services by the public. 

68 
 
4.7 RESEARCH IN POST PARTUM BLEEDING AND LACTATION 
4.7.1 The Member sought to know about the research undertaken in the field of post-partum 
bleeding, early recovery of mothers and improvement of lactation. The Ministry has informed 
that the Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS) has undertaken 
clinical studies including a study titled “Clinical evaluation of the efficacy of Ayush-SS 
Granules in exclusively breastfeeding mothers with insufficient lactation.” The Ministry 
further informed that the Central Council for Research in Siddha (CCRS) is implementing 
community-oriented awareness programmes such as Poshan Abhiyan and Nari Shakti, which 
provide integrated antenatal and postnatal care through Siddha medicines, dietetics and 
lifestyle guidance for pregnant and lactating women. 
4.7.2 The Committee has taken note of research initiatives undertaken by the Ministry 
in the field of maternal health and lactation support through Ayush systems. The 
Committee however, is of the view that greater emphasis is required on expanding such 
research and integrating validated Ayush interventions into maternal healthcare 
programmes. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen 
research efforts and promote community-based interventions for improving maternal 
health, particularly in the areas of post-partum recovery and lactation support.  
4.8 ESTABLISHMENT OF AYURVEDIC HOSPITAL  
4.8.1  The Member emphasised the need for establishing Ayurvedic hospitals in each district 
of Delhi. The Ministry has informed that promotion and development of Ayush systems are 
being undertaken through the National Ayush Mission (NAM), under which financial 
assistance is provided for establishment of new Ayush hospitals and upgradation of existing 
Government hospitals based on proposals submitted by State Governments through State 
Annual Action Plans (SAAPs). The Ministry has further stated that the Government of Delhi 
has recently established a State Ayush Society and has become eligible for financial 
assistance under NAM. A proposal for the year 2025-26 has been received and is under 
consideration. 
4.8.2 The Committee takes note that the establishment and strengthening of Ayush 
hospitals is undertaken through proposals submitted by State Governments under the 
National Ayush Mission. The Committee, however, is of the view that proactive 
coordination between the Ministry and State Government is necessary to expand Ayush 
healthcare infrastructure in Delhi considering the delay in adoption of Ayushman 
Bharat scheme in Delhi. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
actively engage with the Government of NCT of Delhi to facilitate the establishment of 
adequate Ayurvedic hospitals in the National Capital Territory within defined timeline. 
The Committee expects that the Ministry extend adequate support of funds and ensure 
timely release. 
4.8.3 The Member sought to know the plans for establishing an Ayush hospital in 
Moradabad district of Uttar Pradesh. The Ministry has informed that under the National 
Ayush Mission, integrated Ayush hospitals are established based on proposals received from 
State Governments through State Annual Action Plans. The Ministry has further stated that 
no proposal has been received from the Government of Uttar Pradesh for establishing an 
Ayush hospital in Moradabad. 
4.8.4 The Committee notes that no proposal has been received from the State 
Government for establishment of an Ayush hospital in Moradabad district. However, 
considering the growing demand for Ayush healthcare services in the region, the 

69 
 
Committee is of the view that such facilities should be expanded in underserved regions 
like Moradabad. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must 
coordinate with the Government of Uttar Pradesh to explore the feasibility of 
establishing an Ayush hospital in Moradabad under the National Ayush Mission by 
assessing the needs of the Moradabad and providingaccess to traditional healthcare 
services in the region. 
4.9 SCREENING OF STUDENTS IN TRIBAL AREAS 
4.9.1 The Member highlighted the need for conducting health check-ups of students in 
Government schools, including Eklavya Model Residential Schools (EMRS), particularly for 
early detection of Sickle Cell Anaemia in tribal areas. The Ministry has informed that the 
Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS) has undertaken health 
initiatives for tribal students in partnership with the Ministry of Tribal Affairs and the Indian 
Council of Medical Research (ICMR). So far, 14 States have been covered, with 51 EMRS 
included under the programme. A total of 17,911 students have been screened and 52,321 
students have been provided with medical assistance.  
4.9.2 The Committee appreciates for the efforts made for screening for early detection 
of disease and extending healthcare support to tribal students through collaboration 
with other Ministries and research institutions. The Committee, however, is of the view 
that early detection and management of genetic disorders such as Sickle Cell Anaemia 
require wider coverage and sustained intervention at large scale. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry expand such screening programmes to cover 
more tribal districts and strengthen collaboration with the Ministry of Tribal Affairs 
and other health agencies. 
4.10 INTEGRATION OF AYUSH AND ALLOPATHY 
4.10.1 The Member sought to know the strategy being adopted for integration of Ayush and 
modern medicine and emphasised the need for conducting multi-site clinical trials for 
scientific validation and evaluation of long-term effects. The Ministry has informed that 
Memoranda of Understanding have been signed with the Indian Council of Medical Research 
(ICMR) for conducting high-quality research on integrative medicine. The initiative aims to 
formalise integrative healthcare delivery and support policy recommendations through the All 
India Institute of Ayurveda-Academic Consortium for Integrative Health Research (AI-
AciHR). The Ministry has further informed that collaborative research and clinical trials are 
being undertaken with leading biomedical institutions such as AIIMS and the National 
Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS) to generate scientific evidence in 
priority disease areas. 
4.10.2  The Committee acknowledges the collaborative initiatives undertaken by the 
Ministry with premier research institutions for promoting integrative healthcare. The 
Committee is of the view that stronger scientific evidence and large-scale clinical 
validation are essential for effective integration of Ayush with modern medicine. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry expand multi-centric clinical trials 
and strengthen interdisciplinary research to generate robust scientific evidence on 
integrative therapies. The Committee expects that such initiatives will strengthen the 
credibility of integrative healthcare models and promote wider acceptance of Ayush 
systems. 
4.11 UNDERUTILISATION OF FUNDS UNDER NATIONAL AYUSH MISSION 

70 
 
4.11.1 The Members raised concerns regarding the underutilisation of funds under the 
National Ayush Mission (NAM) and sought clarification regarding the increase in the scheme 
allocation from ₹1275 crore to ₹1300 crore. The Ministry has informed that during the 
financial years 2023-24 and 2024-25, the scheme received Budget Estimates of ₹815 crore 
and ₹1046 crore respectively, which were actually utilised to the tune of ₹869.31 crore and 
₹1137.65 crore. However, during the financial year 2025-26, the expenditure was less than 50 
percent of the Budget Estimate till the second quarter due to the transition to the SNA-
SPARSH system. The Ministry has stated that technical support and guidance is being 
provided to implementing agencies to address operational issues arising from the new system. 
4.11.2 The Committee has taken note of challenges faced in fund utilisation under the 
National Ayush Mission due to the transition to the SNA-SPARSH system. The 
Committee believes that the Ministry should have anticipated and acted more 
proactively beforehand and procedural and technical issues should not significantly 
affect the timely implementation of healthcare programmes. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry strengthen technical support mechanisms at Ayush 
Directorate and monitoring systems at State Ayush Society to ensure timely utilisation 
of funds by States and implementing agencies. The Committee further recommends for 
appointment of financial managers in each Ayush Directorates who shall work in 
tandem with State Ayush Society. 
4.11.3 The Members also sought to know the steps taken to support financially weaker 
States/UTs in meeting their 40% share of funds under the National Ayush Mission. The 
Ministry has informed that it has recommended to the Sixteenth Finance Commission that 
financial support be provided as seed money for establishment of Ayush departments or 
strengthening existing units in such States. 
4.11.4 The Committee appreciates that the proposal submitted to the 16thFinance 
Commission for providing financial assistance to financially weaker States as seed 
money for strengthening Ayush infrastructure. The Committee is concerned to note that 
no such seed money has been recommended by the 16thFinance Commission in its 
recommendation. The Committee is of the view that limited financial resources may 
hinder the expansion of Ayush healthcare services in such States. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry explore additional financial support 
mechanisms for States facing financial constraints by providing seed money from NAM 
allocation. The Committee further recommends for setting up Ayush Infrastructure 
Fund having component of each segements of Ayush for meeting such constraints. 
4.12 SUBMISSION OF UTILISATION CERTIFICATES 
4.12.1 The Member raised the issue of significant backlog in submission of Utilisation 
Certificates, particularly under the National Ayush Mission, and emphasised the need to 
address the matter. The Ministry has informed that it has issued a detailed checklist for 
submission of Utilisation Certificates to enable States and Union Territories to submit 
complete and error-free certificates. The Ministry also stated that regular follow-ups are 
conducted with States and Union Territories through meetings and that monitoring is 
undertaken at the Secretary level on a weekly basis. 
4.12.2  The Committee expresses concern over the persistent backlog in the 
submission of Utilisation Certificates (UCs) by States and Union Territories. The 
Committee, however is of the view that delays in submission of Utilisation certificates 
affect financial accountability and timely release of funds for development programmes 
like NAM. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen its 

71 
 
monitoring and coordination mechanisms to ensure timely submission of Utilisation 
Certificates by implementing agencies. The Committee further recommends for 
standardising the Utilisation certificate format and consider the secure digitalisation of 
Utilisation Certificates to ensure compliance. 
4.13 SEPARATE REGULATORY MECHANISM FOR AYUSH DRUGS 
4.13.1 The Member pointed out that the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 does not exclusively 
pertain to Ayush medicines and sought clarification regarding the status of establishing a 
separate regulatory mechanism for Ayush drugs. The Ministry has informed that provisions 
relating to Ayurveda, Siddha and Unani drugs are contained in Chapter IVA and Schedule I 
of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940. Further, the Second Schedule of the Act provides 
standards for Homoeopathic drugs. The Ministry also stated that a separate Ayurvedic, 
Siddha and Unani Drugs Technical Advisory Board and consultative committees exist under 
the current legal framework. In addition, State Governments and Union Territories appoint 
licensing authorities and drug inspectors for enforcement of regulatory provisions related to 
Ayush medicines. 
4.13.2 The Committee notes that regulatory provisions exist for the regulation of Ayush 
drugs under the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, which contains specific provisions for 
traditional systems of medicine. The Committee is of the view that, with the growing 
scope and global expansion of the Ayush sector, there is a need to further strengthen the 
regulatory legislative framework governing Ayush medicines. Moreover, the 
enforcement of the Act lies within the purview of State Licensing Authorities, which has 
often resulted in fragmented implementation, delays, and ineffective enforcement. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry examine the feasibility of 
strengthening the central regulatory mechanism for the regulation and laying down of 
uniform standards of enforcement and enhancing coordination with State Drug 
Authorities to ensure better standardization, safety, and efficacy of Ayush drugs. 
4.14 RESEARCH OUTCOMES BY RESEARCH COUNCILS 
4.14.1 The Members sought to know the number of research papers published by research 
councils such as the Central Council for Research in Homoeopathy (CCRH) and the Central 
Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS). The Ministry has informed that 
CCRAS has published a total of 1,812 research papers during the period 2015-16 to 2024-25 
in various national and international journals. CCRH has published 71 research papers up to 
28 February 2026. Further, the Central Council for Research in Siddha (CCRS) has published 
949 research articles in peer-reviewed and indexed journals and has been granted a patent for 
the process for preparation of “Pooneru”, a formulation used in Siddha medicine. 
4.14.2  The Committee notes the research output produced by various research 
councils under the Ministry of Ayush. However, the Committee is of the view that there 
is a need to further enhance the visibility and scientific impact of Ayush research at 
national and international levels. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry must encourage collaborative research with reputed institutions and promote 
publication of research findings in high-impact national and international journals. The 
Committee expects that the findings are also translated into actual Ayush practice at 
grassroots level. 
4.14.3 The Memberhas sought information regarding the extent to which budgetary 
allocation for Ayush translates into tangible health outcomes, including the proportion of 
funds allocated for research, promotion and infrastructure development. The Ministry 

72 
 
informed that the Ayush Oushadhi Gunvatta Evam Uttpadan Samvardhan Yojana 
(AOGUSY) has an outlay of ₹122 crore for the period 2021-2026 for upgradation of 
infrastructure in State Government pharmacies and drug testing laboratories. The Ministry 
also stated that funds released under the National Ayush Mission have supported 2,023 units 
and that the number of patients availing services at Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (Ayush) has 
increased from 5.07 crore in 2021-22 to 12.65 crore in 2024-25. The Information, Education 
and Communication (IEC) scheme is also utilised for promotion of Ayush systems in the 
country and third-party evaluation is undertaken during revision of schemes. 
4.14.4  The Committee notes the efforts made by the Ministry to strengthen 
infrastructure and expand service delivery under various Ayush schemes. The 
Committee further observes that research institutions under the Ministry receive 
support under the Grant-in-Aid (General) component for undertaking various research 
initiatives; however, these activities have largely remained limited to publications in 
journals rather than resulting in measurable outcomes. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry should undertake periodic independent evaluations and 
impact assessments of the research studies conducted and consider defining the 
expenditure under the GIA-General component for each research project, with a year-
end evaluation to measure outcomes. The Committee expects that such evaluations will 
contribute to improved programme implementation and ensure the optimal utilisation 
of public funds. 
4.14.5 The Member sought to know the plans for establishing an Ayush research centre in 
Shamli district of Uttar Pradesh. The Ministry informed that there is currently no proposal for 
establishing such a centre in Shamli. However, the Central Council for Research in Siddha 
has expressed willingness to participate in the establishment of an Ayush research centre if 
such a proposal is received from the State Government. 
4.14.6 The Committee notes that no proposal has been received for establishing an 
Ayush research centre in Shamli district. However, considering the importance of 
expanding research infrastructure for the development of traditional systems of 
medicine, the Committee is of the view that such initiatives should be encouraged. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry coordinate with the Government 
of Uttar Pradesh to explore the feasibility of establishing an Ayush research centre in 
the region. The Committee expects that expansion of research infrastructure will 
contribute to strengthening research and innovation in Ayush systems. 
4.15 EXPANSION OF AYUSH INFRASTRUCTURE UNDER NAM 
4.15.1 The Member emphasised the need for establishing at least one Ayush hospital in every 
district of the country and sought information regarding the budgetary allocation for 
establishing a 50-bedded Ayush hospital. The Ministry informed that the Sectoral Group of 
Secretaries (SGoS-4) has recommended strengthening secondary and tertiary healthcare 
convergence in accordance with IPHS (Ayush) standards by June 2029. The Ministry also 
stated that under the National Ayush Mission, there are plans to establish five 100-bedded 
integrated Ayush hospitals in Tier-II cities and around 288 districts where such facilities are 
currently unavailable. As per NAM guidelines, a one-time grant of up to ₹15 crore is 
provided for construction and infrastructure along with a recurring grant of ₹1.5 crore per 
annum for manpower, medicines and other operational expenses for establishing a 50-bedded 
integrated Ayush hospital. 
4.15.2  The Committee takes note of the Sectoral Group of Secretaries (SGoS-
4)recommendation for expansion of Ayush hospital infrastructure under the National 

73 
 
Ayush Mission by June 2029. The Committee is of the view that timely implementation 
of proposed 288 Ayush hospitals & otherinitiatives are crucial to improve accessibility 
of Ayush healthcare services across the country. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry must ensure expeditious implementation of proposals for 
establishing integrated Ayush hospitals, particularly in districts currently lacking such 
facilities.  
4.15.3 The Member sought to know the extent of coverage of Ayush hospitals in rural areas 
of Gujarat. In response, the Ministry informed that there are a total of 13 Ayush hospitals 
functioning in rural areas of the State. The Ministry further stated that the State Government 
may seek additional financial assistance for establishment or strengthening of Ayush 
healthcare facilities through the submission of proposals under the State Annual Action Plans 
(SAAPs) in accordance with the guidelines of the National Ayush Mission (NAM). 
4.15.4 The Committee observes that the availability of Ayush hospitals in rural areas 
plays an important role in ensuring access to traditional healthcare services for the 
rural population. The Committee is of the view that the existing number of Ayush 
hospitals in rural areas of Gujarat may not be sufficient to meet the healthcare needs of 
a large rural population. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
encourages State Governments for submitting proposals under the National Ayush 
Mission for expansion and strengthening of Ayush healthcare infrastructure in rural 
areas. The Committee further desires that the guidelines of the National Ayush Mission 
(NAM) be circulated widely among the implementing agencies and State Governments 
so as to facilitate timely submission of proposals and effective utilisation of the scheme 
for strengthening Ayush healthcare infrastructure. 
4.16 ESTABLISHMENT OF MEDICAL HUBS AND DRUG TESTING 
 LABORATORIES 
4.16.1 The Members highlighted the need for establishing medical hubs in different regions 
of the country and strengthening drug testing laboratories in high-capacity healthcare 
facilities. The Ministry informed that currently 34 Government drug testing laboratories and 
108 private laboratories have been approved for testing the identity, purity, quality and 
strength of Ayurvedic, Siddha, Sowa-Rigpa and Unani drugs. The Ministry also stated that a 
three-tier pharmacovigilance network has been established comprising a National 
Pharmacovigilance Centre at the All India Institute of Ayurveda (AIIA), five intermediary 
centres and 97 peripheral pharmacovigilance centres across the country. Additionally, five 
medical hubs have been announced in the Union Budget 2026-27 under the leadership of the 
Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT). 
4.16.2 The Committee notes the existing network of drug testing laboratories and 
pharmacovigilance centres established to ensure the quality and safety of Ayush 
medicines. The Committee is of the view that the present network is not adequate in 
view of the expanding Ayush sector, and there is a need to further strengthen the quality 
assurance infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
explore the establishment of additional drug testing laboratories and strengthen 
pharmacovigilance mechanisms across the country, preferably at district hospitals, 
which would enhance efficiency and help avoid delays in the testing of drugs. 
4.17 INNOVATION AND GLOBAL RESEARCH COLLABORATION IN AYUSH 
4.17.1 The Member sought information regarding the steps being taken to position Ayush 
systems in global research collaboration and to encourage start-ups and biotechnology firms 

74 
 
working in the Ayush sector. The Ministry informed that the scheme for Promotion of 
International Cooperation is being implemented to strengthen global research collaboration in 
Ayush systems. The Ministry also provides time to time support to Indian Ayush drug 
manufacturers to enhance export potential and global outreach of Ayush products and 
services. 
4.17.2  The Committee notes with satisfaction that the global collaboration 
undertaken by the Ministry to promote international cooperation has been encouraging 
for Ayush sector. The Committee, however, is of the view that greater encouragement is 
required for innovation, start-ups and biotechnology firms, MSMEs engaged in Ayush-
based research and product development. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry should strengthen financial support for all the schemes and promote 
MSMEs, start ups to undertake for innovation and promote collaborative research with 
global institutions.  
4.18 AFFORESTATION OF MEDICINAL PLANTS 
4.18.1 The Member raised concerns regarding deforestation and emphasised the need for 
large-scale cultivation and conservation of medicinal plants. The Ministry informed that it is 
implementing the Central Sector Scheme for Conservation and Sustainable Management of 
Medicinal Plants, under which project-based financial assistance is provided to State Forest 
Departments for in-situ and ex-situ conservation and resource augmentation of medicinal 
plants in forest areas across the country. 
4.18.2 The Committee notes the initiatives undertaken for conservation and sustainable 
management of medicinal plants. The Committee is of the view that increased 
cultivation and conservation of medicinal plants are essential to meet the growing 
demand of the Ayush industry. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry strengthen collaboration with State Forest Departments and local communities 
to expand cultivation and conservation programmes for medicinal plants. The Ministry 
should encourage farmers for cultivation of medicinal plants to be used as raw material 
for Ayush drugs and finally the Ministry may explore Ayush market in rural areas 
involving farmers & their family under the scheme suggested as “Tera Tujh ko Arpan” 
ensuring handsome increase in farmer’s income and having sound health through 
Ayush products. 
4.19 INTERACTION/COMMUNICATION IN TRIBAL AREAS 
4.19.1 The Member emphasised the need for improved engagement with tribal communities 
to better understand their traditional knowledge systems related to health and longevity. The 
Ministry informed that the National Institute of Homoeopathy (NIH), Kolkata observed 
Janjatiya Gaurav Varsh from 15 November 2024 to 15 November 2025 and conducted 
various programmes aimed at strengthening healthcare awareness among tribal communities. 
The All India Institute of Ayurveda (AIIA) has also been assigned the responsibility of 
addressing adolescent anaemia in tribal areas of Bastar district in Chhattisgarh under Project 
Utkarsh. 
4.19.2  The Committee appreciates the outreach initiatives undertaken for 
promoting health awareness in tribal communities. The Committee, however, is of the 
view that greater engagement with tribal populations is necessary for understanding 
and documenting traditional health practices. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry strengthen community outreach programmes with tribals and 
promote documentation of traditional medicinal knowledge prevalent among tribal 

75 
 
communities. The Committee furtherdesires that the knowledge so accumulated may be 
integrated in local research repositories and used for treating the people. 
4.19.3 The Members expressed the need for sending research scholars to tribal areas to study 
traditional medicinal systems practiced by tribal communities, particularly for addressing 
prevalent diseases such as malaria and dengue. In response, the Ministry informed that the 
Central Council for Research in Siddha (CCRS) has organised a one-day interaction 
programme involving Vaidyars and ethnopractitioners to document traditional knowledge of 
tribal healers. The Ministry further stated that around 50 such traditional healers participated 
in the interaction and shared their ethnomedicinal knowledge related to various ailments for 
the purpose of research and documentation. 
4.19.4 The Committee notes the one day initiative taken by the Ministry to document 
traditional knowledge of tribal healers through interaction programmes. The 
Committee is of the view that a one-time interaction programme may not be sufficient to 
systematically capture and scientifically validate the rich ethnomedicinal knowledge 
available among tribal communities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry undertake structured field-based research by sending research scholars and 
multidisciplinary teams to tribal areas for systematic documentation, validation and 
preservation of traditional medicinal practices. 
4.20 AYUSHMAN BHARAT YOJNA COVERAGE FOR AYUSH 
4.20.1 The Member raised concerns regarding the limited number of Ayush hospitals 
empanelled under the Ayushman Bharat - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY). 
The Ministry informed that the process for inclusion of Ayush treatment packages under AB-
PMJAY has been initiated and consultations are ongoing with the National Health Authority. 
The proposal has been submitted to the Ministry of Finance for approval. 
4.20.2  The Committee notes that the proposal for inclusion of Ayush treatment 
packages under AB-PMJAY is currently under consideration. However, the Committee 
is of the view that integration of Ayush healthcare services under major national health 
insurance schemes would significantly enhance accessibility and utilisation of traditional 
medicine. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry expedite 
consultations with the National Health Authority and the Ministry of Finance to 
facilitate inclusion of Ayush treatment packages under AB-PMJAY. The Committee 
expects that such inclusion will expand healthcare choices available to beneficiaries and 
promote wider adoption of Ayush systems. 
4.21 NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR YOGA AND NATUROPATHY 
4.21.1 The Ministry has furnished that Central Council for Research in Yoga and 
Naturopathy (CCRYN) is apex central research council for Yoga apart from teaching 
institutions like National Institute of Naturopathy, Pune and Morarji Desai National Institute 
of Yoga, New Delhi (MDNIY). Further, the Yoga Certification Board, functioning under the 
MDNIY is responsible for certifying Yoga professionals and accrediting Yoga institutions. 
Similarly, Naturopathy Registration Board (NRB) is established under aegis of National 
Institute of Naturopathy, Pune is responsible for the central registration of Naturopathy 
practitioners and accreditation of its educational institutions. 
4.21.2 The Committee notes that the National Commission for Indian System of Medicine 
(NCISM) and the National Commission for Homoeopathy (NCH) have been established 
primarily to regulate medical education, academic standards and institutions imparting 

76 
 
education in their respective systems of medicine. The Committee observes that these 
Commissions perform important functions relating to curriculum development, accreditation 
of colleges and maintenance of national registers of practitioners. The Committee also notes 
that there exists a regulatory body for different Ayush systems except Yoga and Naturopathy 
at central level, however Yoga & Naturopathy presently lack a central statutory regulatory 
framework for education and practitioner registration.  
4.21.3 The Committee notes the steps taken by the existing regulatory commissions 
(NCISM & NCH) in introducing educational reforms such as competency-based 
curricula in medical education. However, in view of the increasing role of Yoga and 
Naturopathy in preventive and wellness healthcare, the expanding number of 
educational institutions, courses and practitioners and the growing international 
recognition of these disciplines, the Committee is of the view that the absence of a 
dedicated statutory regulatory framework may affect the long-term development and 
credibility of the Yoga and Naturopathy. The Committee, therefore, strongly 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush examine the feasibility of establishing a 
National Commission for Yoga and Naturopathy to regulate education standards, 
institutional accreditation and practitioner registration. The Committee believes that 
such a mechanism would ensure uniform academic standards, strengthen professional 
regulation and facilitate the orderly growth of Yoga and Naturopathy education and 
practice in the country. 
4.23 FUNCTIONAL INTEGRATION OF TRADITIONAL MEDICINE 
4.23.1 The Committee notes that India has been moving towards a pluralistic 
healthcare system through co-location models and collaborative infrastructure 
development as envisaged under the National Health Mission. The Committee observes 
that such co-location has expanded healthcare choices for the public and has also 
encouraged research in areas such as herb-drug interactions. However, the Committee 
notes that treatment pathways in most institutions continue to function largely within 
their respective systems with modern medicine and Ayush services operating in parallel 
rather than in a inter- coordination manner. The Committee is of the view that greater 
clinical collaboration between the two systems can significantly enhance continuity of 
care, promote better utilisation of Ayush services and support the development of 
evidence-based integrative healthcare models. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry of Ayush, in coordination with the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare explore the possibility of devising Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) for 
structured cross-referral between modern medicine and Ayush departments preferably 
in tertiary care institutions. The Committee further recommends that these protocols 
clearly define referral pathways for appropriate conditions such as rehabilitation, 
chronic disease management, pain management, lifestyle disorders and preventive care, 
while ensuring that each system continues to function within its respective medical 
framework. 
 
4.24 ESTABLISHMENT OF REGIONAL RAW DRUG REPOSITORIES (RRDRS) 
4.24.1 The Committee notes that Ministry of Ayush, through the National Medicinal Plants 

77 
 
Board (NMPB), has initiated the establishment of eight Regional Raw Drug Repositories (RRDRs) & 
one National Raw drug repository across India for documentation, authentication and preservation of 
medicinal plant raw materials. The Committee notes that as on date, five RRDRs (1-NRDR & 4- 
RRDRs) have been established out of the nine originally planned. However, the repositories proposed 
for the Western Himalayan, Eastern Himalayan and Eastern Plateau & coastal plains regions remain 
under process. 
 
4.24.2 The Committee appreciates the efforts of the National Medicinal Plants Board (NMPB) in undertaking systematic documentation through the establishment of Regional Raw Drug Repositories (RRDRs). The Committee believes that these repositories play a pivotal role in the collection, documentation, identification and authentication of raw drugs sourced largely from their respective agro-climatic regions. The Committee, however, observes that India possesses vast biodiversity and immense potential in medicinal plants, while the present number of repositories has limited geographical jurisdiction which may not be sufficient to ensure comprehensive and timely documentation of the country’s rich medicinal plant resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends that NMPB consider establishing at least three additional Regional Raw Drug Repositories, particularly in biodiversity-rich regions such as the Western Ghats, Eastern Ghats and the North-Eastern region, so as to capture the diverse medicinal plant wealth of these ecologically sensitive zones and strengthen the national database of raw drugs for Ayush systems. The Committee also desires that the remaining four Regional Raw Drug Repositories already envisaged under the existing plan viz Western Himalayas, Eastern Himalayas & Eastern Plateau & Western coastal plains be established expeditiously so as to complete the originally proposed network at the earliest. 
4.25 HUMAN RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT: PROMOTION OF AYUSH ALLIED 
 SERVICES 
4.25.1 The Committee notes the submission of the Secretary, Ministry of Ayush, that the 
Ministry has undertaken significant education reforms and aligned the curricula of Ayush 
education with the National Education Policy 2020. The Committee further notes that the 
Ministry’s autonomous institutions have collectively provided treatment to more than 62 lakh 
patients through OPD services and over 2.56 lakh patients through IPD services, while also 
contributing to the training and expansion of the Ayush medical workforce.  
4.25.2 The Committee observes that the increasing strength of doctors and expanding 
patient outreach reflect the growing role of these institutions in service delivery, 
education and clinical training within the Ayush sector. The Committee, however, also 
notes that several Ayush healthcare facilities continue to face shortages of doctors 
especially in Maharashtra which may affect the optimal delivery of services. The 
Committee is further of the view that the absence of a comprehensive and updated 
database of practicing professionals across all Ayush systems, including Yoga 
practitioners limits effective workforce planning and management of Ayush services. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush take necessary steps 
to strengthen the availability of doctors in Ayush facilities and develop a comprehensive 
and regularly updated national database of practicing professionals across all Ayush 
systems, including Yoga, so as to facilitate better workforce planning, policy 
formulation and effective management of Ayush services across the country. The 
Committee is of the further view that the Ministry should introduce legislation for 
Ayush allied services to encourage and promote comprehensive Ayush human resource 
development. 
4.25.3 The Committee comes to know that the number of Ayush CGHS Dispensaries is 
less in comparison to Allopathic CGHS Dispensaries. On the lines of promotion to 

78 
 
Ayush and traditional system of medicines, the number of Ayush CGHS dispensaries 
should also be enhanced or co-located with existing allopathic dispensaries. On the 
service conditions of allied healthcare workforce in Ayush Dispensaries, the Committee 
noted that certain cadres like Ayush doctors, pharmacists, etc. are overburdened with 
present workforce and directly affect the delivery of healthcare services. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department urgently prioritize recruitment 
drives, streamline promotion pathways, time-bound or assured career progression 
scheme to keep the morale of the staff high, skill upgradation and rationalisation of 
recruitment rules. The Committee also notes that certain cadres, particularly 
pharmacists (including Ayurvedic and Homeopathic), have limited or no promotion 
avenues within the existing service structure, leading to career stagnation and affecting 
staff motivation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
undertake a cadre restructuring to establish a clearer career progression pathway for 
such categories of staff, addressing long-standing anomalies which would improve 
retention and ensure a more motivated workforce within the healthcare system. 
4.26 AYUSHMAN AROGYA MANDIR (AYUSH) 
4.26.1 The Committee notes the submission of the Secretary, Ministry of Ayush, that the 
Ministry has operationalised around 12,500 Ayushman Arogya Mandirs under the Ayush 
component and has also undertaken the upgradation of dispensaries to strengthen primary 
healthcare delivery. The Committee further notes that the number of beneficiaries availing 
services under these facilities has increased significantly from about 5 crore to 12.64 crore, 
reflecting the growing utilisation and public acceptance of Ayush services at the grassroots 
level. 
4.26.2 The Committee welcomes the efforts of the Ministry in expanding Ayush-based 
primary healthcare infrastructure and outreach through 12500 Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs(Ayush). Nevertheless, the Committee is of the view that sustained focus on 
adequate staffing, availability of medicines, and strengthening of diagnostic and referral 
linkages would be essential to ensure the effective functioning of Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry continue to 
strengthen the operational capacity of these facilities so that they are able to deliver 
comprehensive and quality Ayush healthcare services to the population, particularly in 
underserved and rural areas. 
4.27 INTERMINISTERIAL COLLABORATION 
4.27.1 The Committee notes that the Ministry of Ayush is increasingly moving towards an 
integrative healthcare approach and has initiated collaborative research with specialised 
institutions such as All India Institute of Medical Sciences and Indian Council of Medical 
Research. The Committee further notes that joint initiatives, including screening programmes 
for tribal students and other collaborative public health interventions, are being undertaken in 
coordination with various institutions and Ministries. The Committee also takes note of the 
Ministry’s proposal to implement programmes for the elderly population in collaboration with 
the Ministry of Women and Child Development. The Committee observes that the expanding 
scope of Ayush across public health, research, preventive healthcare and wellness 
increasingly overlaps with the mandate of several other Ministries and institutions. 
4.27.2 The Committee is of the view that the effectiveness of Inter-Ministerial Ayush 
initiatives would depend upon strong inter-ministerial coordination and clearly defined 
institutional mechanisms for collaboration. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry of Ayush work in close coordination with concerned Ministries, research 

79 
 
institutions and public health agencies to ensure effective implementation, better 
resource utilisation and measurable outcomes from such collaborative initiatives, 
particularly in the areas of integrative healthcare, research and public health 
interventions. The Committee further desires that the outcomes and findings of such 
collaborative initiatives be widely disseminated among stakeholders, healthcare 
institutions and State Governments so as to create greater awareness and facilitate 
effective implementation at the grassroots level. 
4.28  OTHER KEY RECOMMENDATIONS  
4.28.1 The Committee notes that the growing demand for medicines under Ayush presents a significant opportunity to promote the cultivation of medicinal plants across the country. The Committee recommends that the Government encourage and support local farmers to undertake cultivation of medicinal and aromatic plants used in systems such as Ayurveda and Unani through appropriate incentives, assured procurement mechanisms, technical guidance and market linkages. Such initiatives would not only strengthen the raw material supply chain for Ayush medicines but also enhance farmers’ income, promote sustainable agriculture and contribute to rural economic development.  4.28.2 The Committee notes with concern the rapidly rising burden of lifestyle-related non-communicable diseases, including Diabetes Mellitus, hypertension and obesity in the country. Recognizing the strong emphasis of traditional systems on preventive healthcare and healthy living, the Committee recommends that elements of Ayush particularly practices such as Yoga and basic principles of Ayurveda relating to healthy diet and lifestyle- may be gradually introduced in school curricula. Early exposure to these practices can help inculcate healthy habits among children, improve physical and mentalwell-being, and contribute to the long-term prevention of lifestyle diseases. Such initiatives would also help enhance awareness, acceptance, and wider popularity of Ayush systems among the younger generation, thereby strengthening the culture of preventive healthcare in the country.  4.28.3 The Committee is of the view that wider promotion and effective delivery of services under Ayush may be further strengthened through well-structured Public- Private Partnerships. The Committee recommends that the Government explore collaboration with credible private institutions, wellness centres, educational institutions and industry for expanding research, standardization of medicines, training of practitioners, and establishment of integrative wellness facilities. Such partnerships can help leverage private sector expertise, innovation and investment while ensuring adherence to quality and regulatory standards. The Committee believes that encouraging responsible PPP models would improve accessibility of Ayush services, promote evidence-based practices, and enhance the overall reach, popularity and global recognition of India’s traditional systems of healthcare.  4.28.4 The Committee is of the view that greater collaboration between Ayush and modern medicine institutions would help strengthen the evidence base and wider acceptance of traditional healthcare practices. The Committee therefore recommends encouraging collaborative research between Ayush institutions and leading centres of modern medicine to scientifically evaluate therapies, standardize treatment protocols, and generate robust clinical 

80 
 
evidence. Such interdisciplinary research would help identify areas where systems such as Ayurveda and Yoga can complement contemporary medical practice, particularly in preventive care, chronic disease management, and lifestyle disorders. The Committee believes that sustained research collaboration would enhance credibility, promote integrative healthcare, and contribute to improved health outcomes. 
4.29 SECRETARIAT RELATED CAUTION WHILE RESPONDING 
COMMITTEE’S RECOMMENDATION 
4.29.1 The Committee observes that the replies to questionnaires have largely been 
furnished in an institute-wise or section-wise format by the various autonomous bodies 
and organisations under the Ministry. The Committee is of the view that such a format 
often results in multiple responses from different institutions to the same question, 
making the information voluminous and at times leading to fragmented responses and 
varying interpretations of the issue raised. The Committee, therefore, desires that the 
Ministry of Ayush should furnish more consolidated format while furnishing replies to 
the queries raised by the Committee. The Committee desires that the Ministry may 
obtain the required inputs from its subordinate offices, autonomous bodies and research 
councils internally and provide a unified and consolidated response to the Committee’s 
recommendations and observation. The Committee believes that such an approach 
would facilitate clearer presentation of information and enable a more effective and 
holistic examination of the Demands for Grants by the Committee. 
  

81 
 
 
CHAPTER-V 
WAY FORWARD:  ROAD AHEAD FOR AYUSH 
 
Vision for Ayush @ 2047 
5.1 India’s traditional systems of medicine- Ayurveda, Yoga & Naturopathy, Unani, 
Siddha, Sowa-Rigpa and Homoeopathy represent a rich heritage of holistic health knowledge 
developed over centuries has the potential to emerge as a major pillar of India’s healthcare 
system as well as a global leader in traditional and integrative medicine. The Committee 
believes that the coming decades present a unique opportunity to transform Ayush from a 
largely tradition-based healthcare system into a scientifically validated,globally accepted and 
economically vibrant sector contributing significantly to public health, preventive healthcare, 
wellness tourism, pharmaceutical innovation and employment generation. The Committee 
believes that achieving the vision of Ayush @ 2047 will require strategic focus on research, 
education, digitalisation, global outreach, industry development and integration with the 
mainstream healthcare system. 
5.2 UNION BUDGETS HIGHLIGHTS FOR AYUSH SECTOR 
 
5.2.1 The Committee notes that the Union Budget 2026-27 has laid renewed emphasis on 
strengthening the Ayush sector through targeted investments in education, research, quality 
assurance and global outreach. The Budget proposes to support the modernization of Ayush 
education and training, expansion and strengthening of Ayush Health and Wellness Centres 
under the National Ayush Mission, and scaling up of research, standardization and quality 
assurance initiatives in traditional medicine systems. 
 
1. Establishment of New All india Institute of Ayurveda 
5.2.2 The Committee notes the announcement regarding the establishment of three 
new All India Institutes of Ayurveda (AIIA) to strengthen education, clinical services 
and research in Ayurveda. The Committee welcomes this initiative as an important step 
towards expanding tertiary-level Ayush healthcare infrastructure in the country and 
strengthening the quality of Ayurveda education, research and advanced clinical care. 
The Committee, however, is of the view that the effectiveness of these institutions will 
depend upon timely& effective implementation of projects/scheme, creation of adequate 
Ayush infrastructure and the development of a robust research ecosystem. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush formulate a 
comprehensive implementation roadmap for the newly announced AIIAs with clearly 
defined timelines, adequate funding support but with aim of measurable outcomes. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry must take steps to introduce more 
trans-disciplinary and inter-disciplinary courses and research programmes, including 
areas such as biotechnology, public health and engineering-integrated healthcare, so as 
to promote innovation and strengthen the scientific advancement of Ayurveda systems 
of medicine. 

82 
 
2. Upgradation of Ayush Pharmacies and Drug Testing Laboratories 
5.2.3 The Committee notes the announcement in the Union Budget 2026-27 regarding 
the upgradation of Ayush pharmacies and drug testing laboratories aimed at 
strengthening the quality assurance ecosystem of Ayush medicines and ensuring the 
availability of skilled technical manpower. The Committee observes that the credibility, 
safety and wider acceptance of Ayush medicines are closely linked to the robustness of 
quality control and standardisation mechanisms within the sector, particularly in the 
context of expanding domestic use and global trade in Ayush products. However, the 
Committee is of the view that concerns relating to quality standardisation, testing 
capacity, authentication of raw materials and adherence to pharmacopoeial standards 
continue to pose challenges for the sector. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry should take steps to strengthen technical capacity through specialised 
training programmes for laboratory personnel, drug inspectors, pharmacists and 
quality control professionals in collaboration with premier research institutions and 
regulatory bodies. The Committee further recommends that the upgraded laboratories 
be effectively integrated with research and standardisation initiatives, including the 
development of validated testing protocols, authentication of medicinal plant raw 
materials and safety evaluation of classical and proprietary Ayush formulations. The 
Committee believes that such measures would significantly strengthen the quality 
assurance framework of the Ayush sector, enhance consumer confidence and facilitate 
the expansion of Ayush product exports and India’s position in the global traditional 
medicine market. 
3. Establishment of five Regional Medical Hubs 
5.2.4 The Committee notes that the Union Budget 2026-27 proposes to launch a 
scheme to support States in establishing five Regional Medical Hubs in partnership with 
the private sector with the objective of promoting India as a global hub for medical 
tourism services. The Committee welcomes all such initiatives as a step towards 
strengthening India’s position in the global medical value travel ecosystem and observes 
that it offers significant potential to expand the global outreach of Ayush systems of 
medicine. Nevertheless, the Committee is of the view that the success of envisaged hubs 
will depend upon effective coordination between the Central Government, State 
Governments and private sector stakeholders. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry of Ayush work in close convergence with the Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare and other concerned Ministries to ensure adequate integration of Ayush 
services within creating effective professional working equilibrium of hubs. The 
Ministry should also ordain a clear framework for infrastructure development, quality 
standards, accreditation and international promotion to maximise the potential of such 
Ayush hubshubs in promoting India’s medical tourism and Ayush sectors. 
4. Upgradation of  the WHO Global Traditional Medicine Centre in Jamnagar  
5.2.5 The Committee appreciates the announcement in the Union Budget 2026-27 for 
the upgradation of the WHO Global Traditional Medicine Centre (GTMC) at Jamnagar 

83 
 
aimed at strengthening global collaboration, research and knowledge exchange in the 
field of traditional medicine. The Committee observes that the Centre holds significant 
potential to position India as a global leader in traditional medicine by promoting 
evidence-based research, standardisation, capacity building and international 
cooperation in Ayush systems. The Committee, however, is of the considered view that 
the full potential of the Centre can be realised only through a well-defined institutional 
framework, enhanced research collaboration and effective global outreach. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush to work in tandem with 
the World Health Organization and other international partners to strengthen research 
networks, develop global standards and promote scientific validation of traditional 
medicine practices. The Committee further recommends that the Centre be leveraged as 
a global platform for policy dialogue, training, knowledge exchange and innovation in 
traditional medicine so as to enhance the international visibility and acceptance of 
Ayush systems and strengthen India’s leadership in the global traditional medicine 
ecosystem. 
5.3 INSURANCE COVERAGE FOR AYUSH TREATMENT 
5.3.1 The Committee notes that despite the growing public acceptance of Ayush 
systems of medicine, insurance coverage for Ayush treatments remains limited and 
inconsistent across different insurance providers. The Committee observes that wider 
and more uniform insurance coverage for Ayush therapies would significantly improve 
the accessibility and affordability of these treatments for the public and support their 
greater integration within the healthcare system. The Committee, however, is of the 
view that the absence of standardised treatment protocols, package rates and regulatory 
clarity continues to limit the inclusion of Ayush therapies within mainstream health 
insurance frameworks. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of 
Ayush work in close coordination with the Insurance Regulatory and Development 
Authority of India (IRDAI) and insurance providers to expand the coverage of Ayush 
treatment packages under health insurance schemes. The Committee further 
recommends that the Ministry should undertake necessary steps to standardise 
treatment protocols and cost structures for Ayush therapies so as to facilitate their 
wider inclusion within mainstream health insurance frameworks and improve access to 
Ayush-based healthcare services. 
5.4 FINANCIAL PROJECTION 
5.4.1 The Ministry has informed that the present budgetary allocation for Ayush has 
witnessed an increase of approximately 246.57%, from ₹ 1272.15 crore in BE 2014-15 to ₹ 
4408.93 crore in BE 2026-27, reflecting the Government’s robust commitment towards the 
promotion, development and mainstreaming of traditional systems of medicine within the 
national healthcare framework. The Committee further notes that the BE allocation for 2026-
27 of ₹4408.93 crore represents an increase of nearly 20% over the Revised Estimates of 
2025-26 and about 10.4% over the Budget Estimates of 2025-26 (₹3992.90 crore), indicating 

continued prioritisation of the Ayush sector in the Union Budget. The Ministry has further informed that it aims to increase the Ayush sector’s share in India’s GDP from the present 1.1% to about 5% by the year 2047.
