The Committee observes that these initiatives represent an important step towards strengthening evidence generation and promoting scientific research in the Ayush sector. 5.6.2 The Committee takes note of emerging initiatives such as Biopharma SHAKTI 
provide an opportunity to integrate traditional knowledge systems with modern 
biotechnology and pharmaceutical innovation. The Committee observes that Ayush 
systems have significant potential in the development of plant-based therapeutic 
products, particularly phyto-pharmaceuticals, which can serve as a bridge between 
traditional herbal formulations and modern biopharmaceutical products. The 
Committee, however, is of the view that the absence of harmonised quality standards 
and robust clinical validation may limit the global competitiveness of Ayush products. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayushmust promote the 
development of phyto-pharmaceuticals and establish a quality certification framework 
aligned with internationally accepted Good Manufacturing Practices (GMP). The 
Committee further recommends leveraging institutions such as the WHO Global 
Traditional Medicine Centre for multi-centric clinical research so as to strengthen 
scientific validation and enhance India’s position in the global market for nature-based 
therapeutics. 
5.7 STANDARDIZATION OF ASU&H  vis-a-vis PHARMACOVIGILANCE 
5.7.1 The Secretary, Ministry of Ayush informed that the Ministry has taken several 
measures are being taken to strengthen the quality assurance and safety monitoring of Ayush 
drugs through regulatory safeguards, pharmacovigilance mechanisms and scientific research 
initiatives through provisions such as cautionary labelling for medicines containing 
ingredients listed under Schedule E(I) of the Drugs Rules, 1945, regulation of online sales 
through advisories issued by the Central Consumer Protection Authority (CCPA), and 
directions issued by the National Commission for Indian System of Medicine (NCISM) 
regarding clear prescription of dosage and duration represent important steps towards 
ensuring the safe use of Ayush medicines. The Ministry further informed that the 
implementation of the Pharmacovigilance Programme for ASU&H drugs through a 
nationwide three-tier network (NPvCC/IPvCs/PPvCs), along with the launch of the Ayush 
Suraksha Portal for reporting adverse reactions and misleading advertisements, has 
strengthened the institutional framework for safety monitoring and consumer protection in the 
Ayush sector. 
5.7.2 The Committee appreciates the progress made by the Ministry in strengthening 
the quality assurance framework for Ayush drugs and in expanding pharmacovigilance 
mechanisms within the sector. The Committee observes that these initiatives are 
important for ensuring the safety, credibility and wider acceptance of Ayush medicines. 
The Committee, however, is of the view that the scientific validation of Ayurvedic and 
other Ayush formulations needs to be further strengthened through systematic pre-
clinical, clinical and observational studies, including toxicity assessments, herb-drug 
interaction studies, pharmacological evaluations and well-designed clinical trials across 
a range of disease conditions. The Committee further observes that there persists a 

perception among sections of practitioners and the public that natural medicines are inherently harmless, which may discourage the identification and reporting of potential adverse effects. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush strengthen scientific research and pharmacovigilance systems to promote evidencebased validation and safety profiling of Ayush medicines. The Committee further recommends that robust mechanisms for quality control, traceability of raw materials and systematic documentation of adverse drug reactions be strengthened so as to enhance public confidence& acceptabilitymedicines from being primarily based healthcare interventions.5.8 EDUCATION AND HUMAN RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT5.8.1 The Ministry has informed that there has been a significant expansion in educational infrastructure with UG colleges inccolleges rising from 138 to 266. Correspondingly, UG seats have increased from 31,514 to 66,413, while PG seats have grown from 3,167 to 8,106, reflecting enhanced capacity for training Ayush professionals. The key reforms under education sector include the implementation of the NCISM and NCH Act, 2020, introduction of an eManagement System, and promotion of interdisciplinary and creditpostgraduate and Ph.D. programmes inbeen integrated into the MBBS curriculum through a mandatory oneyoga training. 89 
 
evidence-based practice within the sector. The Committee, however, is of the view that 
Ayush education and training require deeper alignment with the objectives of the 
National Education Policy (NEP), 2020, particularly in promoting interdisciplinary, 
research-oriented and skill-based learning. The Committee further observes that with 
increasing emphasis on scientific research, evidence-based practice and digital 
integration, there is a need to strengthen capacity building of faculty and students and 
upgrade existing educational infrastructure with better integration of modern 
diagnostic tools and technologies. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry initiate the establishment of dedicated training institutes for teachers and 
develop skill laboratories in Ayush colleges to improve the quality of teaching and 
practical training. The Committee further recommends that the undergraduate and 
postgraduate curricula be revised to include the management of diseases of national 
importance and other public health challenges prevalent in society. The Committee also 
recommends that emerging research findings and scientific evidence be systematically 
incorporated into the Ayush curriculum so as to make Ayush scholars more aware of 
contemporary scientific developments and strengthen the overall quality of Ayush 
education. 
5.8.3 The Committee observes that the Indian healthcare framework is increasingly 
moving towards a pluralistic model with growing co-location and integration of 
traditional and modern systems of medicine. The Committee further observes that such 
integration requires practitioners across different systems to possess a basic 
understanding of each other’s principles and approaches in order to ensure effective 
and coordinated healthcare delivery. The Committee, however, is of the view that the 
present healthcare education framework provides limited interdisciplinary exposure 
and does not adequately incorporate the Indian Knowledge System (IKS). The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush take appropriate steps to 
strengthen the integration of the Indian Knowledge System within the evolving 
integrative healthcare education framework so as to promote interdisciplinary 
understanding and support a more holistic approach to healthcare delivery in the 
country. 
5.8.4 The Committee observes that the effective delivery of healthcare services 
increasingly requires integration of traditional systems of medicine with modern 
diagnostic tools and technologies. The Committee is of the strong opinion that improved 
familiarity of Ayush practitioners with modern diagnostic techniques would enhance 
clinical decision-making, strengthen patient confidence and facilitate better integration 
of Ayush services within the broader healthcare system. The Committee, however, 
highlights that the existing training framework for Ayush practitioners provides limited 
structured exposure to modern diagnostic tools and technologies. The Committee, 
therefore, desires that the Ministry of Ayush must take steps to promote targeted 
training and capacity-building programmes to familiarise Ayush practitioners with 
modern diagnostic tools and techniques, while preserving the core principles and 
philosophy of traditional systems of medicine. The Committee believes that such 
initiatives would contribute to improving the quality of healthcare delivery and 
strengthening the credibility of Ayush systems in the evolving healthcare landscape. 

90 
 
5.9 AYUSH DIGITAL ECOSYSTEM 
5.9.1 The Ministry has informed that the Ayush Grid Project has been envisaged as a 
comprehensive IT backbone for the Ayush sector with the objective of digitizing service 
delivery across key domains such as healthcare, research and development, education and 
awareness, drug administration, capacity building and medicinal plants. Under this initiative, 
several digital platforms have been developed and rolled out across the country. These 
include the MAISP Portal, which provides a centralized platform for Ayush services and 
supports data-driven decision-making; the Ayush Hospital Management Information System 
(AHMIS) for streamlining hospital management, diagnostics and patient services; the Ayush 
Research Portal for strengthening evidence-based research and knowledge sharing; the Ayush 
Nivesh Saarthi Portal to facilitate investments in the Ayush sector; and the Yoga Portal aimed 
at promoting awareness and participation in yoga practices. The Ministry has further stated 
that these platforms are accessible online nationwide and are being progressively 
implemented to digitally integrate various Ayush services. 
 
5.9.2 The Committee takes into account that the Ministry has initiated several digital 
initiatives under the Ayush Grid Project with the objective of creating a robust digital 
integration framework for the Ayush sector. The Committee observes that such 
initiatives, including emerging areas like Ayurgenomics, have received international 
recognition, including acknowledgement by the World Health Organization (WHO), 
and hold significant potential for strengthening digital health integration, research 
dissemination and evidence generation in the Ayush sector. However, the Committee 


91 
 
finds that the extent of actual adoption of these digital platforms across institutions, 
their interoperability with national digital health systems and their measurable impact 
on service delivery and research outcomes still contain scope for further expansion in 
terms of Artificial Intelligence integration. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry should take time-bound steps to ensure wider adoption and effective 
operationalisation of the digital platforms developed under the Ayush Grid Project 
across Ayush hospitals, research institutions, educational bodies and regulatory 
authorities. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry establish clear 
implementation milestones, monitoring mechanisms and periodic reporting systems to 
track the usage and outcomes of these platforms. The Committee also recommends that 
necessary measures be taken to ensure interoperability of Ayush Grid platforms with 
national digital health initiatives, including the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission and 
that the data generated through these platforms be effectively utilised for policy 
planning, research advancement and evidence generation with incorporation of 
artificial intelligence. The Committee believes that strengthening digital integration 
along with capacity building& training of Ayush practitioners and institutional staff 
would facilitate the effective integration of Ayush services within the broader digital 
health ecosystem. 
5.10 GLOBALISATION OF AYUSH 
5.10.1 The Committee notes that the Government has articulated a long-term vision for 
the globalisation of Ayush as an important component of the broader Viksit Bharat @ 
2047 roadmap. The Committee observes that the increasing global interest in holistic, 
preventive and traditional systems of medicine presents a significant opportunity for 
India to position Ayush systems as an important pillar of global healthcare. The 
Committee further observes that initiatives such as the strengthening of the WHO 
Global Traditional Medicine Centre (GTMC) at Jamnagar, expansion of international 
collaborations, promotion of Ayush-based medical value travel and the global adoption 
of quality certification mechanisms such as the Ayush Mark have the immense potential 
to significantly enhance the international visibility and acceptance of Ayush systems. 
The Committee agrees that initiatives like the International Day of Yoga have played an 
important role in strengthening global awareness and acceptance of traditional Indian 
wellness practices and have emerged as a strong instrument of India’s soft power and 
health diplomacy. However, the Committee is of the firm view that achieving the vision 
of positioning India as a global leader in traditional and holistic healthcare would 
require a more coordinated and structured approach towards international 
engagement, research collaboration, quality assurance and global market development. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must develop a 
comprehensive strategy for the globalisation of Ayush with visionary policy priorities 
relating to establishing Centre of Excellence abroad, international research 
collaborations, global standardisation of Ayush products and therapies, promotion of 
Ayush-based medical value travel and strengthening of export promotion mechanisms. 
The Committee further recommends that initiatives such as the International Day of 
various systems of Ayush especially Yoga be strategically leveraged to enhance global 
awareness and institutional partnerships so as to position Ayush systems more 

92 
 
prominently within the global healthcare ecosystem and contribute to India’s leadership 
in traditional medicine. 
5.10.2 The Committee notes that India has emerged as an important destination for 
medical value travel owing to its skilled healthcare workforce, cost-effective treatments 
and well-established traditional wellness systems. The Committee observes that Ayush 
therapies, particularly Yoga, Panchakarma and other wellness treatments, possess 
considerable potential to attract international patients seeking holistic healthcare 
solutions. The Committee, however, is of the view that since the integration of Ayush 
therapies within the broader medical value travel ecosystem remains limited and 
requires greater policy focus for international promotion. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush develop dedicated strategies to integrate Ayush 
therapies within the broader medical value travel ecosystem in coordination with other 
relevant Ministries and stakeholders. The Committee considers that focused efforts be 
made to promote affordable Ayush-based wellness packages and therapies at the 
international level so as to position India as a global hub for holistic wellness and 
traditional medicine. 
5.11 AYUSH MARKET SIZE VIS-À-VIS EXPORTS 
5.11.1 The Secretary, Ministry of Ayush has informed that the Ayush sector currently 
contributes around 1.06% to India’s GDP and aims to increase its share to 5% by 2047. The 
Ministry further informed that India’s Ayush market size is presently estimated at US$ 43.4 
billion (₹3,599 billion), comprising ₹1,931 billion from manufacturing and ₹1,667 billion 
from services. Further, the Ayush manufacturing sector has witnessed significant growth, 
registering nearly a six-fold increase between 2014 and 2020 and about an eight-fold increase 
between 2014 and 2022 with the manufacturing segment growing at a compound annual 
growth rate (CAGR) of around 17% during 2014-2020. The Secretary has further projected 
that the Ayush market is expected to reach around US$ 200 billion by 2030 and that 
achieving this target would require the sector to grow at an estimated CAGR of about 21% 
over the coming years. 

5.11.2 The Committee envisageswellness products is steadily increasing and presents significant opportunities for the Ayush sector. The Committee observes that while the domestic manufacturingAyush products has expanded considerably in recent years, the sector’s export potential remains underutilised due to challenges relating to quality assurance, product standardisation, certification and international regulatory compliance. The Comvisualizes that achieving the projected Ayush market size of US$ 200 billion by 2030 would require substantial expansion of exports supported by robust quality assurance and international standardisation mechanisms. However, the Committee is of the that the existing ecosystem for promotion of Ayush exports requires further strengthening in terms of product authentication, regulatory alignment and global market access. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush, in coordination with the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, develop a comprehensive export promotion strategy for Ayush products with a focus on strengthening quality assurance, standardisation, certification and international regulatory compliance. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry explore policy measures such as the introduction of export incentives, creation of separate Harmonised System of Nomenclature (HSN) codes for Ayush products and strengthening of authentication and certification mechanisms including Ayush Mark and adherence to WHOguidelines. The Committee believes that such measures would significantly enhance the export competitiveness of Ayush products, improve global acceptance of Ayush systems and contribute towards achieving the proj5.11.3 The Committee points outinternational markets is an important objective of the International Cooperation Scheme and appreciates the efforts made by the Ministry and strengthening global engagement. The Committee establishment of the Ayush Export Promotion Council (Ayush-related provisions in Free Trade Agreements and thecertification and laboratory standards global market access for Ayush

envisages that the global demand for traditional medicine and wellness products is steadily increasing and presents significant opportunities for the Ayush sector. However, the Committee is of the view that 


94 
 
the sustained expansion of Ayush exports would require more focused export promotion 
strategies, greater international visibility and stronger institutional support for 
manufacturers, particularly MSMEs, in accessing regulated global markets. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Ministry must intensify targeted 
export promotion initiatives including the organisation of dedicated International Ayush 
Expos, trade fairs and global business conferences in strategic markets so as to facilitate 
direct engagement between Indian manufacturers and foreign regulators, distributors, 
healthcare providers and investors. The Committee understands that structured global 
awareness campaigns highlighting quality standards, safety certifications and scientific 
validation of Ayush products must be undertaken to enhance consumer confidence and 
brand recognition. The Committee wishes that measurable indicators such as export 
growth, market diversification, certification uptake and regulatory approvals secured 
under Free Trade Agreement frameworks be periodically monitored and reported so as 
to assess the tangible economic impact of these initiatives and strengthen India’s global 
position in the Ayush sector. 
5.12 Way Forward for National Medicinal Plants Board (NMPB) 
5.12.1 The Committee notes that the National Medicinal Plants Board (NMPB) has 
taken initiatives to support farmers through the establishment of nurseries for raising 
planting material of medicinal plants under the Forward and Backward Linkages in the 
Supply Chain of Medicinal Plants. The Committee observes that the medicinal plants 
sector holds considerable potential for strengthening the Ayush industry, supporting 
rural livelihoods and enhancing India’s bio-economy through the development of 
pharmaceuticals, nutraceuticals, wellness products and medicinal plant-based 
industries. The Committee, however wishes to point out that the absence of updated 
demand supply assessment data of medicinal plants and a comprehensive State-wise 
inventory may hinder effective planning for cultivation, value chain development and 
realisation of India’s export potential in medicinal plants and herbal products. The 
Committee further observes that the sector presently needs adequate industrial 
ecosystems to support large-scale product innovation, startup incubation, technology 
transfer and value addition, which in turn will promote the commercialisation and 
global competitiveness of Ayush products. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry must undertake a comprehensive national assessment study in 
coordination with State Medicinal Plants Boards to determine State-wise availability of 
medicinal plants and updated demand-supply projections, including global market 
demand. The Committee is of the strong view that the Ministry must strengthen the 
medicinal plants value chain through the establishment of Medicinal Plants Business 
Processing Centres (MBPCs) along with quality certification and traceability 
mechanisms to enhance value addition and export competitiveness. The Committee 
further recommends that the Ministry should develop supportive industrial ecosystems, 
including dedicated Ayush innovation and industry clusters, to promote product 
development, startup incubation, technology transfer and export promotion so as to 
fully realise the economic potential of the Ayush sector. 
 

95 
 
5.13 AYUSH POTENTIAL IN NORTH EASTERN REGION 
5.13.1 The Committee observes that the North Eastern Region of India possesses rich 
biodiversity and holds immense potential for the cultivation and sustainable utilisation 
of medicinal plants used in Ayush systems of medicine. The Committee further observes 
that the NER can play a pivotal role in strengthening the supply chain of medicinal 
plants and supporting the growth of the Ayush sector through cultivation, processing 
and value addition. However, the Committee is of the view that the full potential of the 
region remains underutilised due to limited infrastructure for cultivation, processing 
and market linkages. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of 
Ayush accord special emphasis to the development of medicinal plant cultivation, 
processing and value addition in the North Eastern States under relevant schemes. The 
Committee further recommends that appropriate measures be taken to strengthen 
market linkages, promote sustainable harvesting practices and support livelihood 
opportunities for local communities so as to harness the region’s biodiversity potential 
while contributing to the growth of the Ayush sector. 
5.14 CROP INSURANCE FOR MEDICINAL PLANTS 
5.14.1  The Committee notes that there has been a gradual shift in approach in 
the medicinal plants sector from forest-based collection to cultivation-based production. 
The Committee further notes that the National Medicinal Plants Board under the 
Ministry of Ayush has been assisting farmers in the cultivation of quality planting 
material and promoting the organised cultivation of medicinal plants. The Committee 
observes that such efforts are important for ensuring a sustainable supply of raw 
materials for Ayush systems while also creating additional livelihood opportunities for 
farmers. The Committee wills to point out that medicinal plant cultivation is often 
subject to price fluctuations and crop damage, which may expose farmers to financial 
risks and discourage wider adoption of such cultivation. The Committee is of the view 
that adequate risk mitigation mechanisms are essential to promote the long-term 
sustainability of medicinal plant cultivation. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry of Ayush, in coordination with the concerned Ministries and 
stakeholders, explore the feasibility of introducing crop insurance framework for 
medicinal plants so as to provide financial protection to farmers and encourage greater 
participation in the cultivation of medicinal plant for export promotion and increasing 
farmer’s income to desirable level and enhancing contribution of Ayush in GDP. 
5.15 PROPAGATION AND PROMOTION OF AYUSH 
5.15.1 The Committee takes note that despite the widespread presence of Ayush systems 
of medicine in the country, public awareness regarding their preventive and promotive 
healthcare benefits remains limited. The Committee observes that Ayush systems offer 
huge potential for promoting preventive healthcare and healthy lifestyles through 
practices such as Yoga, appropriate dietary habits and traditional wellness approaches. 
However, the Committee is of the view that existing awareness initiatives largely focus 
on information dissemination and require greater emphasis on encouraging behavioural 
change towards the adoption of Ayush-based lifestyle practices. The Committee, 

96 
 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush strengthen its public outreach 
programmes with a greater focus on behaviour change communication strategies aimed 
at promoting the adoption of Ayush-based preventive healthcare practices such as Yoga, 
dietary regulation through incorporating Millet diets and traditional wellness practices. 
The Committee believes that such initiatives would contribute to improving public 
health outcomes while strengthening the role of Ayush systems in preventive and 
promotive healthcare. 
 
CONCLUSION 
 
5.16.1 The Committee believes that the vision of Ayush @ 2047 offers a historic 
opportunity to transform India’s traditional systems of medicine into a scientifically 
robust, globally respected and economically vibrant component of the national 
healthcare ecosystem, while reinforcing Bharat’s leadership in promoting traditional 
medicine at the global level. The vision is guided by the civilisational ethos of 
VasudhaivaKutumbakam - the world as one family, the Committee emphasises that 
achieving the vision will require sustained and coordinated efforts towards 
strengthening scientific research and evidence generation, expanding institutional and 
educational infrastructure, ensuring standardisation and quality assurance of Ayush 
medicines and accelerating digital integration through initiatives such as the Ayush 
Grid. The Committee further underscores the importance of promoting global 
engagement, expanding medical value travel, strengthening export competitiveness of 
Ayush products and developing sustainable medicinal plant value chains to support the 
long-term growth of the sector, thereby positioning Bharat as a leading global hub for 
traditional and integrative medicine. The Committee is of the considered view that 
enhanced financial support, stronger inter-ministerial coordination, active participation 
of States and greater private sector involvement will be essential for unlocking the full 
potential of the Ayush ecosystem. The Committee hopes that the Ministry of Ayush will 
take appropriate and time-bound steps to address the issues highlighted in this Report 
and effectively implement the recommendations made therein so as to realise the vision 
of Ayush @ 2047 and further strengthen Bharat’s global leadership in holistic, 
preventive and integrative healthcare.

97 
 
 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE- 
AT A GLANCE 
 
 
Thematic Domains of the Ministry 
 
1. The Committee is of the view that the Ministry of Ayush continue to strive 
progressively system specific development to structured integration within public 
healthcare delivery mechanisms. Such integration should primarily aim at 
operationalising through centrally sponsored Schemes and evolve from standalone 
development to co-located facilities within primary, secondary and tertiary healthcare 
centres, thereby enhancing preventive care and accessibility. The Committee 
recommends for integration of Ayush facilities for boosting preventive care and local 
access to healthcare under Traditional Indian System of medicine. 
PARA 1.5.2 
  
Functional Areas Of Ministry Of Ayush & Its Institutional Framework 
 
2. The Committee strongly believes that integration model under Ministry of Ayush 
must aim to improve accessibility, strengthen preventive healthcare and reduce pressure 
on conventional health infrastructure. The Committee is aware of the fact that since 
effective implementation of policy/programme/scheme depends upon adequate human 
resources, infrastructure utilisation, drug supply mechanisms and coordination with 
State Governments. The Ministry, therefore chalk out specific course of action to 
achieve the set objectives. 
PARA 1.6.4 
 
Financial Overview and Budgetary Trends 
 
3. The Committee understands that the Demand for Grants 2026-27 seeks to 
strengthen institutional capacity, expand equitable access to Ayush services, promote 
scientific validation and digital governance and enhance regulatory oversight in the 
Ayush sector. The Committee observes that such initiatives are pivotal for consolidating 
the progress achieved in recent years and for strengthening the role of traditional 
systems of medicine in the national healthcare framework. The Committee however, is 
of the view that the proposed financial provisions must also be directed towards 
addressing emerging public health challenges while ensuring effective implementation 
and optimal utilisation of resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry ensure that the budget allocations under the Demand for Grants 2026-27 are 
strategically utilised to consolidate past achievements, expand access to quality Ayush 
services and support innovation and research, in alignment with the national vision of 
Viksit Bharat @ 2047 and India’s aspiration to emerge as a global leader in traditional 
medicine systems. 
PARA 1.7.4 
 
Research, Standardisation and Regulatory Strengthening 
 
4. The Committee emphasises that sustained focus is required on outcome-based 
research, multi-centric clinical trials, publication in peer-reviewed journals and 
transparent data dissemination. The Committee therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry further strengthen collaborative research with reputed scientific institutions, 

98 
 
enhance standardisation & quality control mechanism accompanied by systematic 
documentation and global dissemination of validated research outcomes so as to 
reinforce the safety, efficacy and credibility of Ayush systems of medicine. 
PARA 1.8.2 
 
Digital Governance and Technological Integration 
 
5. The Committee has taken note of the Ministry’s multi-dimensional digital 
ongoing initiatives, including the use of AI-based Yoga recommendations and the 
integration of Chatbots into the Ayush Grid. The Committee acknowledges the 
significant potential of these tools, the Committee emphasizes that their ultimate success 
depends on seamless interoperability vis-a-vis national digital health systems. 
Furthermore, the Committee strongly recommends implementing capacity-building 
measures to accelerate the adoption of these platforms and ensure effective 
implementation at the field level. 
PARA 1.9.2 
 
International Cooperation and Global Engagement 
 
6. The Committee observes that such collaborations offer opportunities for 
knowledge exchange, research cooperation and greater global acceptance of Ayush 
systems. The Committee however is of the view that while international engagement is 
important, strengthening domestic service delivery, regulatory mechanisms and 
institutional capacity must remain the primary focus. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must pursue international collaborations in a 
manner that complements and reinforces national priorities, ensuring that global 
outreach contributes to improving the quality, accessibility and credibility of Ayush 
systems. 
 
PARA 1.10.3  
 
MYTH Vs FACTS 
 
7. The Committee observes that myths & misconceptions clouding over the Ayush 
systems of medicine affect public perception and limit the wider acceptance and 
utilisation of Ayush services despite their long-standing traditional knowledge base and 
increasing scientific validation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry of Ayush undertake sustained and evidence-based public awareness initiatives 
to address prevailing misconceptions and disseminate accurate information regarding 
the safety, efficacy and regulatory oversight of Ayush medicines. The Committee further 
recommends that the Ministry undertake structured outreach campaigns, disseminate 
scientific research findings and promote greater engagement with healthcare 
professionals and academic institutions so as to strengthen public confidence in Ayush 
systems of medicine. 
PARA 1.11.2 
Future Priorities 
 
8. The Committee recognises the need for continued institutional strengthening of 
the Ministry of Ayush in view of the expanding role of Ayush systems within the 
national healthcare framework. While the ongoing transition from system-specific 
promotion towards structured integration of Ayush within public health delivery 
reflects a calibrated policy approach, the Committee is of the view that this transition 
requires sustained institutional capacity, effective implementation mechanisms and 

99 
 
strengthened governance structures to translate policy objectives into tangible 
outcomes. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry take appropriate 
steps to further strengthen institutional capacities and governance mechanisms to 
support the progressive integration of Ayush systems within the national healthcare 
framework. The Committee further emphasises that infrastructure expansion, 
regulatory reforms and digital governance initiatives should be implemented in a 
coordinated manner so as to facilitate long-term institutional consolidation and effective 
delivery of Ayush services across the country. 
PARA 1.12.2 
 
 
9. The Committee takes into account various initiatives undertaken by the Ministry 
for the promotion and development of Ayush systems across the country. The 
Committee observes that while these initiatives have contributed to strengthening 
institutional infrastructure and expanding outreach, there is a need to ensure that the 
outcomes of these interventions are systematically evaluated. However, the Committee is 
of the view that greater emphasis must now be placed on measurable health outcomes 
and impact assessment, effective capacity utilisation of newly established institutions, 
strengthening of quality assurance and pharmacovigilance mechanisms and improved 
coordination with State Governments in programme implementation. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry adopt a more outcome-oriented approach by 
promoting evidence-based research validation and ensuring optimal utilisation of 
budgetary allocations so as to maximise the overall effectiveness of Ayush interventions. 
 
PARA 1.12.2 
 
Budget Estimates (BE) - Year-wise Comparison 
10. The Committee observes that a closer examination of the Budget Estimates over 
the past five years reveals that the year-on-year growth has not followed a stable or 
predictable scaling pattern. The annual growth in BE has fluctuated considerably, 
ranging from 2.68 per cent in 2022-23 to 19.59 per cent in 2023-24, followed by a sharp 
moderation to 1.78 per cent in 2024-25 and thereafter moderate increases of 7.55 per 
cent in 2025-26 and 10.42 per cent in 2026-27. The Committee further observes that 
when these nominal increases are adjusted against average inflation levels, certain years 
reflected negative real growth, implying erosion in the effective purchasing power of 
allocations. The Committee is of the view that such volatility suggests episodic 
enhancement rather than a calibrated and programmatic scaling of budgetary support 
for the sector. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush 
formulate its Budget Estimate projections after duly factoring in realistic assessment of 
each scheme/project and keeping in account prevailing and anticipated inflation as well 
so that real growth in allocations is maintained at a meaningful level to support 
sustained expansion of Ayush services. The Committee further recommends that the 
Ministry endeavour to adopt a steadier and more incremental pattern of budgetary 
growth to ensure predictability and continuity in financial planning. A calibrated and 
consistent approach would facilitate stable scaling of programmes, safeguard the 
purchasing power of allocations and enable systematic strengthening of the Ayush 
sector over the medium to long term. 
PARA 2.2.2 
 
DEMAND VS ALLOCATION CREDIBILITY 
 
11. The Committee appreciates the Ministry’s efforts in presenting comprehensive 
projected demands aimed at expanding the scope, reach and institutional capacity of the 

100 
 
sector; however, sustained substantial deviations between projected demand and 
approved allocation at BE and RE stage may adversely impact planning stability and 
execution strategy.  The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry to 
advance plea for the outcome-linked and evidence-based justification of its projected 
demands by clearly demonstrating measurable impact, implementation readiness and 
expected sectoral returns. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry adopt 
a phased and prioritised approach in structuring its demands by distinguishing core 
commitments from expansionary and long-term aspirational components so as to 
facilitate more realistic projection at the stage of budget estimate keeping in view 
feasibility of project implementation and absorption capacity of implementing agencies. 
The Committee also recommends that the Ministry develop a multi-year financial 
projection framework to enable greater predictability in resource planning and 
smoother alignment with the budgetary processes of the Ministry of Finance. 
 
PARA 2.3.2 
 
Major Components Contributing Reduction (Projected Demand Vs BE 2026-27) 
12. The Committee observes with serious concern that against the total projected 
demand of ₹ 5,679.48 crore for the year 2026-27, an allocation of only ₹ 4,408.93 crore 
has been made at the Budget Estimates stage, resulting in a substantial shortfall of ₹ 
1,270.55 crore (22.37 %)of  the projected requirement.The Committee further observes 
that this fiscal compression is not uniformly distributed across components but is 
disproportionately concentrated in certain critical and expansion-oriented segments 
which includes flagship implementation schemes like NAM & CSSCMP; Research 
bodies like CCRYN &CCRH; premier institutes NIH, AIIA and quality strengthening 
initiatives like the Ayush Oushadhi Gunvatta Evam Utpadan Samvardhan Yojana 
(AOUGSY) which may have a cascading impact on capacity augmentation, 
infrastructure expansion, research continuity and the overall momentum of institutional 
growth in strategically important and emerging domains of the Ayush systems.The 
Committee also recalls that in 165th Report DFG 2025-26, it had recommended seeking 
supplementary grants at the Revised Estimates stage; however, the information 
furnished instead reflects a reduction of ₹ 321 crore at the RE stage.  
 
PARA 2.4.1 
 
13. The Committee observes that the reduction in allocations at the Revised 
Estimates stage across several components raises serious concerns regarding the 
adequacy of fiscal support extended to the sector despite acknowledged requirements. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush, in consultation with 
the Ministry of Finance, undertake a mid-year review of allocations with a view to 
providing adequate supplementary support to bridge the critical funding gap. The 
Committee further desires that priority be accorded to key components, particularly 
flagship implementation schemes such as the National Ayush Mission (NAM), research 
councils, premier national institutes and quality-strengthening initiatives, so as to 
prevent disruption of ongoing projects, dilution of research momentum or deferment of 
infrastructure expansion. The Committee emphasizes that these strategic segments 
represent long-term capacity-building investments and should not be subjected to 
disproportionate fiscal contraction. The Committee also urges the Ministry to adopt a 
more realistic and consultative budgeting approach in future cycles to ensure adequate 
BE allocations so that no project/scheme should suffer for want of funds and thereby 
doubly safeguarding programmatic continuity and sustaining the growth trajectory of 
the sector. 
PARA 2.4.2 

101 
 
 
BE - RE  Discipline with Projected Demand  
 
14. The Committee is of the view that the Ministry needs to revisit and refine its 
approach to budget formulation and expenditure planning process so as to minimise 
recurrent dual-stage compression by more realistic estimation, stronger implementation 
preparedness and closer alignment between projection and absorptive capacity. The 
Committee further emphasises the importance of more effective pre-budget engagement 
with the Ministry of Finance by placing proposals based on outcome orientation and 
implementation ready, supported by defined timelines, clarity regarding approval status 
of projects, staffing position, and procurement readiness. The Committee emphasis that 
construction projects should be included in projections only after obtaining the requisite 
administrative and technical approvals. Similarly, existing vacancies, unspent balances 
with CPWD, and the status of proposals pending before PSC/PAC should be 
realistically assessed at the stage of demand formulation. The Committee considers it a 
budgeting prudence for the Ministry to institute regular quarterly reviews of 
expenditure, closely monitor the rollout of the SNA SPAR system at the State level and 
take timely corrective steps to avoid substantial surrenders or downward revisions at 
the RE stage. These measures so undertaken would contribute to greater credibility in 
budget projections, improve stability in resource planning, and ensure that allocations 
made at the BE stage are effectively and fully utilized while potentially implementing 
the scheme to optimum level thereby, setting bulwark against reduction of budget at 
stage. 
PARA 2.5.3 
 
Utilisation& Absorptive Capacity Analysis  
 
15. The Committee highlights that the Ministry has consistently maintained high 
levels of utilisation and administrative discipline in deploying funds allocated at the 
Revised Estimate stage. The Committee notes the Ministry’s sustained utilisation 
performance of allocated funds at RE stage and the same demonstrates 
capacity/potential to effectively deploy resources if budgetary allocation is earmarked 
for utilization as per projected demand. In view of this consistent absorptive strength, 
the Committee is of the considered opinion that higher budgetary allocation levels are 
justified therefore, the Committee recommends that the Ministry should continue to 
strengthen quarterly expenditure planning and milestone-based fund release 
mechanisms to ensure balanced spending patterns and avoid excessive concentration of 
expenditure in the closing months of the financial year. Further, the Committee advises 
the Ministry to improve the Actual Expenditure of National Ayush Mission by 
addressing the transitional challenges being faced. 
PARA 2.6.1 
 
Capital Intensity Vs Growth Ambition 
 
16. The Committee appreciates the Ministry’s efforts to expand infrastructure in the 
Ayush sector through scheme-based interventions and collaborative federal 
mechanisms, particularly under the National Ayush Mission and the integrative 
healthcare framework. However, the Committee is of the view that sustained sectoral 
growth of Ayush services requires a more predictable and strategically designed 
separate capital investment framework at the Ministry level. The preponderance of 
aspiration to position Ayush as a global leader in traditional medicine would require a 
budgetary architecture that moves beyond a predominantly revenue-driven framework, 
as durable growth depends upon sustained asset creation in institutions, laboratories, 

102 
 
testing facilities and digital infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry progressively enhance identifiable capital expenditure so that it constitutes 
about 5-7 per cent of the total Budget Estimates over the next three financial years.  
 
PARA 2.7.2 
 
17. The Committee further recommends for creation of a dedicated Ayush 
Infrastructure Fund with multi years capital option for institutional upgradation, 
modern drug testing and quality control infrastructure, national research and clinical 
networks, and digital integration including Ayush Grid 2.0. Additionally, future 
Demands for Grants should consider annexing a consolidated statement of capital 
formation across direct budget heads, the National Ayush Mission, Research Councils, 
and Autonomous Bodies to reflect level of capital intensity and support structured long-
term investment planning. 
PARA 2.7.3 
 
Strategic Comparison of Minsitry of Ayush Vs Ministry of Health & Welfare Budget 
Estimates Allocation (2014-15 to 2026-27)  
 
18. The Committee appreciates the progressive strengthening of the Ministry’s 
proportional share within the overall health architecture over the past decade, however, 
considering the expanding mandate of the Ministry and its long-term aspirations for 
enhanced global positioning and global leadership, the Committee recommends that 
sustained expansion of Ayush services, research capacity and global engagement would 
require enhanced and consistent budgetary allocation in its share within the national 
health envelope. The Ministry therefore, continue to strengthen evidence-based 
justification of its programmes, demonstrate measurable public health outcomes and 
articulate a medium-term fiscal roadmap aligned with its long-term sectoral objectives. 
The Committee in this regard reiterates its earlier recommendation made in 128th 
Report (Demand for Grants 2025-26) that at least 10% of the total health sector budget 
be earmarked progressively for the Ministry of Ayush for effective pluralistic healthcare 
model. 
PARA 2.8.2 
 
Ayush Ministry’s BE as a share of Union (Total) BE 
 
19. The Committee notes that the Ministry has articulated an ambitious long-term 
vision for the Ayush sector under the Ayush @ 2047 framework, including the objective 
of expanding the Ayush market size to a $200 billion economy by 2030 and 5% of the 
country GDP. The Committee observes that achieving such transformative goals would 
require sustained expansion of institutional capacity, research, education and service 
delivery across the sector. However, the Committee is of the view that the present level 
of budgetary support may not be adequate to fully realise these long-term aspirations. 
Incremental increases from a relatively low fiscal base may limit the pace at which the 
sector can scale its programmes and infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Finance consider undertaking a phased and calibrated 
enhancement of the budgetary allocation for the Ministry of Ayush so as to 
progressively strengthen its fiscal position within the overall Union expenditure 
framework. The Committee is of the considered opinion that a structured medium-term 
fiscal roadmap, aligned with measurable expansion in institutional capacity, research, 
education and service delivery, would be essential to ensure that the Ministry’s 
budgetary positioning adequately reflects its evolving policy mandate and long-term 
developmental objectives. 

103 
 
PARA 2.9.2 
 
Fiscal Flow under the 15th Finance Commission Period (2021-22 to 2025-26) 
20. The Committee appreciates the Ministry’s consistent utilisation performance and 
its demonstrated capacity to effectively deploy funds retained at the Revised Estimate 
stage, however, the Committee observes that there have been recurring and substantial 
rationalisation of the Ministry’s projected demands at both the Budget Estimate and 
Revised Estimate stages. The Committee is of the view that such repeated adjustments 
may affect planning predictability and thereby skewed implementation of the projects. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen the evidence-based 
justification of its projected financial requirements and align its demand projections 
more closely with realistic absorption capacity and phased implementation plans. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry undertake enhanced pre-budget 
consultations and adopt strategic financial planning approach to improve predictability 
in allocation flows and minimise the extent of mid-year rationalisation so that projects 
are implemented as per originally conceived vision. Such measures would help improve 
fiscal credibility, planning stability and better alignment between projected demands 
and actual expenditure outcomes. 
PARA 2.10.2 
 
NORTH EAST BUDGETARY ALLOCATION TRENDS 
 
21. The Committee takes into account the increasing trend of budgetary allocation 
for the North Eastern Region and recognises the significant potential of the region in 
promoting Ayush systems of medicine owing to its rich biodiversity and traditional 
knowledge of medicinal plants. The Committee observes that strengthening Ayush 
infrastructure and services in the North Eastern States can play an important role in 
enhancing the wider acceptability and availability of Ayush systems while also 
supporting the sustainable utilisation of the region’s biodiversity resources by 
harnessing herbs for Ayush drugs. However, the Committee is of the view that 
variations in utilisation and implementation across the region indicate the need for 
better estimation of requirements, improved financial planning and stronger 
implementation mechanisms. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
of Ayush strengthen its planning, monitoring and implementation mechanisms for 
schemes in the North Eastern Region. The Committee is of the view that the Ministry 
must focus on timely implementation of projects and improve coordination with 
implementing agencies and State Governments so that the allocated resources translate 
into tangible outcomes in terms of strengthened Ayush infrastructure, services and 
outreach in the region. The Committee emphasize that attention must be given to 
addressing implementation bottlenecks by providing technical guidance and 
handholding support to States, including assistance in the establishment and 
strengthening of Ayush Directorates for effective implementation of the National Ayush 
Mission, so as to ensure that the funds earmarked for the North Eastern Region are 
fully and effectively utilised. 
PARA 2.11.2 
 
CONTINUATION OF SCHEMES OF MINISTRY OF AYUSH 
 
22. The Committee notes that there exists a significant gap between the financial 
outlays proposed by the Ministry of Ayush and the allocation made by the Ministry of 
Finance in certain schemes, particularly Ayurgyan, Ayurswasthya Yojana and 
AOGUSY. The Committee observes that these schemes support critical areas such as 
research, education and strengthening of drug quality infrastructure in the Ayush 

104 
 
sector and therefore play an important role in the overall development of the sector. The 
Committee, however is of the view that if the reduced ceilings are maintained, several 
schemes may receive allocations lower than those approved during the previous scheme 
cycle, which may adversely affect the expansion and strengthening of these priority 
areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush actively 
pursue the matter with the Ministry of Finance during forthcoming deliberations and 
appraisal meetings and seek enhanced financial outlays so as to ensure that the 
objectives of research promotion, capacity building and quality assurance in the Ayush 
sector are adequately supported during the current scheme cycle. 
PARA 2.12.3 
 
Scheme-wise Allocation F.Y 2026-27 under Central Sector Schemes of Ayush 
 
23. The Committee expresses serious concern over the substantial downward 
revision of Budget Estimates (BE) at the Revised Estimates (RE) stage for 2025-26 
across several schemes, including the National Ayush Mission which indicates persistent 
underutilization of allocated funds. The Committee emphasizes that recurring BE-to-
RE compression reflects a disconnect between budget formulation and ground-level 
preparedness. The Ministry must ensure that Budget Estimates are aligned with 
implementation readiness, supported by approved action plans and confirmed project 
pipelines. The Committee further recommends overhauling pre-budget appraisal 
mechanisms, instituting strict quarterly monitoring of physical and financial progress 
and enhancing coordination with States and implementing bodies to remove procedural 
delays. A structured capacity-building and execution framework should also be put in 
place to prevent future underutilization and ensure timely and effective deployment of 
allocated funds. 
PARA 3.3.2 
 
 
24. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry undertake a 
comprehensive review of the implementation architecture under each scheme with a 
view to identifying structural and procedural bottlenecks affecting timely utilisation of 
funds. The Committee is of the view that delays in approvals, procedural complexities 
and implementation constraints may adversely affect the effective translation of 
budgetary allocations into tangible programme outcomes.The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry establish a time-bound corrective action framework 
aimed at improving implementation efficiency. In this regard, greater decentralisation 
of approvals, streamlining of procedural requirements, advance preparation of Annual 
Action Plans and strengthened monitoring of implementing agencies should be 
institutionalised. The Committee also expects the Ministry to present scheme-wise 
corrective measures along with clearly defined timelines in its Action Taken Notes on 
recommendations contained in this report so as to ensure that future budgetary 
allocations at BE stage is not slashed at RE in ensuing financial year so that originally 
conceived goals and objectives are achieved within stipulated timeframe with 
measurable outcomes.  
PARA 3.3.3 
 
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION OF NATIONAL AYUSH MISSION 
 
25. The Committee observes with serious concern that the allocation for the National 
Ayush Mission (NAM) was reduced by 38.75% at the Revised Estimates (RE) stage in 
2025-26. Such substantial reduction is accompanied by poor utilisation of only about 
34% against the RE allocation of ₹ 780.96 crore, leaving an unspent balance of ₹ 513.54 

105 
 
crore as on 03.02.2026. Such a combination of steep RE reduction and low expenditure 
reflects weaknesses in expenditure planning and evenly distributed expenditure in each 
quarter of financial year under the scheme. The Committee further finds that similar 
BE-to-RE reductions have occurred in preceding years (-18.75% in 2022-23, -32.08% in 
2023-24, -12.83% in 2024-25, and -38.75% in 2025-26), indicating a recurring structural 
issue rather than an isolated instance.  
PARA 3.5.1 
 
26. The Committee notes that although there is increasing budgetary support 
provided to the Mission at the RE Stage. However, the Committee observes with 
concern that the trend of recurring pattern of reductions at the Revised Estimates stage 
and suboptimal fund utilisation during the past five years, which indicate persistent 
weaknesses in planning and absorptive capacity. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry undertake a comprehensive review of its annual 
budgeting practices under the Mission so as to align Budget Estimates more realistically 
formulated & allocated with preconceived implementation capacity, project execution 
readiness and bettering expenditure trends. The Committee further recommends for 
evenly distributed expenditure in each quarter of financial year. 
PARA 3.5.2 
 
27. The Committee finds that the slow pace of fund utilisation has been attributed to 
the transition to the Single Nodal Agency (SNA-SPARSH) fund flow mechanism. While 
acknowledging the objective of improving transparency and financial discipline, the 
Committee emphasises that procedural reforms must not impede programme 
implementation pace. The Committee therefore, recommends that the Ministry institute 
structured and time-bound capacity-building measures for States/UTs to ensure 
seamless operationalisation of SNA accounts towards intended purpose. The Central 
Programme Management Unit (CPMU) may play pro-active & facilitative role by 
providing technical guidance, resolving onboarding bottlenecks and monitoring state-
wise compliance so that the fund flow reform must strengthens funds utilization rather 
than catalyze procedural delays. 
PARA 3.5.3 
 
28. The Committee further observes that persistent delays in utilisation points out 
towards inadequacies in real-time financial and physical monitoring. The absence of 
periodic corrective review results in accumulation of unspent balances and subsequent 
RE-stage reductions. The Committee, therefore, recommends institutionalisation of a 
structured quarterly review mechanism at both Central and State/UT levels, integrating 
financial progress with measurable physical outputs. The framework should enable 
early detection of bottlenecks by Central Project Management Unit and timely 
reallocation of funds to better-performing components, ensuring optimal utilisation 
within the financial year and reducing reliance on post-facto budgetary compression. 
 
PARA 3.5.4 
 
 
29. The Committee observes that while policy emphasis has been placed on 
integration of Ayush services with mainstream healthcare, convergence with NHM 
remains uneven across States. Referral protocols, co-location models, and digital 
integration with national health platforms are not uniformly operationalised. Digital 
systems such as A-HMIS and real-time dashboards remain underutilised in several 
regions. The Committee recommends structured convergence of Ayush services with 
NHM through defined Standard Operating Procedures for co-location, promoting cross 

106 
 
referral pathways and integration into national public health programmes, particularly 
in Non Communicable Diseases, maternal, and geriatric care. Full operationalisation of 
digital platforms including A-HMIS integration, telemedicine services, and real-time 
project tracking systems, should be prioritised alongside capacity-building initiatives to 
ensure effective utilization of allocated funds. 
PARA 3.5.5 
 
 
30. The Committee has been given to understand regarding the detailed status 
statement of construction projects furnished by the Ministry that against a total 
sanctioned cost of ₹1232.21 crore, an expenditure of ₹ 746.55 crore has been incurred up 
to December 2025, reflecting an overall utilisation of approximately 60%. The project-
wise position reveals significant inter-State variation in physical progress, with only a 
limited number of projects completed and a substantial number remaining in Phase-I, 
Phase-II or preliminary stages. This uneven progress indicates differential 
implementation capacity and possible bottlenecks affecting timely completion. The 
Committee recommends that the Ministry adopt a structured, risk-based monitoring 
framework for infrastructure projects under the Mission. Projects progressing as per 
approved timelines may continue under routine review whereas projects that have 
exceeded stipulated completion schedules or exhibit disproportionately slow progress 
should be subjected to intensified supervision, including periodic senior-level review and 
mandatory reporting. The Committee further recommends that fund releases be 
progressively aligned with verified physical progress to prevent idle parking of funds. 
The Digital tracking mechanism of projects need to be strengthen Clear accountability 
mechanisms should be instituted in cases of avoidable delay attributable to 
administrative lapses at the State or implementing agency level. 
PARA 3.5.6 
 
 
31. The Committee has taken note of the third party evaluation findings fursnished 
by the Ministry and is of the view that strengthening of AAM (Ayush) must be 
supported by measurable accountability mechanisms. The Committee recommends 
development of a State/UT-wise performance benchmarking framework based on 
indicators such as human resource availability, patient footfall, NCD screening 
coverage, telemedicine usage, medicine availability, and convergence with national 
health programmes. A composite performance index may be published annually and 
underperforming States/UTs should submit time-bound corrective action plans. The 
Committee further recommends that a portion of central assistance under NAM/AAM 
(Ayush) be specified to capacity building measures and IEC component activities for 
effective implementation of scheme and generating public awareness of the scheme. 
 
PARA 3.5.7 
 
 
32. The Committee also recommends publication of an Annual AAM (Ayush) 
Performance Report in the public domain detailing infrastructure status, HR position, 
financial utilization, and service delivery indicators to enhance transparency and 
legislative oversight. Additionally, a structured social audit mechanism with beneficiary 
feedback, public disclosure of services and medicine availability and grievance redressal 
tracking mechanism should be institutionalized to strengthen community participation 
and accountability. 
PARA 3.5.8 
 

107 
 
PROMOTION OF INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION 
 
33. The Committee observes that there has been a consistent increase in allocation 
under the Scheme for Promotion of International Cooperation, reflecting the 
Government’s commitment to strengthening India’s global leadership in traditional 
medicine. The Committee appreciates the Ministry for its sustained efforts for scaling of 
activity with substantial increased allocation particularly the execution of the ICHI 
agreement, the constitution of the BIMSTEC Task Force on Traditional Medicine, 
active multilateral cooperation across various international forums and the organisation 
of global events that have enhanced India’s diplomatic and technical stature in this 
sector. The Committee further notes with satisfaction that India’s digital initiatives, 
including the Aysuh Grid and AI-based innovations have received recognition at the 
WHO level, thereby reinforcing India’s leadership in integrating traditional medicine 
with emerging technologies. 
PARA 3.6.4 
 
34. The Committee expresses pleasure over India’s growing global leadership in 
traditional medicine reflected through global engagement and outcomes generated in 
form of MoU, Academic Chair etc.  However, the Committee is of the view that these 
gains needs consolidation and sustained once further through recently launched Ayush 
Global Portal. The Committee has also reviewed the Ayush Global Portal and the 
information available for the users on real time basis finding that Ayush Global Portal 
has not been much encouraging as the country specific information is not updated 
including for India and lacks user friendly interface. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends for strengthening of Ayush Global Portal to enhance global visibility, 
interoperability and access to authentic services. The Committee also recommends that 
the Ministry leverage the proposed digital interface to facilitate international 
collaboration, knowledge exchange and global access to authentic Ayush services 
including tele-consultation, academic partnerships and dissemination of evidence-based 
resources. Such an integrated platform would enhance India’s soft power in traditional 
medicine while improving transparency, accessibility and global engagement with 
Ayush systems. 
PARA 3.6.5 
 
35. The Committee appreciates the extensive diplomatic outreach under the 
International Cooperation Scheme, including the signing of 27 bilateral MoUs, 
establishment of 15 Ayush Academic Chairs, and operationalisation of 43 Ayush 
Information Cells across 43 countries. However, the Committee is of the view that the 
momentum of global recognition should be sustained through structured and outcome-
oriented engagement. The Committee therefore recommends the Ministry to identify 
priority partner countries and develop targeted collaboration frameworks focusing on 
joint research projects, academic exchanges, clinical studies and capacity-building 
initiatives in traditional medicine so as to deepen scientific engagement and strengthen 
institutional linkages. The Committee further recommends instituting a structured 
performance assessment mechanism with periodic country-wise outcome reports to 
evaluate the operational status of MoUs, academic output of Chairs and the functional 
impact of Ayush Information Cells.  
PARA 3.6.6 
 
 
INFORMATION, EDUCATION & COMMUNICATION (IEC) 
 

108 
 
36. The Committee observes that the Budget Estimates under the IEC Scheme have 
largely remained at a stable level over successive years with minor modification 
primarily at the Revised Estimates stage to accommodate additional expenditure on 
International Day of Yoga (IDY) celebrations. The Committee recommends that 
adequate budgetary allocation at the Budget Estimate stage be ensured so that IEC 
activities aimed at disseminating Ayush outcomes and awareness at the grassroots level 
are not constrained or made contingent upon mid-year revisions. A more predictable 
and sufficiently provisioned budget would enable sustained outreach to the general 
public beyond event-specific expenditure.  
PARA 3.7.3 
 
 
37. The Committee recognizes that awareness of Ayush systems is praiseworthy but 
comparatively actual practice of Ayush remains largely confined to Ayurveda and 
predominantly for preventive and rejuvenative purposes rather than for therapeutic or 
curative treatment.  The Committee notes with concern that divergence between 
awareness and actual use/practice clearly reflects a structural gap in the translation of 
awareness into sustained and confidence-based healthcare adoption. The Committee is 
of the view that the reason for underuse is public perception of Ayush with limited 
curative efficacy, quick relief requirements and limited visibility of robust clinical 
evidence etc. The Committee recommends that the Ministry’s IEC strategy should shift 
from generic awareness campaigns to evidence-based therapeutic communication 
highlighting disease-specific success stories, clinical research outcomes and standard 
treatment protocols in a simplified and accessible manner. Public Campaigns and 
awareness in fair/seminars must focus on demonstrating the curative and management 
potential of Ayush in chronic and lifestyle disorders rather than limiting messaging to 
wellness and rejuvenation themes. Structured Behaviour Change Communication 
(BCC) modules may be developed to build sustained public confidence through 
testimonials, case documentation, and community-level engagement. Further, IEC 
interventions should intensively integrate digital tools for enhanced outreach, conduct 
district-level impact assessments and tailor region-specific messaging in local languages 
through social media including digital and audio video publicity. The Ministry should 
explore periodic thematic campaigns linked to specific diseases supported by validated 
data to reposition Ayush as a credible therapeutic option alongside preventive care. 
 
PARA 3.7.5 
 
38. The Committee has been given to understand that the average per capita 
expenditure on Ayush treatment remains relatively modest ₹ 472 in rural and ₹ 574 in 
urban areas which indicates its potential affordability as a preventive and supportive 
healthcare option. In this context, the Committee recommends that Yoga and wellness-
based services be systematically integrated and expanded under the National 
Programme for Prevention and Control of Non-Communicable Diseases (NP-NCD) 
particularly targeting rural and geriatric populations who bear a disproportionate 
burden of lifestyle-related disorders. Given the comparatively half out-of-pocket 
expenditure associated with Ayush interventions such integration may serve as a cost-
effective preventive model, reduce long-term NCD treatment costs and generate inter-
generational adoption patterns that gradually influence the younger population towards 
sustained wellness practices. 
PARA 3.7.6 
 
39. The Committee observes that IEC initiatives undertaken by the Ministry 
including fairs, seminars, conferences and promotional campaigns, are presently 

109 
 
evaluated largely on the basis of the number of activities conducted rather than their 
measurable impact on utilisation of Ayush services. The Committee, therefore, desires 
to be apprised of outcome-based indicators to assess the outreach of initiatives that are 
effectively translating public awareness into behavioural change. The Committee 
therefore recommends that the Ministry establish an outcome-oriented monitoring 
framework under the IEC Scheme incorporating measurable key indicators such as 
increase in utilisation of Ayush services, repeat usage patterns, district-level outreach 
penetration and public confidence indices. Periodic impact assessments should be 
conducted so that IEC strategies may be refined based on evidence of their effectiveness. 
 
PARA 3.7.7 
 
40. The Committee notes that a significant portion of IEC activities’s expenditure 
appears to be associated with large-scale events such as International Day of Yoga 
celebrations and national promotional programmes. While such events play an 
important role in enhancing visibility of Ayush systems, sustained behavioural adoption 
requires continuous and community-level engagement beyond episodic outreach 
activities. The Committee therefore recommends that the Ministry of Ayush may 
strengthen year-round community-based communication strategies, encouraging States 
and local implementing agencies to develop region-specific IEC content in local 
languages addressing area-specific health concerns and disease burdens. Greater 
decentralisation of communication efforts, supported by trained grassrootlevel 
functionaries and community institutions would help ensure deeper and more sustained 
outreach translating into actual utilization. 
PARA 3.7.8 
 
41. The Committee is given to understand that International day of Yoga 2025 
celebrated on a massive scale which has led to global recognition with two Guinness 
Book of Records including largest Yoga session (3.02 lakh participants) and mass Surya 
Namaskar (22,122 tribal students). Further, the Ministry plans to launch Ayush 
Samagra Swasthya Mission including “Har Ghar Ayur-Yog- self care model in 75% 
households by 2029” as part of the National Ayush Mission. The Committee appreciates 
the global recognition by IDY in previous years and given the substantial public 
expenditure involved to the tune of ₹ 56 crore in 2025 and ₹ 25 crore in 2024, the 
Ministry may consider setting a progressive roadmap towards achieving 100 % 
household coverage in place of 75% under the “Har Ghar Ayur-Yog” initiative by 2029 
as the name of the initiative itself spells out. The Committee further recommends that a 
structured, advance promotion and preparedness campaign and implementation 
milestones be launched especially in schools, colleges to ensure sustained behavioural 
adoption beyond event-based participation. The Committee further recommends that 
the active engagement of trained Yoga Instructors and certified community-level 
trainers be institutionalised to ensure last-mile outreach and sustained household 
adoption under the “Har Ghar Ayur-Yog” initiative. 
PARA 3.7.9 
 
 
42. The Committee has also been apprised by the Secretary, Ministry of Ayush about 
the current status of the nationwide campaign “Desh Ka Prakriti Parikshan”, under 
which more than 1.40 crore Indian citizens aged 18 years and above have undergone 
app-based Prakriti assessment. The Committee appreciates the initiative and its wide 
outreach and recommends that the Ministry of Ayush leverage the data generated 
through these Prakriti Parikshan assessments for developing evidence-based preventive 
health strategies and personalised lifestyle advisories rooted in Ayurvedic principles. 

110 
 
The Committee further recommends that the Ministry must ensure wider dissemination 
of the campaign through digital platforms, educational institutions and community 
health networks so that a larger segment of the population, particularly in rural and 
underserved areas can benefit from Prakriti-based health awareness and preventive 
healthcare practices. 
PARA 3.7.10 
 
AYURGYAN SCHEME 
 
43. The Committee notes with concern that the allocation under the Ayurgyan 
Scheme has witnessed significant downward revision at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage 
in consecutive financial years In 2024-25, the Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹49.50 crore was 
reduced to ₹ 27.00 crore at the RE stage, representing a reduction of approximately 45 
per cent, with actual expenditure of ₹ 25.70 crore. Similarly, in 2025-26, the BE of ₹ 
50.00 crore was reduced to ₹ 37.81 crore at the RE stage. The Committee takes into 
account the submission of the Ministry that due to this reduction of allocation at the RE 
stage, funds could not be released to certain projects approved by the Project 
Sanctioning Committee (PSC). 
PARA 3.8.3 
 
44. The Committee, while appreciating the efforts of the Ministry in supporting 
research activities under the Ayurgyan Scheme, is of the view that such recurring mid-
year contraction of funds at RE stage adversely affects the expansion and continuity of 
research projects and leads to delays in the execution of approved studies, particularly 
those sanctioned by the Project Sanctioning Committee. Particularly, the Committee 
takes into consideration the submission of the Ministry that reduction of budgetary 
allocation of ₹ 50.00 crore at BE to ₹ 37.81 crore at RE 2025-26 led to non release of 
funds to certain projects as approved by the Committee is further of the view that such 
budgetary uncertainty must have undermined the confidence of participating 
institutions and impede the effective achievement of the objectives of the scheme. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry evolve a protected funding 
mechanism for ongoing research projects so as to avoid slashing of budgetary allocation. 
The Committee further recommends that multi-year research commitments under the 
scheme should be planned and funded in a manner that ensures stability and continuity 
and are not subjected to annual budgetary volatility. 
PARA 3.8.4 
 
45. The Committee finds that there has been under-utilisation of funds available 
under the Ayurgyan Scheme during 2024-25. The Committee takes into account the  
submissions of the Ministry that the under-utilisation was primarily due to procedural 
and operational issues such as delays in utilisation of funds by implementing institutions 
and balances lying in the Central Nodal Agency (CNA) accounts beyond the admissible 
limits, which prevented further release of funds and resulted in savings. The Committee, 
in this regard recommends the Ministry to take appropriate precautionary measures so 
that the incident of underutilization funds under the scheme is not repeated in the 
ensuing financial year. 
PARA 3.8.5 
 
46. The Committee is anguished to observe that on perusal of the list of projects 
which could not receive funds due to under-utilisation and the CNA ceiling limits,  it 
found that several of these projects involved reputed institutions such as the All India 
Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS), New Delhi; All India Institute of Ayurveda 
(AIIA), New Delhi; Rajendra Institute of Medical Sciences, Ranchi; Thiruvalluvar 

111 
 
University, Vellore; Amrita Institute of Medical Sciences, Kochi; Guru Teg Bahadur 
Hospital, Delhi; and other academic and research institutions. The Committee further 
notes that the proposed studies covered diverse areas including clinical trials, 
pharmacological evaluation of drugs, microbial studies and integrative medicine 
interventions relating to conditions such as psoriasis, cancer, COVID-19 management, 
neurological disorders, depression and other health conditions. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommends that no projects should be deprived of fund.  
 
PARA 3.8.6 
 
47. The Committee is of the view that while financial prudence and adherence to 
procedural requirements are essential but such bottlenecks should not impede the 
timely execution of research projects under a scheme intended to promote scientific 
validation and innovation in Ayush systems of medicine. The Committee in this 
connection, recommends the Ministry to evolve increased level of monitoring of projects 
sanctioned under the Scheme and institute periodic reviews of their progress so as to 
avoid recurrence of such situations in future. The Committee is constrained to observe 
that the non-execution of such PSC-approved studies represents a missed opportunity 
for generating scientific evidence and advancing research in Ayush systems of medicine. 
 
PARA 3.8.7 
 
48. The Committee observes that under the Ayurgyan Scheme, the Ministry has 
supported 39 out of the targeted 50 research projects during the year (achievement of 
78%). While the progress in sanctioning and supporting research projects is 
noteworthy, the Committee observes with concern that outcome-level achievements 
remain relatively modest. Against the annual target of 14 validated drugs/treatment 
models, only 6 have been achieved so far (approximately 43%). This indicates that 
although the initiation and funding of research projects is progressing at a modest pace, 
the conversion of funded research into validated, standardized, and clinically accepted 
treatment models remains limited. The Committee further observes that under certain 
components, research studies have been initiated and in some cases have even exceeded 
the targeted numbers; however, the corresponding publication outputs remain a matter 
of concern. In the component of Ayurveda Biology Integrated Health Research, despite 
exceeding the target for research studies, no publications in reputed peer-reviewed 
journals have been reported so far. This reflects a significant lag between project 
initiation and dissemination of research findings, which undermines the larger objective 
of the Ayurgyan scheme to generate credible scientific evidence for Ayush systems. 
 
PARA 3.8.9 
 
 
49. The Committee notes with satisfaction that although a substantial proportion of 
the targeted research projects under the Ayurgyan Scheme have been supported so far 
as reported but despite adequate or even higher initiation of research studies in some 
components, the publication output remains, primarily a matter of committee’s concern 
indicating a lag between project implementation and dissemination of credible scientific 
evidence. In light thereof, the Committee recommends that the Ministry must 
strengthen the monitoring and outcome-tracking mechanisms of the Ayurgyan Scheme 
so that research projects translate into tangible outputs such as validated treatment
