models, standardized drugs and peer-reviewed scientific publications within defined 
timelines. The Ministry may also consider introducing clear milestones, periodic peer 
review and incentives for publication in reputed journals along with stronger 

112 
 
coordination between research institutions and clinical centres to ensure that funded 
projects effectively for contributing to evidence generation and wider acceptance of 
Ayush therapies. 
PARA 3.8.10 
 
 
SCHEME FOR CONSERVATION, DEVELOPMENT AND SUSTAINABLE 
MANAGEMENT OF MEDICINAL PLANTS UNDER NATIONAL MEDICINAL 
PLANTS BOARD (NMPB) 
 
50. The Committee finds that the budgetary allocation for the Central Sector 
Scheme for Conservation, Development and Sustainable Management of Medicinal 
Plants has witnessed significant fluctuations between the Budget Estimates and Revised 
Estimates in recent years. The Committee is of the view that such reductions at the 
Revised Estimate stage may affect the continuity and effectiveness of activities relating 
to conservation, resource augmentation and research support. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush, through the National Medicinal 
Plants Board, undertake more realistic assessment of financial requirements and 
strengthen planning and monitoring mechanisms so that funds allocated under the 
scheme are utilized in a timely and efficient manner, ensuring sustained progress in the 
conservation, development and sustainable management of medicinal plants. 
 
PARA 3.9.2 
 
 
51. The Committee takes note of the policy shift in direction aimed at reducing 
pressure on forest resources to organized cultivation of quality planting material while 
ensuring sustainable supply of medicinal plants for the Ayush industry. However, the 
Committee recommends that the Ministry’s reply to include benchmarks or timelines 
for reducing forest dependency or measurable targets for expanding cultivation of high-
demand species of medicinal plants. The Committee recommends that the Ministry 
transition from a purely promotional role to a target-driven roadmap based on species-
specific categorisation and with charted timelines by mandating a minimum 
procurement quota from organized cultivation clusters for gradually reducing 
dependence on forest-sourced medicinal plants. The Committee further recommends 
that Ministry explore for creating guaranteed market for farmers, effectively bridging 
the price gap between forest-sourced materials and cultivated species. The Committee is 
of the view that Ministry should also check out Action Plan to encourage farmers for 
cultivation of medicinal plants in cluster. 
PARA 3.9.4 
 
52. The Committee acknowledges the E-Charak portal as a positive step towards 
improving market transparency in the medicinal plants sector. However, the Committee 
has taken hand on experience of the portal and is of the view that the platform presently 
functions largely as an information interface providing price details and buyer seller 
contact channel which may not fully address the challenges faced by cultivators in 
accessing reliable buyers and stable markets. The Committee further notes that 
transactions between buyers and sellers are ultimately concluded through offline 
payment mechanisms which may increase the risk of fraud, delays in supply and lack of 
transaction security. In this regard, the Committee recommends that the Ministry to 
further strengthen the E-Charak platform by incorporating additional features such as 
a digital trading interface with secure payment processing, a “soil-to-shelf” traceability 
system for quality assurance, certification and demand forecasting mechanisms for key 

113 
 
medicinal plant species. The Ministry may also undertake focused efforts to promote 
wider adoption of the platform among farmers and Farmer Producer Organisations 
(FPOs) so as to enhance market access and ensure greater transparency and efficiency 
in the trade of medicinal plants. 
PARA 3.9.6 
 
53. The Committee finds that the Ministry has supported seed germplasm centres, 
nurseries and rural collection centres under the Central Sector Scheme of the National 
Medicinal Plants Board. The Ministry has also reported support for quality planting 
material and training of farmers in Good Agricultural Practices. However, the 
adequacy of expansion of these initiatives is still the matter of concern. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry undertake an assessment of the national 
requirement of Quality Planting Material (QPM) for medicinal plants and expand 
nursery networks accordingly, particularly in major cultivation clusters in meeting the 
increasing demand for planting material, particularly in view of the expansion of 
medicinal plant cultivation. 
PARA 3.9.7 
 
54. The Committee is given to understand that the Ministry has supported initiatives 
for Good Agricultural and Collection Practices (GACP) and voluntary certification of 
medicinal plant produce through capacity building programmes. However, the Ministry 
has indicated that presently no cultivation clusters are fully compliant with GACP 
standards which suggest that the adoption of quality certification frameworks remains 
limited. The Committee, in this regard, recommends that the Ministry takes pro active 
approach to develop a structured roadmap for wider adoption of GACP certification by 
providing technical support and financial incentives for cultivation clusters seeking 
certification. 
PARA 3.9.8 
 
55. While appreciating the initiative taken by the Ministry to explore convergence 
with afforestation programmes, the Committee observes that the proposal is still at a 
preliminary stage of inter-ministerial consultation without any timeline for 
operationalising the inclusion of medicinal plant plantations under Compensatory 
Afforestation Fund Management and Planning Authority (CAMPA) supported 
activities. The Committee is of the strong view that integration of medicinal plant 
plantations within national afforestation programmes could significantly contribute to 
sustainable supply of raw materials for the Ayush sector while simultaneously 
supporting ecological restoration and livelihood opportunities. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must pursue the proposal 
with the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change in a time-bound manner 
so that plantation of medicinal plants may be appropriately incorporated within 
CAMPA-supported afforestation activities and related national plantation programmes 
like “Ek Ped Maa ke naam”. The Committee further recommends that, once the 
proposal is finalised, the National Medicinal Plants Board may develop suitable 
guidelines in coordination with State Forest Departments to ensure that plantation of 
medicinal plants are scientifically planned, monitored and linked with conservation as 
well as sustainable utilisation objectives of the Ayush sector.  
PARA 3.9.11 
 
56. The Committee is of the considered view that Medicinal plants have been an 
integral part of traditional and modern healthcare systems due to their therapeutic 
potential and bioactive compounds. Medicinal plants offer natural, cost-effective 
remedies with fewer side effects for a wide range of health conditions, including chronic 

114 
 
diseases, infections, and metabolic disorders. In order to gain maximum benefits of these 
traditional plants, the availability of such medicinal plants are essential. In view of this, 
the Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry of Environment, Forest and 
Climate Change should make it mandatory that, in case of afforestation in degraded 
areas, at least 25 percent of the plantation from CAMPA funds should be of medicinal 
plants, suitable for that particular ecology. The Ministry should ensure that plantations 
contribute meaningfully to carbon sinks and biodiversity restoration rather than short-
term target fulfillment. 
PARA 3.9.12 
 
 
57. The Committee observes that the assessment of data regarding the percentage 
decrease in forest sourced raw material is nearly a decade old and the demand-supply 
dynamics of medicinal plants might have undergone significant changes in view of the 
Ministry’s recent initiatives for promotion and cultivation of medicinal plants. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry should undertake a fresh national 
assessment of demand and supply of medicinal plants to generate updated evidence for 
informed policy planning and scientific development of cultivation clusters. The 
Committee further recommends for establishing a dynamic National Medicinal Plants 
Demand-Supply Database, preferably integrated with the e-Charak Portal and updated 
periodically in coordination with industry stakeholders, State Medicinal Plants Boards 
and Ayush manufacturers. Such a mechanism would enable identification of high-
demand species, facilitate better planning of cultivation initiatives and help address 
issues relating to market uncertainty and price volatility in the medicinal plants sector. 
 
PARA 3.9.14 
 
58. The Committee takes note of the submission of the Ministry that targets such as 
5 medicinal plant species conservation projects, 1500 hectares in-situ resource 
augmentation and 13.5 lakh saplings regeneration have been undertaken. The 
Committee, however, observes that the target set by the Ministry appear limited 
considering India’s vast medicinal plant biodiversity. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that while conservation initiatives are being undertaken, the scale of 
interventions need to be expanded to address threats to rare and endangered medicinal 
plant species. The Committee also recommends the Ministry to scale up conservation 
programmes, particularly for rare, endangered and endemic medicinal plant species, 
and promote community participation in conservation efforts. 
PARA 3.9.15 
 
AYURSWASTHY YOJANA 
 
59. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Ministry to make concerted 
efforts to provide adequate budgetary support to the Ayurswasthya Scheme. The 
Committee views that repeated reduction of Budget Estimates (BE) at the Revised 
Estimate (RE) stage does not provide a stable equilibrium of the scheme’s financial 
management and may affect effective programme implementation. Moreover, a gap 
between the initial budgetary projections and the actual allocation at BE and RE Stage 
hampers the implementation pace and capacity of the scheme. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry review the fund flow and monitoring 
mechanisms under the Central Nodal Agency (CNA) framework and undertake a 
careful assessment of the factors leading to repeated revisions at the RE stage so as to 
ensure that budgetary allocations translate into timely financial support to 
implementing institutions enabling the institute to achieve the cherished goal of the 

115 
 
scheme. The Committee further emphases that the Ministry should ensure more 
realistic budgeting, strengthen appraisal of project proposals and align financial 
allocations with implementable project pipelines so that the allocations made at the BE 
stage translate into tangible and credible outcomes.  
PARA 3.10.2 
 
60. The Committee takes note of the list of 26 projects supported by the Ministry 
under the Ayush and Public Health component of the Ayurswasthya Yojana during the 
financial years 2022-23 to 2024-25. The Committee observes that an overwhelming 
majority of the projects have been undertaken by Government institutions (20 out of 
25), while only a limited number of projects (five) involve non-government institutions, 
including NGOs and trusts. This pattern appears inconsistent with the broader design of 
the scheme, which envisages the participation of a diverse set of stakeholders including 
Government institutions, non-profit and voluntary organisations, NGOs, athletes, and 
other institutions to promote community-based Ayush interventions, research, and 
outreach initiatives. The Committee further notes that the projects supported under the 
scheme appear to be limited in scale and concentrated in a relatively small number of 
institutions and geographical areas. Such trend indicates that the full potential of the 
scheme to mobilise diverse institutional participation for expanding Ayush based 
community health services has not yet been fully realised. The Committee is, therefore, 
of the firm view that the Ministry should take proactive measures to broaden 
institutional participation under the scheme by undertaking targeted outreach and 
awareness initiatives. The Ministry should also encourage the participation of eligible 
non-government institutions by simplifying and standardising the procedures and 
addressing existing bottlenecks. The Committee recommends the Ministry to encourage 
the participants besides ensuring for balanced distribution of projects and promote 
collaborative projects involving government institutions, NGOs and community based 
organisations along with strengthening monitoring and undertaking periodic review of 
project outcomes to ensure that the scheme effectively contributes to expanding 
community-level Ayush services and research activities. 
PARA 3.10.4 
 
 
61. The Committee observes that the financial norms for projects under the 
Ayurswasthya Yojana are framed at the time of the scheme’s launch around 2021-22, 
with a maximum project support of ₹ 1.5 crore for three years under the Public Health 
Intervention component. The Committee is of the view that considering the rise in costs 
of medicines, diagnostics, manpower and programme implementation, the real value of 
financial assistance may not be lucrative for Non-Government organizations to 
undertake initiative under the scheme. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry undertake a periodic review of financial norms and cost ceilings under the 
scheme, taking into account inflation and implementation experience so as to ensure 
that the sanctioned projects are adequately resourced to achieve their intended public 
health outcomes. 
PARA 3.10.5 
 
Ayush Oushadhi Gunvatta Evam Utpadan Samvardhan Yojana (AOGUSY) 
 
62. The Committee expresses serious concern that there has been substantial 
reduction of BE allocation to AOGUSY  at the RE stage in last five years highlighting 
the issue of low utilization of funds as recurring phenomena and the Ministry has not 
taken adequate corrective measures despite a precautionary observation of the 
Committee in its earlier report, instead the Ministry attributing the shortfall to 

116 
 
procedural and operational difficulties faced by the grantee institutions. The Committee 
believes that such procedural bottlenecks, inadequate implementation support and 
delays in procurement processes indicate systemic deficiencies that require stronger 
institutional oversight and facilitation by the Ministry. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush institute a robust monitoring and 
implementation mechanism for the AOGUSY scheme, including periodic review of fund 
utilization, streamlined procurement procedures, and capacity-building support for 
grantee institutions to ensure timely execution of projects under the scheme. The 
Ministry must put in place a comprehensive financial planning framework to prevent 
repeated reduction of Budget Estimates at the Revised Estimates stage and to enhance 
the absorptive capacity of implementing agencies.  
PARA 3.11.3 
 
63. The Committee examined the physical and financial progress indicators along 
with the achievements highlighted by the Ministry. The Committee notes that 
complaints received through the Ayush Suraksha Portal relating to misleading 
advertisements are forwarded to the respective State Drug Licensing Authorities for 
necessary action. The Committee is of the view that such a mechanism may lead to 
inconsistent approaches in dealing with complaints across States, as the capacity, human 
resources, and enforcement priorities of State authorities vary considerably. Further, 
the absence of a uniform timeline and monitoring framework for disposal of complaints 
may defeat the very purpose of the complaint reporting mechanism and could 
potentially discourage citizens from reporting such violations. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ayush vertical under the Central Drugs Standard 
Control Organization (CDSCO) should develop a uniform and standardised mechanism 
for handling complaints relating to misleading advertisements of Ayush products. The 
Committee further recommends that clear timelines must be prescribed for action by 
State authorities, along with active monitoring and periodic review at the Central level, 
to ensure timely and effective enforcement of the scheme in addressing complaints that 
have scaled up to a total of 11425 and 12750 during 2024-25 and 2025-26 (up to 
December 2025) The Committee, therefore, recommends for more stringent penal 
provisions for misleading advertisements. 
PARA 3.11.5 
 
 
64. The Committee observes that despite the establishment of a nationwide 
pharmacovigilance network with multiple Intermediary and Peripheral 
Pharmacovigilance Centres, the number of reported Suspected Adverse Drug Reactions 
(ADRs) remains relatively low, which may indicate possible underreporting or limited 
awareness among practitioners and institutions. The Committee, therefore,  
recommends that the Ministry to consider making ADR reporting mandatory for Ayush 
practitioners and pharmacists and strengthen ADR reporting mechanisms through 
enhanced training of practitioners, increased awareness programmes and integration of 
pharmacovigilance reporting systems with healthcare institutions so as to ensure more 
comprehensive monitoring of drug safety. 
PARA 3.11.6 
 
65. The Committee notes the financial mobilisation under the scheme with nearly 
78% of the total cumulative grants (₹13.76 crore out of ₹17.50 crore) released during 
FY 2024-25. The Committee, however, observes that a gap appears to exist between 
financial outlays and corresponding physical or regulatory outcomes as only three out of 
the five funded pharmacies have progressed from infrastructure upgradation to the 
stage of WHO-GMP certification. The Committee is of the view that despite substantial 

117 
 
financial allocation, delays in regulatory compliance may hinder the timely achievement 
of the scheme’s objective of ensuring international quality standardisation in Ayush 
drug manufacturing. In this regard, the Committee recommends that the Ministry 
should develop a technical mentorship mechanism including pre-audit inspections for 
institutions that remain at the “in-process” stage. The Committee further recommends 
the introduction of targeted capacity-building programmes for pharmacists, 
technicians, and other technical staff working in the pharmacies so as to ensure that 
human resource capabilities keep pace with the upgraded infrastructure and 
technological investments made under the scheme. 
PARA 3.11.8 
 
INSTITUTE OF TEACHING AND RESEARCH IN AYURVEDA (ITRA) 
 
66. The Committee appreciates the ITRA for leveraging its autonomous status and 
expresses satisfaction that ITRA has been making commendable efforts in the field of 
Ayurveda education & research upholding its status as an Institute of National 
Importance. The Committee is of the view that the Ministry of Ayush should continue to 
ensure adequate and timely financial support to the Institute so that ongoing 
infrastructure projects and academic activities are implemented efficiently and the 
Institute is further strengthened to advance research, education and global outreach in 
Ayurveda. The Committee recommends the Ministry to encourage ITRA for exploring 
the enhancement of student intake capacity across all courses (of the institute) 
considering its pivotal role in propagation and promotion of Ayurveda learning and 
practices domestically and internationally. 
PARA 3.12.2 
 
 
67. The Committee appreciates these short term initiatives proposed to be 
undertaken by the ITRA and and considers that present budgetary allocation may not 
be sufficient to meet the proposed capital intensive initiatives. The Committee therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry must examine the needs of the institute positively to 
support the establishment of the Ayush Start-up Incubation Centre with dedicated 
funding, structured mentorship and collaboration with industry and research 
institutions to encourage innovation in Ayurveda-based technologies. The Committee 
further recommends that the Institute undertake a comprehensive human resource 
assessment to strengthen the pharmacovigilance framework by ensuring the 
recruitment of adequate technical manpower, the development of robust digital 
reporting systems and enhanced coordination with national regulatory bodies, so that 
the safety monitoring of Ayurvedic medicines aligns with global standards. In addition, 
the Committee recommends that structured international clinical training programmes 
be developed in collaboration with foreign universities and WHO to promote academic 
exchange, standardised training modules and wider global acceptance of Ayurveda. 
 
PARA 3.12.4 
 
68. The Committee expresses concern over vacancy of 106 posts against sanctioned 
strength of 321 posts i.e  that one-third of manpower shortage in ITRA that must have  
adversely affected the institute’s ability to discharge its mandate effectively, particularly 
in the areas of research output, academic instruction and patient care services. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Ministry to undertake an immediate 
review of the vacant posts so that time-bound measures are undertaken to fill up the 
existing live vacancies so that the institutional capacity of ITRA is strengthened and its 
core functions are not compromised. 

118 
 
PARA 3.12.4 
 
 
69. The Committee has taken note that the Institute’s reported publication of 160 
research articles and in-house journal “AYU” received 411 article submissions during 
2024-25, out of which only 20 articles were published after the peer review process, 
thereby meaning the acceptability rate is 4.86%. The Committee is of the view that, 
while the volume of research activity appears significant, greater emphasis needs to be 
placed on improving the quality, international indexing and citation impact of research 
outputs. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush and the 
Institute take focused steps to promote high quality research, encourage publications in 
reputed international journals and strengthen collaborative research with national and 
global institutions so as to enhance the global academic visibility of Ayurveda research. 
 
PARA 3.12.5 
 
70. The Committee appreciates the global collaborations of the Institute of Teaching 
and Research in Ayurveda (ITRA), including its recognition as a WHO Collaborating 
Centre for Traditional Medicine and the existence of eight active international MoUs. 
The Committee however, observes that foreign student enrolment remains limited. As 
per the Annual Report 2024-25 of the institute, Under International Centre for 
Ayurvedic studies, 03 scholars had completed 3-month introductory course of Ayurveda 
programme (300 classes) in this year and there were 13 new admissions for foreign 
nationals across BAMS (8), M.D. (3), and Ph.D. (2) indicating modest global academic 
engagement for an Institute of National Importance. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that ITRA, in coordination with the Ministry of Ayush & WHO strengthen 
international outreach, expand collaborations with foreign universities and introduce 
targeted fellowship or scholarship schemes and short-term training programmes to 
attract more foreign students.   
PARA 3.12.6 
 
NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR HOMOEPATHY (NCH) 
 
71. The Committee observes that against the actual expenditure of ₹ 16.00 crore in 
2024-25, an amount of ₹ 20.00 crore has been allocated to the National Commission for 
Homoeopathy (NCH) in 2025-26, and the allocation to the tune of ₹ 20.00 crore has been 
earmarked in BE 2026-27. The Committee notes that the National Commission for 
Homoeopathy has been seeking additional funds for engagement of manpower to 
effectively discharge its regulatory and supervisory functions. The Committee has been 
given to understand that the NCH has granted permissions to a significant number of 
homoeopathy colleges in recent years, including 541 permissions in 2025-26 and 239 in 
2024-25 indicating a substantial expansion in the number of institutions and admissions 
in the homoeopathy education sector. However, the budgetary allocation to the 
Commission does not appear to be commensurate with the growing regulatory 
responsibilities arising from such scale of expansion. The Committee is of the view that 
the increase in the number of institutions and student intake necessarily requires 
strengthening of regulatory oversight, including adequate manpower and institutional 
capacity. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the National Commission for 
Homoeopathy undertake a comprehensive assessment of its manpower and functional 
requirements and that the Ministry of Ayush examine the matter and take up the issue 
of increased allocation to Ministry of Finance to consider enhancing the financial 
allocation so as to enable the Commission to effectively regulate homoeopathy education 
and ensure quality standards across institutions. 

119 
 
PARA 3.13.2 
 
72. The Committee has undertaken a comparative assessment of the expenditure 
heads of the National Commission for Homoeopathy (NCH) and the National 
Commission for Indian System of Medicine (NCISM). The Committee finds that NCH 
has incurred ₹4.42 crore under the head “Assessment & Rating” whereas NCISM has 
reported ₹2.57 crore under the same head. However, NCISM also reflects a separate 
expenditure of ₹4.25 crore under the head “Inspection” which appears to relate to 
regulatory assessment activities. The Committee is of the view that the regulatory 
expenditure should have common & uniform heads for accessing comparative actual 
cost of institutions. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush 
adopt a uniform classification of regulatory expenditure across its statutory bodies 
clearly defining heads such as assessment, inspection and rating ensuring enhanced 
transparency, enable meaningful comparison of regulatory costs and promote more 
efficient utilisation of public funds.  
PARA 3.13.3 
 
NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR INDIAN SYSTEM OF MEDICINE (NCISM) 
 
73. The Committee observes that the NCISM is largely functioning through 
contractual and outsourced manpower, the number of which is more than double the 
sanctioned strength of regular staff. Such heavy dependence on non-regular personnel 
may adversely affect institutional continuity, accountability and long-term capacity 
building within the Commission. The Committee, therefore recommends that the 
Ministry of Ayush undertake a comprehensive review of manpower deployment in its 
statutory regulatory bodies, particularly with regard to the large number of vacant 
regular posts and the growing reliance on contractual and outsourced personnel. The 
Committee further recommends that time-bound steps be taken to fill up the vacant 
sanctioned posts especially in key administrative positions. Such measures would help 
strengthen long-term regulatory capacity, enhance institutional continuity and 
accountability, and reduce excessive dependence on outsourced manpower. 
 
PARA 3.14.3 
 
74. The Committee takes into consideration the steps taken by the NCISM for 
improving quality and affordable medical education in ASUS system including the new 
curricula. The Committee appreciates the reforms taken to integrate modern medical 
science and clinical research with traditional Ayurvedic knowledge and expresses 
satisfaction for adoption of student-centered pedagogy. The new reforms also mandate 
multi-disciplinary education for the students which shall enhance pluralistic healthcare 
in practice. The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Ayush and NCISM ensure 
effective implementation of the revised curricula across all institutions through regular 
monitoring, faculty capacity-building and the provision of adequate clinical, teaching 
and laboratory infrastructure. The Committee further recommends that structured 
feedback mechanisms be instituted to obtain inputs from students, faculty and other 
stakeholders so as to assess whether the reforms are achieving their intended outcomes. 
The Ministry should ensure that institutions possess the required infrastructure, 
maintain faculty strength and promote clinical exposure necessary for the effective 
delivery of the revised competency-based curriculum. 
PARA 3.14.4 
 
 

120 
 
75. The Committee takes note of the curriculum reforms undertaken by the National 
Commission for Indian System of Medicine indicating the paradigm shift from 
traditional lecture-based teaching to a more competency and skill-based learning 
framework, particularly the restructuring of lecture to non-lecture hours in the ratio of 
1:2 encouraging greater practical exposure and active learning. The Committee also 
finds inconsistencies in internship requirements across different systems and a lack of 
structured research components during these internships. The absence of a structured 
research-oriented component during internship may also limit students’ engagement 
with evidence-based practices. The Committee recommends that the National 
Commission for Indian System of Medicine must consider introducing a mandatory 
internship component of suitable duration for undergraduates across all systems at their 
respective Central Research Councils under the Ministry of Ayush to provide uniform 
research exposure, strengthen clinical and scientific training and promote integration of 
education with ongoing research activities, thereby improving the overall quality and 
consistency of medical training in the Indian Systems of Medicine. 
PARA 3.14.5 
 
 
76. The Committee was apprised that the National Commission for Indian System of 
Medicine has proposed a “Publication Grant” scheme to support eligible candidates in 
publishing research papers in quality journals. The Committee appreciates the proposal 
and considers it a positive step towards encouraging research orientation and academic 
curiosity among scholars. The Committee in this regard recommends that the Ministry 
of Ayush to get approved the schemes and implement them as soon as possible. The 
Committee adds that the Ministry should explore the possibility of scaling the scheme to 
other institutes to promote research output across the Indian systems of medicine. 
 
PARA 3.14.6 
 
CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN AYURVEDIC SCIENCES (CCRAS)  
 
77. The Committee finds that the Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic 
Sciences (CCRAS) has undertaken numerous clinical trials, community studies and 
public health initiatives. The Committee, however, observes that the completion of 130 
research projects (92 during 2024-25 and an additional 38 up to January 2026) over the 
last two years. The Committee, therefore, recommends for large-scale interdisciplinary 
research collaborations with premier institutions such as AIIMS, ICMR, IITs and other 
major biomedical institutes for enlargement of the scope for generating robust evidence 
and wider scientific validation of Ayurveda-based interventions. The Committee 
believes that expanding multicentric and interdisciplinary research collaborations with 
premier institutions such as AIIMS, ICMR, IITs and reputed international 
organisations, along with greater emphasis on timely completion of projects and 
publication of research in high-quality peer-reviewed journals would strengthen the 
scientific credibility and global acceptance of Ayurveda-based interventions. The 
Committee is of the firm view that the Ministry of Ayush and CCRAS must enhance the 
scale, quality and impact of research undertaken across its institutes. Moreover, the 
Ministry should ensure better translation of CCRAS research findings into evidence-
based clinical guidelines, national health programmes and public health interventions so 
that the outcomes of Ayurveda research can be effectively integrated into the broader 
healthcare system.  
PARA 3.15.3 
 

121 
 
78. The Committee has been given to understand that the Central Council for 
Research in Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS) has undertaken research on preventive health 
approaches such as Dinacharya, Ritucharya, Rasayana therapies and lifestyle 
interventions aimed at improving quality of life and addressing non-communicable 
diseases. While such initiatives highlight the preventive potential of Ayurveda, the 
Committee observes that there is limited structured research on behaviour change 
models that promote sustained adoption of Ayurvedic lifestyle practices among different 
population groups. The Committee, therefore, recommends that CCRAS must 
undertake structured research on behaviour-change interventions enshrined in 
Ayurvedic principles such as Dinacharya, Ritucharya and Ahara-Vihara. The Committee 
in this regard, recommends that the Ministry must guide CCRAS to undertake the 
development of evidence-based behaviour change modules and community-based 
intervention models integrating Ayurvedic principles with modern behavioural science 
approaches and their pilot implementation across diverse population groups including 
urban communities, school students and high-risk groups vulnerable to lifestyle 
diseases. The Committee desires that the ongoing recruitment drive in CCRAS must be 
completed expeditiously so as to augment the human resource capacity required for 
implementation of the proposed research initiatives. 
PARA 3.15.4 
 
79. The Committee takes into account the Five-Year Action Plan (2026-27 to 2030-
31) formulated by CCRAS to expand clinical research in Ayurveda with a focus on non-
communicable diseases, lifestyle disorders, geriatric health, women’s health and 
Panchakarma procedure validation, among others. The Committee notes the submission 
of CCRAS that the Action Plan is only indicative and its implementation is subject to 
necessary administrative, technical, Institutional Ethics Committee (IEC) and financial 
approvals, as well as availability of funds. The Committee therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry must ensure timely administrative, technical and financial approvals for 
Action Plan so that the proposed Action Plan does not remain merely indicative but is 
implemented in letter and spirit in a time-bound manner. The Ministry should also 
ensure adequate allocation of financial resources and strengthening of institutional 
capacity to facilitate the conduct of multicentric clinical trials and collaborative 
research.  
PARA 3.15.5 
 
80. The Committee is of the considered view that periodic monitoring mechanisms 
must be instituted to track the progress of the Action Plan and ensure that research 
outcomes should translate into evidence-based policy interventions and standard 
treatment protocols for wider adoption of Ayurveda in the healthcare system.  
 
PARA 3.15.6 
 
CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN HOMOEOPATHY (CCRH)  
 
81. The Committee takes note of the expansion of research activities undertaken by 
the Central Council for Research in Homoeopathy (CCRH) over the last three years. 
The Committee observes that the increased research activities have not translated into 
commensurate research outcomes as the rate of project conclusion remains relatively 
slow and limited. The Committee is cognizant of the fact that many research projects 
might longer gestation duration and might not yet have reached due date for 
completion. The Committee, nevertheless, recommends CCRH to undertake a 
comprehensive assessment of ongoing projects and strengthen project planning and 
monitoring so that research & activities result in tangible outcomes capable of 

122 
 
strengthening the evidence base and contributing to the formulation of standard 
treatment guidelines. Further, in view of the continuing observation regarding the 
delayed efficacy of homoeopathic medicines, the Committee emphasizes that projects 
undertaken in the areas of Drug Standardization and Fundamental Research category 
should be accelerated so that the findings can support the development of 
pharmacopoeial standards and quality assurance framework for Homoeopathy. The 
Committee also recommends that research studies in the Public Health Research 
category be expanded given its pivotal role in demonstrating the broader applicability of 
homoeopathy in public health interventions. 
PARA 3.16.4 
 
82. Responding to the Committee’s query, the Ministry apprised the Committee that 
certain research infrastructure projects have been deprioritised or delayed due to 
funding constraints including redevelopment and expansion of research facilities and 
construction of new research institutes. Additionally, the Council has submitted that 
delays in some research projects occurred due to issues such as procurement 
procedures, staffing shortages and administrative processes. The Committee expresses 
serious concern over such delays that might have adversely affected the pace and quality 
of research activities undertaken by the Council. The Committee, in this connection, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must pay adequate attention to strengthening 
laboratory infrastructure and advanced research facilities to enable high-quality 
experimental and translational research. The Committee is of the firm view that the 
research activities should not be subjected to funds constraints. The Committee, 
therefore recommends CCRH to streamline administrative procedures relating to 
project approvals, procurement and recruitment so as to ensure timely implementation 
of research projects and work towards the capacity building framework. 
 
PARA 3.16.5 
 
83. The Committee is not satisfied to observe the response of the Ministry admitting 
that no independent performance evaluation of the Central Council for Research in 
Homoeopathy (CCRH) has been conducted so far. Considering the increasing allocation 
of public funds for research in traditional systems of medicine, the absence of an 
external evaluation mechanism may leave scope for an objective assessment of the 
Council’s research productivity, institutional efficiency, and public health impact. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush should institute a 
periodic independent performance evaluation of CCRH by a panel of experts drawn 
from biomedical sciences, public health and research management. Such an evaluation 
would help ensure greater transparency, accountability and continuous assessment in 
the Council’s research activities vis-à-vis mandate and goals & objectives of the Council. 
 
PARA 3.16.6 
 
CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN UNANI (CCRUM)  
 
 
84. The Committee finds that out of 766 sanctioned posts in the Central Council for 
Research in Unani Medicine, 345 posts are presently vacant. The Committee further 
notes that while the revival of 131 posts is under process, 21 posts have been proposed to 
be abolished. The Committee observes with concern that such a high level of vacancies 
may adversely affect the effective functioning of the Council and impede the progress of 
research activities in Unani medicine. The Committee is of the view that adequate 
human resources are essential for the timely implementation of research programmes 

123 
 
and for strengthening the scientific base of the Unani system of medicine. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must take expeditious 
steps to fill up the vacant posts in the Council so as to ensure that it is adequately staffed 
to effectively carry out its research mandate and promote the development of evidence-
based research in Unani medicine. 
PARA 3.17.2 
 
85. The Committee notes that medicinal plants constitute the primary raw material 
base for Unani formulations and are essential for sustaining research and therapeutic 
practices in the Unani system of medicine. However, the availability of several medicinal 
plant species used in Unani pharmacopoeia remains underutilised due to limited 
systematic surveys, inadequate cultivation practices and over-dependence on wild 
sources. The Committee is of firm view that the absence of coordinated efforts for large-
scale cultivation, conservation, and scientific documentation of medicinal plants might 
have affected the long-term sustainability and quality of raw materials required for 
Unani drug development and research. The Committee, therefore, recommends for 
expanding the survey, cultivation and conservation of medicinal plants used in Unani 
medicine and explore further collaboration with agricultural universities and state 
governments to ensure sustainable availability of raw materials and to prevent depletion 
of rare medicinal species. 
PARA 3.17.4 
 
ALL INDIA INSTITUTE OF AYURVEDA (AIIA) 
 
86. The Committee is concerned by the 40 % reduction in allocation under the 
Grants-in-Aid (General) component vis-à-vis the amount projected in BE 2026-27. Since 
the component primarily supports academic activities, outreach programmes, research 
projects, and maintenance of institutional facilities, the Committee apprehends that the 
reduced allocation may constrain the institution’s operational capacity and proposed 
initiatives including research programmes and evidence generation in Ayurveda. The 
Committee observes that recent recruitment is likely to augment the human resource 
capacity of the institute and optimal utilisation of this manpower would require the 
expansion of research initiatives and patient-centric activities. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry reassess the fund requirements of AIIA, 
particularly under the GIA (General) head so that the institution’s core activities are 
not adversely affected. Considering the critical role of AIIA as a Centre of Excellence in 
advancing the development and recognition of Ayush, the Committee further 
recommends that AIIA should not face constraints in funding for its core activities and 
that the Ministry may consider providing additional allocation at the Revised Estimate 
stage, keeping in view projected demand of Rs. 370.00 core for 2026-27 for ensuring 
implementation of originally envisaged Action Plan during the year. 
PARA 3.18.3 
 
87. The Committee has also taken cognizance of the key concern areas highlighted 
by AIIA, including the development of training centres, establishment of an 
International School campus, and expansion of the existing campus, which indicate the 
need for enhanced budgetary support. The Committee is of the firm view that AIIA, 
being a premier Centre of Excellence in Ayurveda, requires planned expansion of its 
campus, including the establishment of domain-specific Centres of Excellence and 
academic infrastructure such as an international school campus. The Committee point 
out that such view is reinforced by the recent budget announcement regarding the 
establishment of three new AIIA institutions across the country which reflects the 
growing pivotal role of AIIAs in the Ayush sector. In this context, the Committee 

124 
 
believes that the increasing national and global stature of Ayush necessitates expansion 
of the AIIA campus to support advanced research, training and academic activities. The 
Committee therefore recommends that the Ministry undertake a comprehensive 
assessment of AIIA’s expansion requirements vis-à-vis Phase 2 expansions and prepare 
a well-structured development plan and present the same to the Ministry of Finance for 
enhanced budgetary allocation so that the proposed initiatives can be implemented in a 
timely and effective manner. 
PARA 3.18.4 
 
88. The Committee finds that there has been a significant increase in OPD footfall at 
AIIA and its outreach centres, rising from 4.65 lakh patients in 2023 to about 7.78 lakh 
patients in 2025, however, the data indicates that nearly 96% of the OPD services are 
concentrated at the two main institutes in New Delhi and Goa, while the outreach 
centres account for only a marginal share. The Committee observes that AIIA Goa has 
shown the most rapid growth, with OPD footfall increasing from 83,096 in 2023 to 
2,59,125 in 2025, representing an increase of about 211%. Such substantial rise suggests 
that establishing a major Ayurveda institute in a prominent tourism destination like 
Goa has been successful in attracting patients and promoting Ayurveda services 
including supporting medical value travel and wellness tourism. The Committee feels 
that the excellence and specialised services of AIIA need to be expanded beyond the 
existing limited outreach network so that the benefits of the institute’s expertise can 
reach a wider population. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
may consider establishing one AIIA institution out of the three AIIAs proposed in 
budget 2026-27 in the North Eastern region which would attract tourists from south east 
countries and  support the Act East Policy by promoting Ayurveda based medical value 
travel and strengthening India’s traditional medicine outreach in the region.  The 
Committee further recommends that AIIA develop stronger referral linkages with 
district hospitals and Ayush health centres, which would help in decentralizing patient 
load from the main institutes while extending specialised Ayurveda services to a larger 
population across the country.  
PARA 3.18.6 
 
89. The Committee has been given understand that the All India Institute of 
Ayurveda (AIIA), Delhi operates a 200-bed hospital which, based on an average length 
of stay of about 9 days (based on the three-year data provided by the Ministry), has the 
capacity to handle approximately 8,100 inpatient admissions annually. However, the 
data indicates that the actual number of admissions has remained significantly below 
this potential capacity, with 4,660 admissions in 2023, 4,937 in 2024, and 5,344 in 2025, 
reflecting utilisation levels of only about 57%, 61%, and 66% respectively. The 
Committee is of the view that the hospital infrastructure has remained underutilised, 
with an estimated unused capacity ranging from about 2,700 to 3,400 admissions 
annually during the said period. The Committee observes that bed utilisation has shown 
gradual improvement over the years, a substantial proportion of the available bed 
capacity still remains unutilised. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry must take steps to optimiseutilisation of the existing inpatient infrastructure at 
AIIA, Delhi by strengthening referral linkages with district hospitals, Ayush health 
centres and other public healthcare facilities. The Ministry may also explore measures 
such as expanding specialised therapies, promoting Panchakarma and integrative 
treatment services, and increasing awareness about inpatient Ayurveda care, so that the 
available bed capacity is utilised more efficiently. Such measures would help in 
maximising the use of existing infrastructure while improving access to institutional 
Ayurveda treatment services.  
PARA 3.18.7 

125 
 
 
 
NATIONAL INSTITUTE OF HOMOEOPATHY, KOLKATA 
 
90. The Committee appreciates NIH, Kolkata for servicing 2200 OPD patients per 
day, however, the underutilisation of the existing IPD capacity coupled with shortage of 
doctors and staff raises concern. The Committee is of the view that the OPD patient load 
should be managed by expanding the existing space so that the current infrastructure be 
utilized for the intended purpose as diversion may adversely affect clinical training and 
inpatient-based academic activities of the institute. The Committee recommends the 
Ministry undertake comprehensive assessment of the proposal for expansion of the 
hospital capacity to 250 beds keeping into account the institute’s operational, 
infrastructural and manpower constraints. The Committee views that the Ministry 
should take concrete steps for strengthening human resources, the proposal for 
expansion of hospital capacity should be considered and approved. 
PARA 3.19.2 
 
NATIONAL INSTITUTE OF AYURVEDA, JAIPUR 
 
91. The Committee observes that an inverted admission seats structure of NIA, 
Jaipur could dilute the organic academic progression within the institution and may 
also raise concerns regarding the adequacy of clinical exposure, faculty availability and 
teaching infrastructure required for a large PG cohort. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush review the student-faculty-clinical load balance 
and overall academic structure of the institute to ensure that the UG-PG seat ratio 
remains academically sustainable and aligned with regulatory norms. The Ministry may 
consider gradually increasing UG seats to strengthen the institutional academic 
pipeline; or rationalising PG intake in accordance with available teaching faculty, 
clinical material and infrastructure. 
PARA 3.20.5 
 
RASHTRIYA AYURVEDA VIDYAPEETH (RAV) 
 
92. The Committee acknowledges that the Guru Shisya Parampara programme in 
RAV plays an important role in bridging the gap between classical textual knowledge 
and practical clinical application. However, the Committee observes that the coverage of 
the programme remains limited in scale considering the large pool of experienced 
traditional practitioners and the growing demand for advanced practical training 
among Ayurveda graduates. The Committee, therefore recommends that the Ministry 
may consider expanding the scope and outreach of the Guru-Shishya Parampara 
programme by increasing the number of participating Gurus and Shishyas and 
ensuring wider geographical coverage.  
PARA 3.21.2 
 
CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN SIDDHA  
 
93. The Committee observes that these initiatives play an important role in 
promoting research and documentation of the Siddha system of medicine. However, the 
Committee is of the view that Siddha research activities and institutional presence 
remain largely concentrated in the southern region of the country, particularly in Tamil 
Nadu. The Committee further observes that the North Eastern Region, with its rich 
biodiversity and strong traditions of herbal medicine, offers significant potential for 
expanding Siddha research and medicinal plant cultivation. The Committee, therefore, 

126 
 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush explore the expansion of Siddha research 
activities and institutional presence in suitable regions beyond southern India, including 
the North Eastern States/Railways/PSUs/CGHS facilities, in order to promote wider 
dissemination of Siddha knowledge and strengthen research on medicinal plants 
relevant to the Siddha system. The Committee believes that such initiatives would 
contribute to the broader development and national outreach of the Siddha system of 
medicine. 
PARA 3.22.2 
 
ISSUES RAISED BY THE MEMBERS 
 
94. The Committee notes that the Ministry has undertaken several initiatives for the 
promotion of Ayurveda through research institutions, awareness programmes and 
outreach activities. The Committee however is of the view that these efforts need to 
translate into greater adoption of Ayurveda practices and increased use of medicinal 
plants among the general public. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry should intensify its outreach and awareness programmes with a focused 
approach towards encouraging the practical use of Ayurveda at the community level. In 
this regard, wider dissemination of knowledge regarding commonly used medicinal 
plants such as Aloe vera, Giloy and Sarpagandha etc. should be undertaken through 
educational institutions and community-level campaigns. The Committee expects that 
such initiatives will promote preventive healthcare practices and strengthen the 
grassroots acceptance of Ayurveda across the country. 
PARA 4.1.2 
 
95. The Committee notes the outreach initiatives taken by the Ministry to enhance 
the global market and export potential of Ayush products through policy support, 
quality standardization and international outreach. The Committee, however, is of the 
view that sustained efforts are required to further strengthen the global recognition, 
standardisation & regulatory acceptance and quality assurance of Ayush medicines and 
wellness products. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must 
intensify its efforts towards strengthening international certification mechanisms, 
scientific validation and market access support for Ayush products in foreign markets. 
The Committee further recommends for extending export incentives and hand holding 
facilitation for foreign market penetration and enhance India’s share in the global 
traditional medicine market and strengthen the international positioning of Ayush 
systems. 
PARA 4.1.4 
 
96. The Committee notes that a substantial portion of the allocated funds of RE 
2025-26 (around 16%) still remains to be utilised during the closing month of the 
financial year. The Committee is concerned that such last-minute expenditure may lead 
to “March-rush” spending, which has been consistently discouraged by the Ministry of 
Finance as well as by previous recommendations of the Committee. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen its financial planning and quarterly 
monitoring mechanisms to ensure more balanced and timely utilisation of funds 
throughout the financial year. The Committee expects that improved expenditure 
management will enhance financial discipline and ensure more effective implementation 
of Ayush programmes. 
PARA 4.2.2 
 
97. The Committee notes the multiple initiatives taken by the Ministry and the 
regulatory bodies to strengthen the quality of Ayush education through curriculum 

127 
 
revision, competency-based training and awareness programmes. The Committee, 
however, is of the view that sustained efforts are required to further improve 
interdisciplinary academic standards, research orientation and practical clinical 
exposure for students pursuing Ayush education. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry continue to strengthen faculty development programmes, 
field level research integration and practical training in teaching institutions as well as 
in research councils. The Committee expects that such measures will contribute towards 
producing well-trained Ayush professionals capable of addressing evolving healthcare 
needs. 
PARA 4.3.2 
 
98. The Committee observes that the Ministry has initiated measures to incorporate 
modern diagnostics and research literacy within the Ayush curriculum and to promote 
cross-disciplinary engagement with modern medical institutions. However, the 
Committee is of the view that sustained and structured efforts are required to further 
strengthen interdisciplinary learning and practical exposure to integrative healthcare 
systems. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry expand bridging 
programmes and institutional collaborations between Ayush and modern medical 
institutions. The Committee expects that such initiatives will enhance the professional 
competence of Ayush practitioners and contribute to the development of a robust 
integrative healthcare framework in the country.  
PARA 4.3.4 
 
99. The Committee observes that a significant proportion of Ayush medical colleges 
in the country are managed by private organisations. However, the Committee is of the 
view that expansion of Ayush education must be accompanied by strict adherence to 
prescribed standards of infrastructure, faculty strength and quality of training. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen its monitoring and 
regulatory mechanisms to ensure that private Ayush institutions maintain the required 
academic and clinical standards. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry 
should make concerted efforts to ensure the complete coverage of the country.  The 
Committee believes that such measures will help in maintaining the quality and 
credibility of Ayush medical education while supporting its expansion in the country. 
 
PARA 4.3.6 
 
100. The Committee takes note of the steps taken by the Ministry to address the 
shortage of Ayush doctors through recruitment and contractual engagement. The 
Committee, however, is of the view that a long-term strategy is required to ensure 
adequate availability of qualified Ayush practitioners in healthcare institutions. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry, in coordination with State 
Governments, undertake periodic structured assessment of manpower requirements 
and ensure timely recruitment of Ayush doctors. The Committee expects that the 
Ministry assess the feasibility of replacing contractual doctors with regular doctors for 
improved learning and better education. 
PARA 4.4.2 
 
101. The Committee observes that the response of the Ministry in regard to the 
number of registered Homoeopathy practitioners in Maharashtra, does not adequately 
address the concern raised regarding the shortage of Ayush doctors in Government 
hospitals. The Committee is of the view that availability of qualified Ayush practitioners 
in public healthcare institutions is essential for strengthening service delivery, 
particularly in underserved areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 

128 
 
Ministry, in coordination with the State Governments undertake a comprehensive 
assessment of the requirement of Ayurvedic and Homoeopathic doctors in Government 
hospitals and take necessary steps to address the existing shortages through appropriate 
recruitment and deployment. The Committee further recommends for accessing the 
active practitioners in all the system of medicine. 
PARA 4.4.4 
 
102. The Committee observes that Yoga plays a vital role in promoting preventive 
healthcare and improving physical and mental well-being, particularly among school-
going children. While the existing provision under the National Ayush Mission enables 
Yoga instructors to conduct outreach activities in schools, the Committee is of the view 
that a more structured approach is required to promote Yoga practices among students. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry, in coordination with the 
Ministry of Education and State Governments, explore the feasibility of strengthening 
Yoga programmes in schools and encourage wider adoption of regular Yoga sessions. 
The Committee desires that the Ministry of Ayush should take lead in ensuring regular 
Yoga session at school level and updation of school curriculum to promote healthy 
lifestyle practices and improving the overall well-being of students across the country. 
 
PARA 4.4.6 
 
103. The Committee notes the research initiatives undertaken by the Ministry for the 
management of dermatological conditions through Ayurveda. The Committee, however, 
is of the view that greater emphasis is required to be given on scientific validation and 
wider dissemination of research findings in dermatology domain. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry should intensify the clinical research and 
promote evidence-based studies on Ayurvedic treatments for common and chronic skin 
diseases. The Committee expects the Ministry to ensure dissemination of 
outcomes/findings which will enhance the credibility and wider acceptance of Ayurvedic 
dermatological treatments. 
 
PARA 4.5.2 
 
104. The Committee appreciates the co-location model adopted by the Ministry for 
providing Ayush services in public healthcare facilities. However, the Committee is of 
the view that many of the PCs/CHCs are suffering from shortage of Ayush medicines 
and adequate consultation facilities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry strengthen E-Aushadhi portal for effective management and ensuring 
adequate and timely supply of Ayush medicines and explore the mechanism for setting  
Ayurvedic consultation cell at district and sub-district healthcare institutions. The 
Committee expects that such measures will improve accessibility and utilisation of 
Ayush healthcare services by the public. 
PARA 4.6.2 
 
105. The Committee has taken note of research initiatives undertaken by the Ministry 
in the field of maternal health and lactation support through Ayush systems. The 
Committee however, is of the view that greater emphasis is required on expanding such 
research and integrating validated Ayush interventions into maternal healthcare 
programmes. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen 
research efforts and promote community-based interventions for improving maternal 
health, particularly in the areas of post-partum recovery and lactation support.  
 
PARA 4.7.2 

129 
 
106. The Committee takes note that the establishment and strengthening of Ayush 
hospitals is undertaken through proposals submitted by State Governments under the 
National Ayush Mission. The Committee, however, is of the view that proactive 
coordination between the Ministry and State Government is necessary to expand Ayush 
healthcare infrastructure in Delhi considering the delay in adoption of Ayushman 
Bharat scheme in Delhi. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
actively engage with the Government of NCT of Delhi to facilitate the establishment of 
adequate Ayurvedic hospitals in the National Capital Territory within defined timeline. 
The Committee expects that the Ministry extend adequate support of funds and ensure 
timely release. 
PARA 4.8.2 
 
107. The Committee notes that no proposal has been received from the State 
Government for establishment of an Ayush hospital in Moradabad district. However, 
considering the growing demand for Ayush healthcare services in the region, the 
Committee is of the view that such facilities should be expanded in underserved regions 
like Moradabad. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must 
coordinate with the Government of Uttar Pradesh to explore the feasibility of 
establishing an Ayush hospital in Moradabad under the National Ayush Mission by 
assessing the needs of the Moradabad and providing access to traditional healthcare 
services in the region. 
PARA 4.8.4 
  
108. The Committee appreciates for the efforts made for screening for early detection 
of disease and extending healthcare support to tribal students through collaboration 
with other Ministries and research institutions. The Committee, however, is of the view 
that early detection and management of genetic disorders such as Sickle Cell Anaemia 
require wider coverage and sustained intervention at large scale. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry expand such screening programmes to cover 
more tribal districts and strengthen collaboration with the Ministry of Tribal Affairs 
and other health agencies. 
PARA 4.9.2 
 
109. The Committee acknowledges the collaborative initiatives undertaken by the 
Ministry with premier research institutions for promoting integrative healthcare. The  
Committee is of the view that stronger scientific evidence and large-scale clinical 
validation are essential for effective integration of Ayush with modern medicine. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry expand multi-centric clinical trials 
and strengthen interdisciplinary research to generate robust scientific evidence on 
integrative therapies. The Committee expects that such initiatives will strengthen the 
credibility of integrative healthcare models and promote wider acceptance of Ayush 
systems. 
PARA 4.10.2 
  
110. The Committee has taken note of challenges faced in fund utilisation under the 
National Ayush Mission due to the transition to the SNA-SPARSH system. The 
Committee believes that the Ministry should have anticipated and acted more 
proactively beforehand and procedural and technical issues should not significantly 
affect the timely implementation of healthcare programmes. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry strengthen technical support mechanisms at Ayush 
Directorate and monitoring systems at State Ayush Society to ensure timely utilisation 
of funds by States and implementing agencies. The Committee further recommends for 

130 
 
appointment of financial managers in each Ayush Directorates who shall work in 
tandem with State Ayush Society. 
PARA 4.11.2 
 
111. The Committee appreciates that the proposal submitted to the 16th Finance 
Commission for providing financial assistance to financially weaker States as seed 
money for strengthening Ayush infrastructure. The Committee is concerned to note that 
no such seed money has been recommended by the 16th Finance Commission in its 
recommendation. The Committee is of the view that limited financial resources may 
hinder the expansion of Ayush healthcare services in such States. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry explore additional financial support 
mechanisms for States facing financial constraints by providing seed money from NAM 
allocation. The Committee further recommends for setting up Ayush Infrastructure 
Fund having component of each segements of Ayush for meeting such constraints. 
 
PARA 4.11.4 
 
112. The Committee expresses concern over the persistent backlog in the submission 
of Utilisation Certificates (UCs) by States and Union Territories. The Committee, 
however is of the view that delays in submission of Utilisation certificates affect financial 
accountability and timely release of funds for development programmes like NAM. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry strengthen its monitoring and 
coordination mechanisms to ensure timely submission of Utilisation Certificates by 
implementing agencies. The Committee further recommends for standardising the 
Utilisation certificate format and consider the secure digitalisation of Utilisation 
Certificates to ensure compliance. 
PARA 4.12.2 
 
113. The Committee notes that regulatory provisions exist for the regulation of Ayush 
drugs under the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, which contains specific provisions for 
traditional systems of medicine. The Committee is of the view that, with the growing 
scope and global expansion of the Ayush sector, there is a need to further strengthen the 
regulatory legislative framework governing Ayush medicines. Moreover, the 
enforcement of the Act lies within the purview of State Licensing Authorities, which has 
often resulted in fragmented implementation, delays, and ineffective enforcement. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry examine the feasibility of 
strengthening the central regulatory mechanism for the regulation and laying down of 
uniform standards of enforcement and enhancing coordination with State Drug 
Authorities to ensure better standardization, safety, and efficacy of Ayush drugs. 
 
PARA 4.13.2 
 
114. The Committee notes the research output produced by various research councils 
under the Ministry of Ayush. However, the Committee is of the view that there is a need 
to further enhance the visibility and scientific impact of Ayush research at national and 
international levels. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must 
encourage collaborative research with reputed institutions and promote publication of 
research findings in high-impact national and international journals. The Committee 
expects that the findings are also translated into actual Ayush practice at grassroot 
level. 
PARA 4.14.2 
  

131 
 
115. The Committee notes the efforts made by the Ministry to strengthen 
infrastructure and expand service delivery under various Ayush schemes. The 
Committee further observes that research institutions under the Ministry receive 
support under the Grant-in-Aid (General) component for undertaking various research 
initiatives; however, these activities have largely remained limited to publications in 
journals rather than resulting in measurable outcomes. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry should undertake periodic independent evaluations and 
impact assessments of the research studies conducted and consider defining the 
expenditure under the GIA-General component for each research project, with a year-
end evaluation to measure outcomes. The Committee expects that such evaluations will 
contribute to improved programme implementation and ensure the optimal utilisation 
of public funds. 
PARA 4.14.4  
 
116. The Committee notes that no proposal has been received for establishing an 
Ayush research centre in Shamli district. However, considering the importance of 
expanding research infrastructure for the development of traditional systems of 
medicine, the Committee is of the view that such initiatives should be encouraged. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry coordinate with the Government 
of Uttar Pradesh to explore the feasibility of establishing an Ayush research centre in 
the region. The Committee expects that expansion of research infrastructure will 
contribute to strengthening research and innovation in Ayush systems. 
PARA 4.14.6 
 
117. The Committee takes note of the Sectoral Group of Secretaries (SGoS-4) 
recommendation for expansion of Ayush hospital infrastructure under the National 
Ayush Mission by June 2029. The Committee is of the view that timely implementation 
of proposed 288 Ayush hospitals & other initiatives are crucial to improve accessibility 
of Ayush healthcare services across the country. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry must ensure expeditious implementation of proposals for 
establishing integrated Ayush hospitals, particularly in districts currently lacking such 
facilities.  
PARA 4.15.2 
 
118. The Committee observes that the availability of Ayush hospitals in rural areas 
plays an important role in ensuring access to traditional healthcare services for the 
rural population. The Committee is of the view that the existing number of Ayush 
hospitals in rural areas of Gujarat may not be sufficient to meet the healthcare needs of 
a large rural population. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
encourages State Governments for submitting proposals under the National Ayush 
Mission for expansion and strengthening of Ayush healthcare infrastructure in rural 
areas. The Committee further desires that the guidelines of the National Ayush Mission 
(NAM) be circulated widely among the implementing agencies and State Governments 
so as to facilitate timely submission of proposals and effective utilisation of the scheme 
for strengthening Ayush healthcare infrastructure. 
PARA 4.15.4 
 
ESTABLISHMENT OF MEDICAL HUBS AND DRUG TESTING LABORATORIES 
119. The Committee notes the existing network of drug testing laboratories and 
pharmacovigilance centres established to ensure the quality and safety of Ayush 
medicines. The Committee is of the view that the present network is not adequate in 
view of the expanding Ayush sector, and there is a need to further strengthen the quality 
assurance infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 

132 
 
explore the establishment of additional drug testing laboratories and strengthen 
pharmacovigilance mechanisms across the country, preferably at district hospitals, 
which would enhance efficiency and help avoid delays in the testing of drugs. 
PARA 4.16.2 
 
INNOVATION AND GLOBAL RESEARCH COLLABORATION IN AYUSH 
 
120. The Committee notes with satisfaction that the global collaboration undertaken 
by the Ministry to promote international cooperation has been encouraging for Ayush 
sector.  The Committee, however, is of the view that greater encouragement is required 
for innovation, start-ups and biotechnology firms, MSMEs engaged in Ayush-based 
research and product development. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry should strengthen financial support for all the schemes and promote MSMEs, 
start ups to undertake for innovation and promote collaborative research with global 
institutions.  
PARA 4.17.2  
 
AFFORESTATION OF MEDICINAL PLANTS 
 
121. The Committee notes the initiatives undertaken for conservation and sustainable 
management of medicinal plants. The Committee is of the view that increased 
cultivation and conservation of medicinal plants are essential to meet the growing 
demand of the Ayush industry. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry strengthen collaboration with State Forest Departments and local communities 
to expand cultivation and conservation programmes for medicinal plants. The Ministry 
should encourage farmers for cultivation of medicinal plants to be used as raw material 
for Ayush drugs and finally the Ministry may explore Ayush market in rural areas 
involving farmers & their family under the scheme suggested as “Tera Tujh ko Arpan” 
ensuring handsome increase in farmer’s income and having sound health through 
Ayush products. 
PARA 4.18.2 
 
INTERACTION/COMMUNICATION IN TRIBAL AREAS 
 
122.  The Committee appreciates the outreach initiatives undertaken for 
promoting health awareness in tribal communities. The Committee, however, is of the 
view that greater engagement with tribal populations is necessary for understanding 
and documenting traditional health practices. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry strengthen community outreach programmes with tribals and 
promote documentation of traditional medicinal knowledge prevalent among tribal 
communities. The Committee further desires that the knowledge so accumulated may be 
integrated in local research repositories and used for treating the people. 
PARA 4.18.2 
 
123. The Committee notes the one day initiative taken by the Ministry to document 
traditional knowledge of tribal healers through interaction programmes. The 
Committee is of the view that a one-time interaction programme may not be sufficient to 
systematically capture and scientifically validate the rich ethnomedicinal knowledge 
available among tribal communities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry undertake structured field-based research by sending research scholars and 
multidisciplinary teams to tribal areas for systematic documentation, validation and 
preservation of traditional medicinal practices. 
PARA 4.19.4 

133 
 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT YOJNA COVERAGE FOR AYUSH 
 
124.  The Committee notes that the proposal for inclusion of Ayush treatment 
packages under AB-PMJAY is currently under consideration. However, the Committee 
is of the view that integration of Ayush healthcare services under major national health 
insurance schemes would significantly enhance accessibility and utilisation of traditional 
medicine. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry expedite 
consultations with the National Health Authority and the Ministry of Finance to 
facilitate inclusion of Ayush treatment packages under AB-PMJAY. The Committee 
expects that such inclusion will expand healthcare choices available to beneficiaries and 
promote wider adoption of Ayush systems. 
PARA 4.20.2 
 
NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR YOGA AND NATUROPATHY 
 
125. The Committee notes the steps taken by the existing regulatory commissions 
(NCISM & NCH) in introducing educational reforms such as competency-based 
curricula in medical education. However, in view of the increasing role of Yoga and 
Naturopathy in preventive and wellness healthcare, the expanding number of 
educational institutions, courses and practitioners and the growing international 
recognition of these disciplines, the Committee is of the view that the absence of a 
dedicated statutory regulatory framework may affect the long-term development and 
credibility of the Yoga and Naturopathy. The Committee, therefore, strongly 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush examine the feasibility of establishing a 
National Commission for Yoga and Naturopathy to regulate education standards, 
institutional accreditation and practitioner registration. The Committee believes that 
such a mechanism would ensure uniform academic standards, strengthen professional 
regulation and facilitate the orderly growth of Yoga and Naturopathy education and 
practice in the country. 
PARA 4.22.2  
 
FUNCTIONAL INTEGRATION OF TRADITIONAL MEDICINE 
 
126. The Committee notes that India has been moving towards a pluralistic 
healthcare system through co-location models and collaborative infrastructure 
development as envisaged under the National Health Mission. The Committee observes 
that such co-location has expanded healthcare choices for the public and has also 
encouraged research in areas such as herb-drug interactions. However, the Committee 
notes that treatment pathways in most institutions continue to function largely within 
their respective systems with modern medicine and Ayush services operating in parallel 
rather than in a inter- coordination manner. The Committee is of the view that greater 
clinical collaboration between the two systems can significantly enhance continuity of 
care, promote better utilisation of Ayush services and support the development of 
evidence-based integrative healthcare models. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry of Ayush, in coordination with the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare explore the possibility of devising Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) for 
structured cross-referral between modern medicine and Ayush departments preferably 
in tertiary care institutions. The Committee further recommends that these protocols 
clearly define referral pathways for appropriate conditions such as rehabilitation, 
chronic disease management, pain management, lifestyle disorders and preventive care, 
while ensuring that each system continues to function within its respective medical 
framework. 
PARA 4.23.1 

134 
 
 
ESTABLISHMENT OF REGIONAL RAW DRUG REPOSITORIES (RRDRS) 
 
127. The Committee appreciates the efforts of the National Medicinal Plants Board 
(NMPB) in undertaking systematic documentation through the establishment of 
Regional Raw Drug Repositories (RRDRs). The Committee believes that these 
repositories play a pivotal role in the collection, documentation, identification and 
authentication of raw drugs sourced largely from their respective agro-climatic regions. 
The Committee, however, observes that India possesses vast biodiversity and immense 
potential in medicinal plants, while the present number of repositories has limited 
geographical jurisdiction which may not be sufficient to ensure comprehensive and 
timely documentation of the country’s rich medicinal plant resources. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that NMPB consider establishing at least three additional 
Regional Raw Drug Repositories, particularly in biodiversity-rich regions such as the 
Western Ghats, Eastern Ghats and the North-Eastern region, so as to capture the 
diverse medicinal plant wealth of these ecologically sensitive zones and strengthen the 
national database of raw drugs for Ayush systems. The Committee also desires that the 
remaining four Regional Raw Drug Repositories already envisaged under the existing 
plan viz Western Himalayas, Eastern Himalayas & Eastern Plateau & Western coastal
