plains be established expeditiously so as to complete the originally proposed network at 
the earliest. 
PARA 4.24.2 
 
HUMAN RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT: PROMOTION OF AYUSH ALLIED 
SERVICES 
 
128. The Committee observes that the increasing strength of doctors and expanding 
patient outreach reflect the growing role of these institutions in service delivery, 
education and clinical training within the Ayush sector. The Committee, however, also 
notes that several Ayush healthcare facilities continue to face shortages of doctors 
especially in Maharashtra which may affect the optimal delivery of services. The 
Committee is further of the view that the absence of a comprehensive and updated 
database of practicing professionals across all Ayush systems, including Yoga 
practitioners limits effective workforce planning and management of Ayush services. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush take necessary steps 
to strengthen the availability of doctors in Ayush facilities and develop a comprehensive 
and regularly updated national database of practicing professionals across all Ayush 
systems, including Yoga, so as to facilitate better workforce planning, policy 
formulation and effective management of Ayush services across the country. The 
Committee is of the further view that the Ministry should introduce legislation for 
Ayush allied services to encourage and promote comprehensive Ayush human resource 
development. 
PARA 4.25.2 
 
129. The Committee comes to know that the number of Ayush CGHS Dispensaries is 
less in comparison to Allopathic CGHS Dispensaries. On the lines of promotion to 
Ayush and traditional system of medicines, the number of Ayush CGHS dispensaries 
should also be enhanced or co-located with existing allopathic dispensaries. On the 
service conditions of allied healthcare workforce in Ayush Dispensaries, the Committee 
noted that certain cadres like Ayush doctors, pharmacists, etc. are overburdened with 
present workforce and directly affect the delivery of healthcare services. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department urgently prioritize recruitment 
drives, streamline promotion pathways, time-bound or assured career progression 

135 
 
scheme to keep the morale of the staff high, skill upgradation and rationalisation of 
recruitment rules. The Committee also notes that certain cadres, particularly 
pharmacists (including Ayurvedic and Homeopathic), have limited or no promotion 
avenues within the existing service structure, leading to career stagnation and affecting 
staff motivation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
undertake a cadre restructuring to establish a clearer career progression pathway for 
such categories of staff, addressing long-standing anomalies which would improve 
retention and ensure a more motivated workforce within the healthcare system. 
 
PARA 4.25.3 
 
AYUSHMAN AROGYA MANDIR (AYUSH) 
 
130. The Committee welcomes the efforts of the Ministry in expanding Ayush-based 
primary healthcare infrastructure and outreach through 12500 Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs(Ayush). Nevertheless, the Committee is of the view that sustained focus on 
adequate staffing, availability of medicines, and strengthening of diagnostic and referral 
linkages would be essential to ensure the effective functioning of Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry continue to 
strengthen the operational capacity of these facilities so that they are able to deliver 
comprehensive and quality Ayush healthcare services to the population, particularly in 
underserved and rural areas. 
PARA 4.26.2 
INTERMINISTERIAL COLLABORATION 
131. The Committee is of the view that the effectiveness of Inter-Ministerial Ayush 
initiatives would depend upon strong inter-ministerial coordination and clearly defined 
institutional mechanisms for collaboration. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry of Ayush work in close coordination with concerned Ministries, research 
institutions and public health agencies to ensure effective implementation, better 
resource utilisation and measurable outcomes from such collaborative initiatives, 
particularly in the areas of integrative healthcare, research and public health 
interventions. The Committee further desires that the outcomes and findings of such 
collaborative initiatives be widely disseminated among stakeholders, healthcare 
institutions and State Governments so as to create greater awareness and facilitate 
effective implementation at the grassroots level. 
PARA 4.27.2 
 
OTHER KEY RECOMMENDATIONS 
 
132. The Committee notes that the growing demand for medicines under Ayush 
presents a significant opportunity to promote the cultivation of medicinal plants across 
the country. The Committee recommends that the Government encourage and support 
local farmers to undertake cultivation of medicinal and aromatic plants used in systems 
such as Ayurveda and Unani through appropriate incentives, assured procurement 
mechanisms, technical guidance and market linkages. Such initiatives would not only 
strengthen the raw material supply chain for Ayush medicines but also enhance 
farmers’ income, promote sustainable agriculture and contribute to rural economic 
development. 
PARA 4.28.1 
 
133. The Committee notes with concern the rapidly rising burden of lifestyle-related 
non-communicable diseases, including Diabetes Mellitus, hypertension and obesity in 
the country. Recognizing the strong emphasis of traditional systems on preventive 

136 
 
healthcare and healthy living, the Committee recommends that elements of Ayush 
particularly practices such as Yoga and basic principles of Ayurveda relating to healthy 
diet and lifestyle- may be gradually introduced in school curricula. Early exposure to 
these practices can help inculcate healthy habits among children, improve physical and 
mental well-being, and contribute to the long-term prevention of lifestyle diseases. Such 
initiatives would also help enhance awareness, acceptance, and wider popularity of 
Ayush systems among the younger generation, thereby strengthening the culture of 
preventive healthcare in the country. 
PARA 4.28.2 
 
134. The Committee is of the view that wider promotion and effective delivery of 
services under Ayush may be further strengthened through well-structured Public-
Private Partnerships. The Committee recommends that the Government explore 
collaboration with credible private institutions, wellness centres, educational institutions 
and industry for expanding research, standardization of medicines, training of 
practitioners, and establishment of integrative wellness facilities. Such partnerships can 
help leverage private sector expertise, innovation and investment while ensuring 
adherence to quality and regulatory standards. The Committee believes that 
encouraging responsible PPP models would improve accessibility of Ayush services, 
promote evidence-based practices, and enhance the overall reach, popularity and global 
recognition of India’s traditional systems of healthcare. 
PARA 4.28.3 
 
135. The Committee is of the view that greater collaboration between Ayush and 
modern medicine institutions would help strengthen the evidence base and wider 
acceptance of traditional healthcare practices. The Committee therefore recommends 
encouraging collaborative research between Ayush institutions and leading centres of 
modern medicine to scientifically evaluate therapies, standardize treatment protocols, 
and generate robust clinical evidence. Such interdisciplinary research would help 
identify areas where systems such as Ayurveda and Yoga can complement 
contemporary medical practice, particularly in preventive care, chronic disease 
management, and lifestyle disorders. The Committee believes that sustained research 
collaboration would enhance credibility, promote integrative healthcare, and contribute 
to improved health outcomes. 
PARA 4.28.4  
 
SECRETARIAT RELATED CAUTION WHILE RESPONDING COMMITTEE’S 
RECOMMENDATION 
 
136. The Committee observes that the replies to questionnaires have largely been 
furnished in an institute-wise or section-wise format by the various autonomous bodies 
and organisations under the Ministry. The Committee is of the view that such a format 
often results in multiple responses from different institutions to the same question, 
making the information voluminous and at times leading to fragmented responses and 
varying interpretations of the issue raised. The Committee, therefore, desires that the 
Ministry of Ayush should furnish more consolidated format while furnishing replies to 
the queries raised by the Committee. The Committee desires that the Ministry may 
obtain the required inputs from its subordinate offices, autonomous bodies and research 
councils internally and provide a unified and consolidated response to the Committee’s 
recommendations and observation. The Committee believes that such an approach 
would facilitate clearer presentation of information and enable a more effective and 
holistic examination of the Demands for Grants by the Committee. 
PARA 4.29.1 

137 
 
Establishment of New All india Institute of Ayurveda 
 
137. The Committee notes the announcement regarding the establishment of three 
new All India Institutes of Ayurveda (AIIA) to strengthen education, clinical services 
and research in Ayurveda. The Committee welcomes this initiative as an important step 
towards expanding tertiary-level Ayush healthcare infrastructure in the country and 
strengthening the quality of Ayurveda education, research and advanced clinical care. 
The Committee, however, is of the view that the effectiveness of these institutions will 
depend upon timely & effective implementation of projects/scheme, creation of adequate 
Ayush infrastructure and the development of a robust research ecosystem. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush formulate a 
comprehensive implementation roadmap for the newly announced AIIAs with clearly 
defined timelines, adequate funding support but with aim of measurable outcomes. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry must take steps to introduce more 
trans-disciplinary and inter-disciplinary courses and research programmes, including 
areas such as biotechnology, public health and engineering-integrated healthcare, so as 
to promote innovation and strengthen the scientific advancement of Ayurveda systems 
of medicine. 
PARA 5.2.2 
 
Upgradation of Ayush Pharmacies and Drug Testing Laboratories 
 
138. The Committee notes the announcement in the Union Budget 2026-27 regarding 
the upgradation of Ayush pharmacies and drug testing laboratories aimed at 
strengthening the quality assurance ecosystem of Ayush medicines and ensuring the 
availability of skilled technical manpower. The Committee observes that the credibility, 
safety and wider acceptance of Ayush medicines are closely linked to the robustness of 
quality control and standardisation mechanisms within the sector, particularly in the 
context of expanding domestic use and global trade in Ayush products. However, the 
Committee is of the view that concerns relating to quality standardisation, testing 
capacity, authentication of raw materials and adherence to pharmacopoeial standards 
continue to pose challenges for the sector. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry should take steps to strengthen technical capacity through specialised 
training programmes for laboratory personnel, drug inspectors, pharmacists and 
quality control professionals in collaboration with premier research institutions and 
regulatory bodies. The Committee further recommends that the upgraded laboratories 
be effectively integrated with research and standardisation initiatives, including the 
development of validated testing protocols, authentication of medicinal plant raw 
materials and safety evaluation of classical and proprietary Ayush formulations. The 
Committee believes that such measures would significantly strengthen the quality 
assurance framework of the Ayush sector, enhance consumer confidence and facilitate 
the expansion of Ayush product exports and India’s position in the global traditional 
medicine market. 
PARA 5.2.3 
Establishment of five Regional Medical Hubs 
 
139. The Committee notes that the Union Budget 2026-27 proposes to launch a 
scheme to support States in establishing five Regional Medical Hubs in partnership with 
the private sector with the objective of promoting India as a global hub for medical 
tourism services. The Committee welcomes all such initiatives as a step towards 
strengthening India’s position in the global medical value travel ecosystem and observes 
that it offers significant potential to expand the global outreach of Ayush systems of 
medicine. Nevertheless, the Committee is of the view that the success of envisaged hubs 

138 
 
will depend upon effective coordination between the Central Government, State 
Governments and private sector stakeholders. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry of Ayush work in close convergence with the Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare and other concerned Ministries to ensure adequate integration of Ayush 
services within creating effective professional working equilibrium of hubs. The 
Ministry should also ordain a clear framework for infrastructure development, quality 
standards, accreditation and international promotion to maximise the potential of such 
Ayush hubs hubs in promoting India’s medical tourism and Ayush sectors. 
PARA 5.2.4 
 
Upgradation of  the WHO Global Traditional Medicine Centre in Jamnagar  
 
140. The Committee appreciates the announcement in the Union Budget 2026-27 for 
the upgradation of the WHO Global Traditional Medicine Centre (GTMC) at Jamnagar 
aimed at strengthening global collaboration, research and knowledge exchange in the 
field of traditional medicine. The Committee observes that the Centre holds significant 
potential to position India as a global leader in traditional medicine by promoting 
evidence-based research, standardisation, capacity building and international 
cooperation in Ayush systems. The Committee, however, is of the considered view that 
the full potential of the Centre can be realised only through a well-defined institutional 
framework, enhanced research collaboration and effective global outreach. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush to work in tandem with 
the World Health Organization and other international partners to strengthen research 
networks, develop global standards and promote scientific validation of traditional 
medicine practices. The Committee further recommends that the Centre be leveraged as 
a global platform for policy dialogue, training, knowledge exchange and innovation in 
traditional medicine so as to enhance the international visibility and acceptance of 
Ayush systems and strengthen India’s leadership in the global traditional medicine 
ecosystem. 
PARA 5.2.5 
 
INSURANCE COVERAGE FOR AYUSH TREATMENT 
 
145. The Committee notes that despite the growing public acceptance of Ayush 
systems of medicine, insurance coverage for Ayush treatments remains limited and 
inconsistent across different insurance providers. The Committee observes that wider 
and more uniform insurance coverage for Ayush therapies would significantly improve 
the accessibility and affordability of these treatments for the public and support their 
greater integration within the healthcare system. The Committee, however, is of the 
view that the absence of standardised treatment protocols, package rates and regulatory 
clarity continues to limit the inclusion of Ayush therapies within mainstream health 
insurance frameworks. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of 
Ayush work in close coordination with the Insurance Regulatory and Development 
Authority of India (IRDAI) and insurance providers to expand the coverage of Ayush 
treatment packages under health insurance schemes. The Committee further 
recommends that the Ministry should undertake necessary steps to standardise 
treatment protocols and cost structures for Ayush therapies so as to facilitate their 
wider inclusion within mainstream health insurance frameworks and improve access to 
Ayush-based healthcare services. 
 
 
PARA 5.3.1 
 

139 
 
FINANCIAL PROJECTION 
 
146. The Committee takes into account the trend of budgetary support for the 
promotion and mainstreaming of traditional systems of medicine with concern. The 
Committee observes that the Ministry has articulated an ambitious vision of increasing 
the Ayush sector’s share in India’s GDP from the present 1.1 per cent to about 5 per 
cent by 2047. The Committee notes that achieving such expansion would require the 
Ayush ecosystem to grow at a significantly higher budgetary trajectory over the coming 
decades for the expansion of industries, exports, medical value travel and wellness 
services. The Committee is of the firm view that such growth would require stronger 
financial support, greater private sector participation under PPP model and a 
progressive strengthening of infrastructure, research and global outreach initiatives 
within the Ayush sector. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry take 
necessary steps to ensure that the growth trajectory of budgetary allocations for the 
Ayush sector must progressively accelerated beyond the existing trend to about 10.9 per 
cent. The Committee, in this regard recommends that, considering inflationary 
pressures and the need for real expansion in research, infrastructure and global 
outreach, the Budget Estimates for the Ministry of Ayush may be targeted to grow at a 
minimum of 15 per cent annually in nominal terms in the coming years so as to 
adequately support the long-term expansion and global positioning of the Ayush sector. 
 
PARA 5.4.1 
 
INSTITUTIONAL EXPANSION AND INFRASTRUCTURE 
 
147. The Committee takes note with concern that the Ministry of Finance has 
restricted the financial outlay of Ministry of Ayush for the next five years to ₹4,019.25 
crore based on a formula limiting the allocation to 5.5 times the average annual 
expenditure of FY 2021-22 to FY 2022-23 (Actual Expenditure) and FY 2024-25 
(Revised Estimates). The Committee observes that there exists a significant gap between 
the outlay proposed by the Ministry of Ayush and the allocation approved by the 
Ministry of Finance, which may adversely impact the pace of infrastructure 
augmentation and programme expansion envisaged for the Ayush sector under the 
vision of Viksit Bharat @ 2047. The Committee highlights that the restriction formula 
applied by the Ministry of Finance for the Ayush sector may not fully capture the actual 
requirements of the scheme, particularly in light of implementation challenges faced in 
recent years, including delays associated with the Single Nodal Agency (SNA) fund flow 
mechanism considering visionary goals and objectives of Ayush @ 2047. At the same 
time, the Committee is of the view that preceding expenditure trends affected by such 
bottlenecks may not accurately reflect the genuine financial needs for the expansion of 
Ayush infrastructure and services under the National Ayush Mission (NAM). The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush ensure optimal 
utilisation of the Budget Estimates for 2026-27 under NAM and take proactive steps to 
address implementation bottlenecks, including issues related to the SNA fund flow 
mechanism. The Committee recommends that while the Ministry must progressively 
engage with the Ministry of Finance for enhanced allocations in subsequent years and 
concurrently, the Ministry of Ayush may strengthen coordination between the Central 
Project Management Unit and the State Ayush Societies so as to ensure maximum fund 
utilisation and facilitate the timely expansion of Ayush infrastructure and services 
across the country to achieve the objective and goals of Ayush at 2047. 
 
PARA 5.5.2 
 

140 
 
SCIENTIFIC RESEARCH AND EVIDENCE GENERATION 
 
148. The Committee notes that the Ministry of Ayush has undertaken several 
initiatives in recent years to strengthen research in Ayush systems, including the 
activities of the Central Research Councils, collaborative research programmes and the 
launch of digital platforms such as the National Ayush Research Portal for 
dissemination of scientific publications and research outcomes. The Committee observes 
that these initiatives represent an important step towards strengthening evidence 
generation and promoting scientific research in the Ayush sector. However, the 
Committee is of the view that large-scale clinical research and translational studies in 
Ayush remain limited, which may constrain the systematic scientific validation and 
wider acceptance of Ayush therapies, medicines and treatment protocols. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must take steps to undertake 
large-scale multicentric clinical trials to evaluate Ayush interventions for major non-
communicable diseases and lifestyle disorders. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that collaboration between Ayush research institutions and premier biomedical 
institutions such as AIIMS, ICMR institutes, IITs and CSIR laboratories be expanded, 
and that dedicated Centres for Integrative Medicine Research be established to evaluate 
combined Ayush and modern medicine treatment models. The Committee also 
recommends that the National Ayush Research Portal be further strengthened as a 
comprehensive repository of research data, clinical studies and scientific publications. 
The Committee believes that such measures would significantly strengthen the evidence 
base of Ayush systems and enhance their credibility, acceptance and global integration. 
 
PARA 5.6.1 
 
149. The Committee takes note of emerging initiatives such as Biopharma SHAKTI 
provide an opportunity to integrate traditional knowledge systems with modern 
biotechnology and pharmaceutical innovation. The Committee observes that Ayush 
systems have significant potential in the development of plant-based therapeutic 
products, particularly phyto-pharmaceuticals, which can serve as a bridge between 
traditional herbal formulations and modern biopharmaceutical products. The 
Committee, however, is of the view that the absence of harmonised quality standards 
and robust clinical validation may limit the global competitiveness of Ayush products. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must promote the 
development of phyto-pharmaceuticals and establish a quality certification framework 
aligned with internationally accepted Good Manufacturing Practices (GMP). The 
Committee further recommends leveraging institutions such as the WHO Global 
Traditional Medicine Centre for multi-centric clinical research so as to strengthen 
scientific validation and enhance India’s position in the global market for nature-based 
therapeutics. 
PARA 5.6.2 
 
STANDARDIZATION OF ASU&H  vis-a-vis PHARMACOVIGILANCE 
 
150. The Committee appreciates the progress made by the Ministry in strengthening 
the quality assurance framework for Ayush drugs and in expanding pharmacovigilance 
mechanisms within the sector. The Committee observes that these initiatives are 
important for ensuring the safety, credibility and wider acceptance of Ayush medicines. 
The Committee, however, is of the view that the scientific validation of Ayurvedic and 
other Ayush formulations needs to be further strengthened through systematic pre-
clinical, clinical and observational studies, including toxicity assessments, herb-drug 
interaction studies, pharmacological evaluations and well-designed clinical trials across 

141 
 
a range of disease conditions. The Committee further observes that there persists a 
perception among sections of practitioners and the public that natural medicines are 
inherently harmless, which may discourage the identification and reporting of potential 
adverse effects. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush 
strengthen scientific research and pharmacovigilance systems to promote evidence-
based validation and safety profiling of Ayush medicines. The Committee further 
recommends that robust mechanisms for quality control, traceability of raw materials 
and systematic documentation of adverse drug reactions be strengthened so as to 
enhance public confidence & acceptability and support the transition of Ayush 
medicines from being primarily faith-based to scientifically validated and evidence-
based healthcare interventions. 
PARA 5.7.2 
 
EDUCATION AND HUMAN RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT 
 
151. The Committee takes note of the attention of the Ministry of Ayush for 
strengthening Ayush education through institutional expansion and various policy 
reforms. The Committee observes that strengthening the quality of Ayush education is 
the need of hour for developing competent healthcare professionals and promoting 
evidence-based practice within the sector. The Committee, however, is of the view that 
Ayush education and training require deeper alignment with the objectives of the 
National Education Policy (NEP), 2020, particularly in promoting interdisciplinary, 
research-oriented and skill-based learning. The Committee further observes that with 
increasing emphasis on scientific research, evidence-based practice and digital 
integration, there is a need to strengthen capacity building of faculty and students and 
upgrade existing educational infrastructure with better integration of modern 
diagnostic tools and technologies. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry initiate the establishment of dedicated training institutes for teachers and 
develop skill laboratories in Ayush colleges to improve the quality of teaching and 
practical training. The Committee further recommends that the undergraduate and 
postgraduate curricula be revised to include the management of diseases of national 
importance and other public health challenges prevalent in society. The Committee also 
recommends that emerging research findings and scientific evidence be systematically 
incorporated into the Ayush curriculum so as to make Ayush scholars more aware of 
contemporary scientific developments and strengthen the overall quality of Ayush 
education. 
PARA 5.8.2 
 
152. The Committee observes that the Indian healthcare framework is increasingly 
moving towards a pluralistic model with growing co-location and integration of 
traditional and modern systems of medicine. The Committee further observes that such 
integration requires practitioners across different systems to possess a basic 
understanding of each other’s principles and approaches in order to ensure effective 
and coordinated healthcare delivery. The Committee, however, is of the view that the 
present healthcare education framework provides limited interdisciplinary exposure 
and does not adequately incorporate the Indian Knowledge System (IKS). The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush take appropriate steps to 
strengthen the integration of the Indian Knowledge System within the evolving 
integrative healthcare education framework so as to promote interdisciplinary 
understanding and support a more holistic approach to healthcare delivery in the 
country. 
PARA 5.8.3 
 

142 
 
153. The Committee observes that the effective delivery of healthcare services 
increasingly requires integration of traditional systems of medicine with modern 
diagnostic tools and technologies. The Committee is of the strong opinion that improved 
familiarity of Ayush practitioners with modern diagnostic techniques would enhance 
clinical decision-making, strengthen patient confidence and facilitate better integration 
of Ayush services within the broader healthcare system. The Committee, however, 
highlights that the existing training framework for Ayush practitioners provides limited 
structured exposure to modern diagnostic tools and technologies. The Committee, 
therefore, desires that the Ministry of Ayush must take steps to promote targeted 
training and capacity-building programmes to familiarise Ayush practitioners with 
modern diagnostic tools and techniques, while preserving the core principles and 
philosophy of traditional systems of medicine. The Committee believes that such 
initiatives would contribute to improving the quality of healthcare delivery and 
strengthening the credibility of Ayush systems in the evolving healthcare landscape. 
 
PARA 5.8.4 
AYUSH DIGITAL ECOSYSTEM 
 
154. The Committee takes into account that the Ministry has initiated several digital 
initiatives under the Ayush Grid Project with the objective of creating a robust digital 
integration framework for the Ayush sector. The Committee observes that such 
initiatives, including emerging areas like Ayurgenomics, have received international 
recognition, including acknowledgement by the World Health Organization (WHO), 
and hold significant potential for strengthening digital health integration, research 
dissemination and evidence generation in the Ayush sector. However, the Committee 
finds that the extent of actual adoption of these digital platforms across institutions, 
their interoperability with national digital health systems and their measurable impact 
on service delivery and research outcomes still contain scope for further expansion in 
terms of Artificial Intelligence integration. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry should take time-bound steps to ensure wider adoption and effective 
operationalisation of the digital platforms developed under the Ayush Grid Project 
across Ayush hospitals, research institutions, educational bodies and regulatory 
authorities. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry establish clear 
implementation milestones, monitoring mechanisms and periodic reporting systems to 
track the usage and outcomes of these platforms. The Committee also recommends that 
necessary measures be taken to ensure interoperability of Ayush Grid platforms with 
national digital health initiatives, including the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission and 
that the data generated through these platforms be effectively utilised for policy 
planning, research advancement and evidence generation with incorporation of 
artificial intelligence. The Committee believes that strengthening digital integration 
along with capacity building & training of Ayush practitioners and institutional staff 
would facilitate the effective integration of Ayush services within the broader digital 
health ecosystem. 
PARA 5.9.2 
 
GLOBALISATION OF AYUSH 
 
155. The Committee notes that the Government has articulated a long-term vision for 
the globalisation of Ayush as an important component of the broader Viksit Bharat @ 
2047 roadmap. The Committee observes that the increasing global interest in holistic, 
preventive and traditional systems of medicine presents a significant opportunity for 
India to position Ayush systems as an important pillar of global healthcare. The 
Committee further observes that initiatives such as the strengthening of the WHO 

143 
 
Global Traditional Medicine Centre (GTMC) at Jamnagar, expansion of international 
collaborations, promotion of Ayush-based medical value travel and the global adoption 
of quality certification mechanisms such as the Ayush Mark have the immense potential 
to significantly enhance the international visibility and acceptance of Ayush systems. 
The Committee agrees that initiatives like the International Day of Yoga have played an 
important role in strengthening global awareness and acceptance of traditional Indian 
wellness practices and have emerged as a strong instrument of India’s soft power and 
health diplomacy. However, the Committee is of the firm view that achieving the vision 
of positioning India as a global leader in traditional and holistic healthcare would 
require a more coordinated and structured approach towards international 
engagement, research collaboration, quality assurance and global market development. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush must develop a 
comprehensive strategy for the globalisation of Ayush with visionary policy priorities 
relating to establishing Centre of Excellence abroad, international research 
collaborations, global standardisation of Ayush products and therapies, promotion of 
Ayush-based medical value travel and strengthening of export promotion mechanisms. 
The Committee further recommends that initiatives such as the International Day of 
various systems of Ayush especially Yoga be strategically leveraged to enhance global 
awareness and institutional partnerships so as to position Ayush systems more 
prominently within the global healthcare ecosystem and contribute to India’s leadership 
in traditional medicine. 
PARA 5.10.1 
 
156. The Committee notes that India has emerged as an important destination for 
medical value travel owing to its skilled healthcare workforce, cost-effective treatments 
and well-established traditional wellness systems. The Committee observes that Ayush 
therapies, particularly Yoga, Panchakarma and other wellness treatments, possess 
considerable potential to attract international patients seeking holistic healthcare 
solutions. The Committee, however, is of the view that since the integration of Ayush 
therapies within the broader medical value travel ecosystem remains limited and 
requires greater policy focus for international promotion. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush develop dedicated strategies to integrate Ayush 
therapies within the broader medical value travel ecosystem in coordination with other 
relevant Ministries and stakeholders. The Committee considers that focused efforts be 
made to promote affordable Ayush-based wellness packages and therapies at the 
international level so as to position India as a global hub for holistic wellness and 
traditional medicine. 
PARA 5.10.2 
 
AYUSH MARKET SIZE VIS-À-VIS EXPORTS 
 
157. The Committee envisages that the global demand for traditional medicine and 
wellness products is steadily increasing and presents significant opportunities for the 
Ayush sector. The Committee observes that while the domestic manufacturing base for 
Ayush products has expanded considerably in recent years, the sector’s export potential 
remains underutilised due to challenges relating to quality assurance, product 
standardisation, certification and international regulatory compliance. The Committee 
visualizes that achieving the projected Ayush market size of US$ 200 billion by 2030 
would require substantial expansion of exports supported by robust quality assurance 
and international standardisation mechanisms. However, the Committee is of the view 
that the existing ecosystem for promotion of Ayush exports requires further 
strengthening in terms of product authentication, regulatory alignment and global 
market access. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush, in 

144 
 
coordination with the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, develop a comprehensive 
export promotion strategy for Ayush products with a focus on strengthening quality 
assurance, standardisation, certification and international regulatory compliance. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry explore policy measures such as the 
introduction of export incentives, creation of separate Harmonised System of 
Nomenclature (HSN) codes for Ayush products and strengthening of authentication and 
certification mechanisms including Ayush Mark and adherence to WHO-GMP 
guidelines. The Committee believes that such measures would significantly enhance the 
export competitiveness of Ayush products, improve global acceptance of Ayush systems 
and contribute towards achieving the projected growth of the Ayush sector. 
 
PARA 5.11.2 
 
158. The Committee points out that the promotion of Ayush products and services in 
international markets is an important objective of the International Cooperation 
Scheme and appreciates the efforts made by the Ministry in facilitating export growth 
and strengthening global engagement. The Committee views that initiatives such as the 
establishment of the Ayush Export Promotion Council (AyushEXCIL), incorporation of 
Ayush-related provisions in Free Trade Agreements and the strengthening of quality 
certification and laboratory standards signals significant steps towards improving 
global market access for Ayush products. However, the Committee is of the view that 
the sustained expansion of Ayush exports would require more focused export promotion 
strategies, greater international visibility and stronger institutional support for 
manufacturers, particularly MSMEs, in accessing regulated global markets. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Ministry must intensify targeted 
export promotion initiatives including the organisation of dedicated International Ayush 
Expos, trade fairs and global business conferences in strategic markets so as to facilitate 
direct engagement between Indian manufacturers and foreign regulators, distributors, 
healthcare providers and investors. The Committee understands that structured global 
awareness campaigns highlighting quality standards, safety certifications and scientific 
validation of Ayush products must be undertaken to enhance consumer confidence and 
brand recognition. The Committee wishes that measurable indicators such as export 
growth, market diversification, certification uptake and regulatory approvals secured 
under Free Trade Agreement frameworks be periodically monitored and reported so as 
to assess the tangible economic impact of these initiatives and strengthen India’s global 
position in the Ayush sector. 
 
PARA 5.11.3 
 
Way Forward for National Medicinal Plants Board (NMPB) 
 
159. The Committee notes that the National Medicinal Plants Board (NMPB) has 
taken initiatives to support farmers through the establishment of nurseries for raising 
planting material of medicinal plants under the Forward and Backward Linkages in the 
Supply Chain of Medicinal Plants. The Committee observes that the medicinal plants 
sector holds considerable potential for strengthening the Ayush industry, supporting 
rural livelihoods and enhancing India’s bio-economy through the development of 
pharmaceuticals, nutraceuticals, wellness products and medicinal plant-based 
industries. The Committee, however wishes to point out that the absence of updated 
demand supply assessment data of medicinal plants and a comprehensive State-wise 
inventory may hinder effective planning for cultivation, value chain development and 
realisation of India’s export potential in medicinal plants and herbal products. The 
Committee further observes that the sector presently needs adequate industrial 

145 
 
ecosystems to support large-scale product innovation, startup incubation, technology 
transfer and value addition, which in turn will promote the commercialisation and 
global competitiveness of Ayush products. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry must undertake a comprehensive national assessment study in 
coordination with State Medicinal Plants Boards to determine State-wise availability of 
medicinal plants and updated demand-supply projections, including global market 
demand. The Committee is of the strong view that the Ministry must strengthen the 
medicinal plants value chain through the establishment of Medicinal Plants Business 
Processing Centres (MBPCs) along with quality certification and traceability 
mechanisms to enhance value addition and export competitiveness. The Committee 
further recommends that the Ministry should develop supportive industrial ecosystems, 
including dedicated Ayush innovation and industry clusters, to promote product 
development, startup incubation, technology transfer and export promotion so as to 
fully realise the economic potential of the Ayush sector. 
PARA 5.12.1 
 
AYUSH POTENTIAL IN NORTH EASTERN REGION 
 
160. The Committee observes that the North Eastern Region of India possesses rich 
biodiversity and holds immense potential for the cultivation and sustainable utilisation 
of medicinal plants used in Ayush systems of medicine. The Committee further observes 
that the NER can play a pivotal role in strengthening the supply chain of medicinal 
plants and supporting the growth of the Ayush sector through cultivation, processing 
and value addition. However, the Committee is of the view that the full potential of the 
region remains underutilised due to limited infrastructure for cultivation, processing 
and market linkages. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of 
Ayush accord special emphasis to the development of medicinal plant cultivation, 
processing and value addition in the North Eastern States under relevant schemes. The 
Committee further recommends that appropriate measures be taken to strengthen 
market linkages, promote sustainable harvesting practices and support livelihood 
opportunities for local communities so as to harness the region’s biodiversity potential 
while contributing to the growth of the Ayush sector. 
PARA 5.12.1 
 
 
CROP INSURANCE FOR MEDICINAL PLANTS 
 
161. The Committee notes that there has been a gradual shift in approach in the 
medicinal plants sector from forest-based collection to cultivation-based production. 
The Committee further notes that the National Medicinal Plants Board under the 
Ministry of Ayush has been assisting farmers in the cultivation of quality planting 
material and promoting the organised cultivation of medicinal plants. The Committee 
observes that such efforts are important for ensuring a sustainable supply of raw 
materials for Ayush systems while also creating additional livelihood opportunities for 
farmers. The Committee wills to point out that medicinal plant cultivation is often 
subject to price fluctuations and crop damage, which may expose farmers to financial 
risks and discourage wider adoption of such cultivation. The Committee is of the view 
that adequate risk mitigation mechanisms are essential to promote the long-term 
sustainability of medicinal plant cultivation. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Ministry of Ayush, in coordination with the concerned Ministries and 
stakeholders, explore the feasibility of introducing crop insurance framework for 
medicinal plants so as to provide financial protection to farmers and encourage greater 

146 
 
participation in the cultivation of medicinal plant for export promotion and increasing 
farmer’s income to desirable level and enhancing contribution of Ayush in GDP. 
 
PARA 5.14.1 
 
PROPAGATION AND PROMOTION OF AYUSH 
 
162. The Committee takes note that despite the widespread presence of Ayush systems 
of medicine in the country, public awareness regarding their preventive and promotive 
healthcare benefits remains limited. The Committee observes that Ayush systems offer 
huge potential for promoting preventive healthcare and healthy lifestyles through 
practices such as Yoga, appropriate dietary habits and traditional wellness approaches. 
However, the Committee is of the view that existing awareness initiatives largely focus 
on information dissemination and require greater emphasis on encouraging behavioural 
change towards the adoption of Ayush-based lifestyle practices. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Ayush strengthen its public outreach 
programmes with a greater focus on behaviour change communication strategies aimed 
at promoting the adoption of Ayush-based preventive healthcare practices such as Yoga, 
dietary regulation through incorporating Millet diets and traditional wellness practices. 
The Committee believes that such initiatives would contribute to improving public 
health outcomes while strengthening the role of Ayush systems in preventive and 
promotive healthcare. 
PARA 5.15.1  
 
 
163. The Committee believes that the vision of Ayush @ 2047 offers a historic 
opportunity to transform India’s traditional systems of medicine into a scientifically 
robust, globally respected and economically vibrant component of the national 
healthcare ecosystem, while reinforcing Bharat’s leadership in promoting traditional 
medicine at the global level. The vision is guided by the civilisational ethos of 
Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam - the world as one family, the Committee emphasises that 
achieving the vision will require sustained and coordinated efforts towards 
strengthening scientific research and evidence generation, expanding institutional and 
educational infrastructure, ensuring standardisation and quality assurance of Ayush 
medicines and accelerating digital integration through initiatives such as the Ayush 
Grid. The Committee further underscores the importance of promoting global 
engagement, expanding medical value travel, strengthening export competitiveness of 
Ayush products and developing sustainable medicinal plant value chains to support the 
long-term growth of the sector, thereby positioning Bharat as a leading global hub for 
traditional and integrative medicine. The Committee is of the considered view that 
enhanced financial support, stronger inter-ministerial coordination, active participation 
of States and greater private sector involvement will be essential for unlocking the full 
potential of the Ayush ecosystem. The Committee hopes that the Ministry of Ayush will 
take appropriate and time-bound steps to address the issues highlighted in this Report 
and effectively implement the recommendations made therein so as to realise the vision 
of Ayush @ 2047 and further strengthen Bharat’s global leadership in holistic, 
preventive and integrative healthcare. 
PARA 5.16.1
